Theme 1: Introduction to Constitutional Law — Short Question and MCQ Foundation

Source warning: Theme 1 has reliable lecture/case material, but some older textbook scans still carry OCR/manual reconstruction warnings. Do not treat those corrupted extraction files as complete.

1. What law is

  • Law is made by people, for people.
  • Law is not fixed or unquestionable.
  • Law changes over time and can be criticised.
  • Law is an unfinished project shaped by society.

2. Why law exists

  • Law presupposes society.
  • Rules become necessary when people live together.
  • Law manages competing interests.
  • Without law, society risks arbitrariness, inequality, unfairness, unreasonableness and self-help.

3. Law and society

  • A single person alone needs no rules.
  • When people interact, conflicts over resources arise.
  • Larger societies require agreed rules and structures of authority.

4. Rule of law and social contract

Rule of law means obedience to legal rules and government under law. Social contract theory explains that individuals give up some freedom in exchange for order, security and peace.

Warning: Do not reduce rule of law only to obedience. In constitutional democracy it also requires legality, non-arbitrariness and accountability.

5. Ubuntu and law

Ubuntu: umuntu ngumuntu ngabantu. A person is a person through other persons. Law is grounded in dignity, relationship, community and shared humanity. Link Ubuntu to constitutional belonging and City of Tshwane.

6. Where constitutional law fits

  • Law divides into national/international, substantive/procedural, and public/private.
  • Constitutional law is public law.
  • Public law regulates relationships between the state and individuals and among state institutions.
  • Private law manages disputes between private parties.

7. What constitutional law is

  • It concerns state, society and law.
  • It regulates principal state institutions.
  • It regulates the legislature, executive and judiciary.
  • It sets out composition, powers, duties and procedures of organs of state.
  • It regulates relationships between organs of state and between organs of state and members of society.

8. What a constitution is

Lay definition: fundamental rules applicable to a group.

Legal definition: fundamental law of a state according to which a country is governed.

A constitution structures government, allocates power and guarantees rights.

9. What a constitution does

  • Creates state institutions.
  • Awards powers to institutions.
  • Limits those powers.
  • Protects rights.
  • Structures the relationship between law and power.

10. Constitutionalism

Constitutionalism is the theory of constitutional law: a political philosophy or doctrine that government authority is derived from and limited by fundamental law. It prescribes what a constitution and constitutional law should do.

  • limited government
  • rule of law
  • separation of powers
  • checks and balances
  • protection of rights
  • transformative constitutionalism

11. Separation of powers introductory box

Legislature makes law. Executive implements or executes law. Judiciary interprets and adjudicates law. The purpose is to prevent concentration of power. Checks and balances mean each branch monitors and limits the others. Deeper separation of powers belongs mainly to Theme 4 and the guest lecture material.

Types of constitutions

TypeMeaningSouth African position
Codified / writtenA constitution collected in a written constitutional text.South Africa is written/codified.
Uncodified / unwrittenConstitutional rules spread across statutes, conventions, cases and practices.Not South Africa's main model.
RigidSpecial amendment procedures or thresholds are required.South Africa is rigid, especially because section 74 creates special amendment thresholds.
FlexibleOrdinary legislative procedure can amend the constitution.Not South Africa's model.
UnitaryCentral government holds ultimate authority.South Africa has unitary features but is not purely unitary.
Quasi-unitary / cooperativeMultiple spheres exist, but they cooperate and national power remains significant.South Africa is multi-sphere with cooperative governance.
FederalSovereign or strongly autonomous subnational units share constitutional power.South Africa is not a classic federation.
PresidentialPresident is usually separately elected and is both head of state and executive.South Africa has a President, but the President is elected by the National Assembly.
ParliamentaryExecutive depends on parliamentary confidence or majority support.South Africa has parliamentary features because the National Assembly elects the President.

13. Preamble and constitutional purpose

A preamble gives background, values and purpose. The 1996 Preamble recognises injustices of the past, honours those who suffered for justice and freedom, aims to heal divisions, establishes a society based on democratic values, social justice and fundamental human rights, lays foundations for a democratic and open society, seeks to improve quality of life, and aims to build a united and democratic South Africa.

14. City of Tshwane v Afriforum as Theme 1/2 bridge

Facts: AfriForum opposed the change of 25 Pretoria street names linked to the colonial/apartheid past.

Issue: symbolic redress, belonging, cultural heritage and public space.

Court reasoning: The Preamble is a constitutional compass; belonging must be inclusive, not exclusive; public symbols are not neutral; transformation may require symbolic change; Ubuntu supports relational belonging and mutual recognition.

Constitutional significance: The Preamble is not mere decoration. It guides interpretation and transformation.

Exam use: Use for Preamble, belonging, symbols, Ubuntu, transformation and redress.

15. Theme 1 short-answer bank

1. Define constitutionalism.

Model answer: Constitutionalism is the doctrine that government authority is derived from and limited by fundamental law. It requires limited government, rule of law, separation of powers, checks and balances, protection of rights and, in South Africa, transformation.

2. What is constitutional law?

Model answer: Constitutional law is public law concerned with the state, society and law. It regulates principal state institutions, their composition, powers, duties and procedures, and the relationships between organs of state and between the state and people.

3. What does a constitution do?

Model answer: A constitution creates state institutions, allocates powers to them, limits those powers, protects rights and structures the relationship between law and political power.

4. Distinguish public law and private law.

Model answer: Public law regulates the relationship between the state and individuals and among state institutions. Private law regulates disputes and relationships between private parties.

5. Name the three branches of government.

Model answer: The legislature makes law, the executive implements or executes law, and the judiciary interprets and adjudicates law.

6. Explain separation of powers.

Model answer: Separation of powers divides legislative, executive and judicial authority to prevent concentration of power. Checks and balances allow each branch to monitor and limit the others.

7. What is the purpose of the Preamble?

Model answer: The Preamble states the Constitution's historical background, values and purposes. It recognises past injustice, honours those who struggled for justice and freedom, and points toward healing, democracy, social justice, human rights and unity.

8. Explain Ubuntu's relevance to law.

Model answer: Ubuntu, expressed as umuntu ngumuntu ngabantu, means a person is a person through other persons. It links law to dignity, relationship, community, mutual recognition and shared humanity.

9. Distinguish codified and uncodified constitutions.

Model answer: A codified constitution is collected in a written constitutional text. An uncodified constitution is found across statutes, conventions, cases and practices rather than one supreme written document.

10. Distinguish rigid and flexible constitutions.

Model answer: A rigid constitution requires special procedures or higher thresholds for amendment. A flexible constitution can be amended through ordinary legislative processes.

11. Explain South Africa as quasi-unitary/cooperative governance.

Model answer: South Africa has national, provincial and local spheres, but national power remains strong and the spheres must cooperate. It is not a classic federal system.

12. What is the rule of law?

Model answer: Rule of law means government under law, legality, non-arbitrariness, accountability and obedience to legal rules. In constitutional democracy it is not merely obedience to whatever government commands.

13. Why do we have law?

Model answer: Law exists because people live together, resources and interests conflict, and society needs rules and institutions to prevent arbitrariness, inequality, unfairness and self-help.

14. What does City of Tshwane show about belonging?

Model answer: City of Tshwane shows that public symbols are not neutral and that the Preamble can guide inclusive belonging, symbolic redress, ubuntu and transformation.

15. Why is law not fixed or unquestionable?

Model answer: Law is made by people for people. It changes over time, can be criticised, and remains an unfinished project shaped by society, politics, values and historical context.

16. Theme 1 MCQ bank

15 Theme 1 MCQs

1. Which statement best captures law in Theme 1?
  • Law is natural and unchangeable
  • Law is made by people, for people, and can be criticised
  • Law is only court decisions

Answer: Law is made by people, for people, and can be criticised

Theme 1 treats law as a human, social and unfinished project.

2. Why does law presuppose society?
  • Rules become necessary when people interact
  • A single person alone needs Parliament
  • Law exists only for punishment

Answer: Rules become necessary when people interact

Law manages social interaction and competing interests.

3. Constitutional law is mainly part of:
  • Private law
  • Public law
  • International trade law

Answer: Public law

It regulates public power, state institutions and state-individual relationships.

4. The legislature primarily:
  • Makes law
  • Executes law
  • Interprets law

Answer: Makes law

Legislative authority is law-making authority.

5. The executive primarily:
  • Implements or executes law
  • Declares final constitutional invalidity
  • Drafts judgments

Answer: Implements or executes law

Executive power gives practical effect to law and policy.

6. The judiciary primarily:
  • Interprets and adjudicates law
  • Runs departments
  • Passes statutes

Answer: Interprets and adjudicates law

Courts resolve disputes and interpret law.

7. Ubuntu is best captured by:
  • umuntu ngumuntu ngabantu
  • majority rule is unlimited
  • law is fixed

Answer: umuntu ngumuntu ngabantu

Ubuntu links personhood to relationship and community.

8. A codified constitution is:
  • Collected in a written text
  • Never written down
  • Only a political speech

Answer: Collected in a written text

South Africa has a written/codified Constitution.

9. A rigid constitution is one that:
  • Requires special amendment procedures
  • Can never change
  • Can be amended by any minister

Answer: Requires special amendment procedures

Rigidity means amendment is deliberately harder than ordinary law-making.

10. South Africa is best described as:
  • Written, rigid and cooperative/multi-sphere
  • Unwritten and flexible
  • Purely federal with sovereign provinces

Answer: Written, rigid and cooperative/multi-sphere

South Africa has three spheres with cooperative governance and strong national features.

11. The Preamble is:
  • A constitutional compass, not mere decoration
  • Legally irrelevant poetry
  • Only a heading

Answer: A constitutional compass, not mere decoration

It guides constitutional purpose, interpretation and transformation.

12. City of Tshwane is useful for:
  • Preamble, symbols, belonging and redress
  • Contract damages
  • Company liquidation

Answer: Preamble, symbols, belonging and redress

The case links public space, symbolic transformation and inclusive belonging.

13. Constitutionalism requires:
  • Limited government and rights protection
  • Unlimited government power
  • A constitution that cannot be criticised

Answer: Limited government and rights protection

Constitutionalism constrains government through fundamental law.

14. Rule of law in constitutional democracy includes:
  • Legality, non-arbitrariness and accountability
  • Only obedience to whoever governs
  • Only police enforcement

Answer: Legality, non-arbitrariness and accountability

Modern South African rule of law is tied to constitutional supremacy and accountable power.

15. Private law mainly regulates:
  • Private relationships and disputes
  • Parliament and the President
  • Only international treaties

Answer: Private relationships and disputes

Public law concerns state power; private law concerns private parties.

Theme 2: Constitutional History — Short Question, MCQ and Timeline Foundation

Source warning: Theme 2 uses reliable lecture, revision and curated essay-bank material. Currie and De Waal pages 46-51 and the Reservation of Separate Amenities Act extraction still need OCR/manual reconstruction, so their warnings remain.

1. Why constitutional history matters

The Constitution emerged from historical context. The past continues to shape social, economic and cultural realities. Constitutions can be a snapshot of hopes and aspirations, but they can also reflect the political power of their drafters. Constitutional law is political because it structures public power.

2. Five official constitutions

1910

Union Constitution / South Africa Act 1909.

1961

Republic Constitution.

1983

Tricameral Constitution.

1993

Interim Constitution.

1996

Final Constitution.

3. Pre-1910 history

  • Khoi-San communities and San hunter-gatherer communities existed before colonial rule.
  • Important features included equality, respect, headmen, councils of elders and open forums.
  • Khoi-Khoi communities had political and social structures.
  • Bantu-speaking communities migrated southwards and developed farming, craft, trade and metal work.
  • Complex political systems included villages, chiefdoms and kingdoms.
  • Warning: colonisation did not create civilisation.

4. European colonial history

  • 1488: Bartolomeu Dias rounded the Cape.
  • 1652: Jan van Riebeeck and the Dutch East India Company established a refreshment station.
  • Colonial rule brought land dispossession, enslavement and forced labour.
  • Dutch settlers/Afrikaners moved inland; Britain occupied the Cape in 1795/1806.
  • Slavery was abolished in 1834, increasing tensions and contributing to the Great Trek.
  • Transvaal and Orange Free State emerged.
  • Diamonds in 1867 and gold in 1886 produced industrialisation, urbanisation, migrant labour and racial labour control.
  • The Anglo-Boer War / South African War of 1899-1902 involved scorched earth and concentration camps.
  • British victory led to unification.

5. Why pre-1910 history matters

  • Racial exclusion was already entrenched.
  • Land dispossession was widespread.
  • Labour control systems were already in place.
  • White unity was prioritised over racial justice.
  • Black South Africans were excluded from constitutional design.
  • Union formalised and consolidated existing patterns.

6. South Africa Act 1909 / 1910 Union Constitution

  • Royal Proclamation: 2 December 1909.
  • Union established: 31 May 1910.
  • Cape, Natal, Orange Free State and Transvaal were brought together.
  • The state was unitary and Westminster-style.
  • White men could be elected to the legislature.
  • The Cape limited franchise was retained but not extended.
  • Winner-takes-all single constituency system.
  • Prime Minister was leader of the majority party.
  • Symbolic head of state represented the Crown.
  • Parliament was sovereign, subject to limited entrenched manner-and-form requirements.

7. Parliamentary supremacy

Parliamentary supremacy means the legislative body is supreme over the judiciary and executive. Parliament may change or repeal legislation, and old-order courts could not examine the validity of Acts on substantive rights grounds because there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation.

Accuracy warning: South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan because limited entrenched manner-and-form provisions existed under the South Africa Act 1909. Courts could enforce manner-and-form requirements, but not substantive justice.

8. Creation of ANC and land laws

  • SANNC/ANC founded 8 January 1912 in Bloemfontein in response to Union exclusion.
  • 1913 Natives Land Act restricted land ownership and tenancy and attacked sharecropping.
  • 1936 Native Trust and Land Act deepened land division.
  • These laws produced long-term poverty and spatial planning consequences.
  • The land-law framework was replaced in 1991, but its effects persisted.

9. Apartheid legislation

Legislation / systemFunction
Prohibition of Mixed Marriages Act 1949Racial control over marriage and intimate family life.
Population Registration Act 1950Racial classification as a foundation for apartheid administration.
Group Areas Act 1950Spatial segregation and forced removals.
Reservation of Separate Amenities Act 1953Separate public facilities and petty apartheid.
Detention/security legislationRepression of political opposition and resistance.
Separate Representation of Voters Act 1951Attempted removal of Coloured voters from the common roll.

Petty apartheid: everyday segregation of facilities and social life.

Grand apartheid: territorial, political and structural separation through homelands, forced removals and political exclusion.

10. Constitutional crisis and parliamentary supremacy

The crisis concerned entrenched language provisions and limited voting rights of Coloured persons, which required a two-thirds majority. Parliament passed the Separate Representation of Voters Act 1951. Harris I enforced manner-and-form requirements; Harris II rejected the High Court of Parliament; Collins showed that once formal procedure was complied with, courts would not review motive or substantive injustice. In 1956, substantive review was excluded. Verwoerd rejected a Bill of Rights because rights and parliamentary supremacy are inconsistent.

Correct formulation: courts could enforce manner-and-form requirements but not substantive justice; procedural review could delay but not prevent constitutional abuse.

11. Resistance and constitutional vision

Cross-link this to Theme 3 Essay Bank 2. Core markers: Defiance Campaign; Freedom Charter 1955; Sharpeville 1960; Rivonia Trial 1963-64; Soweto uprising; 1983 Constitution and Tricameral Parliament; ANC Constitutional Guidelines 1988.

13. Theme 2 short-answer bank

1. Why does constitutional history matter?

Model answer: It explains why the Constitution was needed, how public power was abused, and why supremacy, rights, accountability and transformation became central.

2. List the five official constitutions.

Model answer: 1910 Union Constitution/South Africa Act 1909, 1961 Republic Constitution, 1983 Tricameral Constitution, 1993 Interim Constitution, and 1996 Final Constitution.

3. Why was the Union created?

Model answer: It unified the Cape, Natal, Orange Free State and Transvaal under British imperial settlement and prioritised white political unity after the South African War.

4. What did the South Africa Act 1909 do?

Model answer: It created the Union of South Africa, a unitary Westminster-style state, brought the four colonies together, retained limited Cape franchise protections and made Parliament sovereign subject to limited manner-and-form requirements.

5. Why was the Union Constitution racially exclusionary?

Model answer: Black South Africans were excluded from constitutional design and white men dominated representation; limited Cape voting rights were retained but not extended.

6. Define parliamentary supremacy.

Model answer: Parliamentary supremacy means the legislative body is supreme over the executive and judiciary and can make, amend or repeal laws without a supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling legislative substance.

7. Why was parliamentary supremacy dangerous in apartheid South Africa?

Model answer: It allowed unjust racial laws to be valid if properly enacted, leaving courts unable to review legislative substance on rights grounds.

8. What were the entrenched provisions?

Model answer: Certain provisions, including language protections and limited Cape voting rights of Coloured persons, required special manner-and-form procedures such as a two-thirds majority.

9. What happened in Harris I?

Model answer: The court held that Parliament had failed to follow the required entrenched procedure when removing voters from the common roll, so the Act was invalid.

10. What happened in Harris II?

Model answer: Parliament created the High Court of Parliament to overturn the Appellate Division, but the court held Parliament could not turn itself into a court of appeal.

11. What happened in Collins?

Model answer: Parliament expanded the Senate, complied with the formal procedure and succeeded; the court would not review the political motive or substantive injustice.

12. Distinguish petty and grand apartheid.

Model answer: Petty apartheid controlled everyday segregation in facilities and social life. Grand apartheid created territorial, political and structural separation through homelands, forced removals and political exclusion.

13. Name apartheid legislation and its function.

Model answer: The Prohibition of Mixed Marriages Act controlled interracial marriage; Population Registration Act classified people racially; Group Areas Act segregated space; Reservation of Separate Amenities Act segregated facilities; security laws enabled detention and repression.

14. Why was the ANC founded in 1912?

Model answer: The SANNC/ANC was founded on 8 January 1912 in Bloemfontein in response to racial exclusion from the Union and the need for organised political resistance.

15. What was the constitutional significance of the Natives Land Act?

Model answer: It entrenched racial land dispossession, limited ownership and tenancy, undermined sharecropping, and created long-term poverty and spatial inequality.

16. What was Sharpeville's significance?

Model answer: Sharpeville exposed violent state repression and intensified national and international pressure against apartheid.

17. What was Rivonia's significance?

Model answer: Rivonia showed legality versus legitimacy: apartheid used formally valid laws to criminalise resistance, exposing why rights and democratic legitimacy mattered.

18. Why was the 1961 Constitution not democratic transformation?

Model answer: It created a republic but preserved white minority rule and parliamentary supremacy rather than inclusive constitutional democracy.

19. Why was the 1983 Constitution not genuine democracy?

Model answer: It created a Tricameral Parliament for white, Coloured and Indian representation while excluding African majority political participation.

20. How does Theme 2 explain the need for constitutional supremacy?

Model answer: It shows that procedural review under parliamentary sovereignty could delay abuse but not prevent substantive injustice; South Africa needed a supreme, rights-based Constitution.

14. Theme 2 MCQ bank

20 Theme 2 MCQs

1. Which instrument created the Union of South Africa?
  • South Africa Act 1909
  • Interim Constitution 1993
  • Citation of Constitutional Laws Act

Answer: South Africa Act 1909

The South Africa Act 1909 established the Union, which began on 31 May 1910.

2. Union was established on:
  • 31 May 1910
  • 4 February 1997
  • 10 December 1996

Answer: 31 May 1910

Royal Proclamation was 2 December 1909; Union began 31 May 1910.

3. The Union Constitution was mainly:
  • Unitary and Westminster-style
  • A justiciable Bill of Rights system
  • A federal equality constitution

Answer: Unitary and Westminster-style

It centralised power and adopted Westminster features.

4. Parliamentary supremacy meant:
  • Parliament was the highest law-maker subject only to limited manner-and-form constraints
  • Courts could strike down all unjust Acts
  • Provinces were sovereign

Answer: Parliament was the highest law-maker subject only to limited manner-and-form constraints

South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan because some entrenched procedure existed.

5. Harris I is about:
  • Failure to follow entrenched procedure
  • High Court of Parliament
  • Senate expansion after formal compliance

Answer: Failure to follow entrenched procedure

Harris I enforced manner-and-form requirements.

6. Harris II is about:
  • High Court of Parliament
  • Senate expansion
  • Sharpeville

Answer: High Court of Parliament

Parliament could not make itself a court to overturn judgments.

7. Collins is about:
  • Senate expansion and formal compliance
  • Invalid High Court of Parliament
  • Freedom Charter adoption

Answer: Senate expansion and formal compliance

Once procedure was followed, courts did not review motive.

8. Petty apartheid means:
  • Everyday segregation of facilities and social life
  • Homelands and political exclusion only
  • Constitutional certification

Answer: Everyday segregation of facilities and social life

Grand apartheid is the structural/territorial system.

9. Grand apartheid means:
  • Territorial, political and structural separation
  • Only separate benches
  • Only mixed-marriage rules

Answer: Territorial, political and structural separation

It includes homelands, forced removals and exclusion.

10. The ANC/SANNC was founded in:
  • 1912
  • 1948
  • 1996

Answer: 1912

Founded 8 January 1912 in Bloemfontein.

11. The 1913 Natives Land Act is significant because it:
  • Entrenched racial land dispossession
  • Created the Constitutional Court
  • Certified the Final Constitution

Answer: Entrenched racial land dispossession

It had long-term property, poverty and spatial consequences.

12. The Freedom Charter was:
  • A political/normative precursor, not binding legal text
  • A court judgment
  • The 1996 Constitution

Answer: A political/normative precursor, not binding legal text

Use it for liberation constitutional vision, not as binding law.

13. Sharpeville occurred in:
  • 1960
  • 1955
  • 1983

Answer: 1960

Sharpeville exposed state violence against protest.

14. Rivonia is useful for:
  • Legality versus legitimacy under apartheid
  • Section 36 proportionality only
  • Municipal street names

Answer: Legality versus legitimacy under apartheid

It shows formal law criminalising resistance without democratic legitimacy.

15. The 1961 Constitution:
  • Created a republic but not genuine democratic transformation
  • Introduced universal adult suffrage
  • Created the Constitutional Court

Answer: Created a republic but not genuine democratic transformation

It preserved white minority rule.

16. The 1983 Constitution:
  • Created Tricameral Parliament but excluded Africans
  • Created inclusive democracy
  • Abolished parliamentary sovereignty

Answer: Created Tricameral Parliament but excluded Africans

It was not genuine democracy.

17. The Interim Constitution was enacted in:
  • 1993
  • 1909
  • 1983

Answer: 1993

The Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993 created the interim order.

18. The Final Constitution commenced on:
  • 4 February 1997
  • 10 December 1996
  • 31 May 1910

Answer: 4 February 1997

It was signed on 10 December 1996 and commenced 4 February 1997.

19. Theme 2 best explains:
  • Why supremacy, rights and transformation were necessary
  • Why old Parliament was always rights-protecting
  • Why courts had full rights review before 1994

Answer: Why supremacy, rights and transformation were necessary

History supplies the reason for the constitutional break.

20. Verwoerd rejected a Bill of Rights because:
  • Rights and parliamentary supremacy are inconsistent
  • Courts already had full review powers
  • The Freedom Charter was binding

Answer: Rights and parliamentary supremacy are inconsistent

A justiciable Bill of Rights limits parliamentary supremacy.

5/10/15-mark versions

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain parliamentary sovereignty and limited judicial review in the 1950s.”

“What do Harris I, Harris II and Collins show about judicial review?”

Answer:

Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was the supreme law-maker. It could make or unmake law, and there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution allowing courts to strike down legislation for substantive injustice. Courts could only conduct limited procedural review, especially where Parliament had to follow entrenched “manner and form” requirements.

The three key cases show this. Harris I held that Parliament had to follow the special procedure when removing voters from the common roll, so the Act was invalid. Harris II held that Parliament could not create a High Court of Parliament to overturn court judgments. Collins held that once Parliament complied with the formal procedure, courts would not question Parliament’s motives or the substance of the law.

Therefore, judicial review in the 1950s was limited: courts could enforce procedure, but not substantive justice.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

Common mistakes / warnings

10. Common Mistakes to Avoid

MistakeWhy it loses marksBetter version
Saying courts had no power at allToo absoluteCourts had limited procedural / manner and form review.
Saying courts could review apartheid laws for injusticeWrongCourts could not conduct substantive rights-based review.
Confusing Harris II and CollinsMajor lecturer warningHarris II = High Court of Parliament; Collins = Senate expansion.
Saying Harris I created constitutional supremacyWrongIt enforced procedure under parliamentary sovereignty.
Saying Collins was about High Court of ParliamentWrongCollins was about Senate expansion and formal compliance.
Ignoring the through-lineLoses impression marksExplain: procedure enforceable, substance not reviewable.
Writing a broad apartheid history dumpSpaghetti answerUse only history that explains the doctrine and cases.
Saying the 1996 Constitution alone abolished parliamentary sovereigntyImpreciseThe Interim Constitution first abolished it; the 1996 Constitution confirmed and deepened it.

11. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton

Parliamentary sovereignty = Parliament supreme, but with no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation.

Memory triggers

11. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton

Parliamentary sovereignty = Parliament supreme, but with no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation.

Courts could not review substance.

Courts could enforce limited procedure / manner and form.

Separate Representation of Voters Act = tried to remove voters from common roll.

Harris I = procedure not followed → Act invalid.

Harris II = High Court of Parliament Act invalid → Parliament cannot become a court.

Collins = Senate expanded + procedure followed → court would not question motive.

Lesson = old judicial review was procedural, not substantive.

Constitutional response = IC/FC created supremacy, Bill of Rights and Constitutional Court.

One-sentence memory trigger

Harris I says Parliament must follow the rules; Harris II says Parliament cannot become a court; Collins says once Parliament follows the rules, courts cannot question the injustice.

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

12. 70 / 80 / 90+ Upgrade Guide

Mark rangeWhat your answer must include
70%Define parliamentary sovereignty; explain limited judicial review; mention Harris I, Harris II and Collins correctly
80%Add manner and form, Separate Representation of Voters Act, High Court of Parliament Act, Senate expansion, no substantive review
90%+Add the through-line, distinguish all three cases sharply, link to constitutional supremacy and explain why procedural legality was insufficient for justice

Final exam warning for this essay

This topic is case-driven. The lecturer’s feedback says marks were awarded for accurately explaining the facts, key principles and arguments of Harris I, Harris II and Collins, with each case carrying marks in the assessment-style question. So for this essay, do not memorise only the doctrine — memorise the three-case sequence.

Theme 3 Essay Bank 1

FINAL CORRECTION PASS: This version applies the final Theme 3 accuracy fixes from the audit cycle. It preserves the essay-bank format while tightening legal precision, authority wording, dates, certification details and exam-risk phrasing.

Parliamentary Sovereignty, Limited Judicial Review and the Constitutional Crisis of the 1950s

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

Possible exam questionWhat the examiner wants
“Explain parliamentary sovereignty in apartheid South Africa.”Definition + old-order legal structure + limits of courts
“Discuss the limited role of judicial review in the 1950s.”Harris I, Harris II, Collins + procedural vs substantive review
“Explain the constitutional crisis caused by the Separate Representation of Voters Act.”Entrenched provisions + common roll + manner and form
“Discuss Harris I, Harris II and Collins.”Accurate facts + legal principles + through-line
“How did parliamentary sovereignty enable apartheid legislation?”Parliament supreme + courts limited + no substantive rights review
“Explain the difference between parliamentary sovereignty and constitutional supremacy.”Old order vs Interim/Final Constitution
“Why was the move to constitutional supremacy revolutionary?”Old limits exposed by Harris/Collins + new justiciable Constitution
“Critically discuss the role of courts under the 1910 constitutional order.”Courts could enforce procedure, but not justice/substance

2. Core Thesis

Under South Africa’s pre-1994 parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament remained the highest law-maker for substantive purposes: courts could not invalidate legislation merely because it was unjust or rights-violating. However, South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan because the South Africa Act 1909 imposed enforceable manner-and-form requirements for certain entrenched matters, including Cape franchise and language protections. The Harris and Collins litigation shows that courts could enforce procedure but not substantive justice, exposing the need for constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights and stronger judicial review.

Use this as the opening sentence for a longer answer.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

The revision lecture frames this topic around parliamentary sovereignty and judicial review, explaining that Parliament could make or unmake law, there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation, and courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament based on substance.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: why this topic matters

The constitutional crisis of the 1950s is important because it shows the limits of judicial review under South Africa’s pre-1994 constitutional order. Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was treated as the supreme law-maker. Courts could interpret legislation and, in limited cases, enforce procedural requirements, but they could not invalidate legislation simply because it was unjust, oppressive or inconsistent with fundamental rights.

This matters because apartheid was built through law. If Parliament had the final say and there was no entrenched, justiciable Bill of Rights, then racially oppressive laws could be legally valid even if they were morally illegitimate. The revision lecture says parliamentary sovereignty enabled apartheid legislation because there were no effective legal checks and balances on Parliament.

The key through-line is therefore:

Harris I shows that courts could enforce procedure; Harris II shows that Parliament could not convert itself into a court; Collins shows that once Parliament complied with procedure, courts could not review the substance or motive of the legislation.

That is the line you must keep clear in the exam.

B. Definition of parliamentary sovereignty

Precision point: South African parliamentary sovereignty was not pure Diceyan parliamentary sovereignty. Parliament was substantively supreme, but the South Africa Act 1909 created limited entrenched provisions and manner-and-form procedures. Courts could enforce those procedures, but they could not invalidate legislation because it was morally unjust, racially oppressive or inconsistent with rights.

Parliamentary sovereignty means that Parliament is the highest law-making authority. In the classical Westminster sense, Parliament can make, amend or repeal any law, and no ordinary court can invalidate an Act of Parliament merely because it is unjust or rights-violating. South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan because limited manner-and-form requirements existed under the South Africa Act 1909.

In the South African pre-1994 context, parliamentary sovereignty meant that:

Parliament was the supreme law-maker;

there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation, although the South Africa Act 1909 imposed limited manner-and-form requirements for entrenched provisions;

courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament based on substantive injustice;

rights protection depended mainly on political will, not entrenched constitutional law;

judicial review was limited mainly to whether correct procedures had been followed.

The revision lecture states this very clearly: Parliament was the supreme law-maker, could make or unmake law, there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation, and courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament based on substance.

Exam sentence

Parliamentary sovereignty meant that courts could not invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive injustice, although they could enforce procedural or “manner and form” requirements where those requirements existed.

That sentence is important because it avoids the inaccurate claim that courts had no review power at all.

C. The old judicial role: limited review, not substantive review

Under the old system, judicial review was narrow. Courts could review whether Parliament had followed required procedures, but they could not ask whether the content of the legislation was consistent with equality, dignity, freedom or rights.

So the old judicial role was:

Type of reviewWas it allowed?Meaning
Procedural / manner and form reviewYes, in limited casesCourts could check whether Parliament followed entrenched procedural requirements.
Substantive rights-based reviewNoCourts could not strike down legislation because it violated rights or was unjust.
Review of political motiveGenerally noCourts would not ask why Parliament secured a majority if the formal procedure was followed.

The revision lecture explains that during apartheid, courts could declare Acts invalid only in a limited procedural sense, and that they could not review legislation based on substance.

D. Historical background: entrenched provisions under the 1910 Constitution

Statutory precision: section 152 of the South Africa Act 1909 supplied the amendment mechanism for altering the Act, subject to entrenched procedures. The protected subject matter included provisions such as the Cape franchise protection in section 35 and language equality protection in section 137. In an exam, the safest phrasing is: the South Africa Act contained entrenched provisions that required special manner-and-form procedures.

The 1910 constitutional framework contained entrenched provisions. The lecture identifies two important entrenched areas:

language provisions; and

limited voting rights / Cape franchise protections, especially affecting Coloured voters and other non-white voters on the common roll.

To amend an entrenched provision, Parliament had to comply with a special procedure, including a two-thirds majority. The constitutional crisis began when Parliament attempted to remove voters from the common roll without following the prescribed procedure.

In 1951, Parliament passed the Separate Representation of Voters Act. Its purpose was to remove Coloured voters from the common roll and place them on a separate roll. Parliament passed the Act with the Houses sitting separately and by simple majority, rather than complying with the special entrenched procedure.

Exam sentence

The crisis was not simply about voting; it was about whether Parliament could evade entrenched constitutional procedures in order to advance racial disenfranchisement.

E. Case 1: Harris I

Facts

In Harris v Minister of the Interior — usually called Harris I in the lectures — Parliament had passed the Separate Representation of Voters Act 1951 to remove voters, especially Coloured voters in the Cape, from the common roll. Parliament did not comply with the special entrenched amendment procedure required by the 1910 constitutional framework.

Issue

Could Parliament validly remove voters from the common roll without complying with the entrenched “manner and form” amendment procedure?

Court reasoning

The Appellate Division said no. Even under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament had to follow the procedural requirements laid down in the Constitution when amending entrenched provisions. The court was not saying that the Act was invalid because racial disenfranchisement was substantively unjust. Rather, it was saying that Parliament had failed to comply with the required procedure.

The revision lecture states that the Appellate Division held Parliament had to follow the “manner and form” requirements, and that the Act was declared invalid because the prescribed procedure had not been followed.

Judgment

The Act was declared invalid.

Constitutional significance

Harris I is important because it shows that courts were not completely powerless. They could enforce procedural limits on Parliament where the Constitution required a specific procedure. The lecture says Harris I recognised that procedural limits on Parliament were enforceable by courts and confirmed the idea of “manner and form” limitations.

Exam use

Use Harris I for:

  1. limited judicial review;
  2. manner and form requirements;
  3. entrenched provisions;
  4. procedural invalidity;
  5. the beginning of the constitutional crisis.

One-sentence case memory

Harris I = Parliament must follow entrenched constitutional procedure; courts could enforce manner and form limits, but not substantive rights.

F. Case 2: Harris II

Facts

After losing in Harris I, the government refused to accept defeat. Parliament passed the High Court of Parliament Act 35 of 1952. This Act created a body made up of Members of Parliament and gave it the power to review and overturn decisions of the Appellate Division. In effect, Parliament tried to make itself the final court of appeal in constitutional matters.

Issue

Could Parliament create a parliamentary body that functioned like a court and could overturn judgments of the Appellate Division?

Court reasoning

The Appellate Division held that Parliament could not do this. The issue was not that Parliament could never regulate courts, but that it could not create an artificial parliamentary body outside the constitutional framework to function as a court of appeal over Appellate Division judgments or to override judicial decisions. This protected a limited boundary between legislative and judicial authority, even under the old order.

Judgment

The High Court of Parliament Act was declared invalid.

Constitutional significance

Harris II is important because it asserted a limited form of judicial independence and separation of powers under the old order. It did not create modern constitutional supremacy, but it did show that Parliament could not openly collapse the distinction between Parliament and the courts.

Exam use

Use Harris II for:

High Court of Parliament Act;

separation of powers;

judicial independence;

Parliament cannot turn itself into a court;

distinction from Collins.

One-sentence case memory

Harris II = Parliament cannot create an artificial parliamentary court to overturn judicial decisions.

G. Case 3: Collins v Minister of the Interior

Facts

Collins involved a different strategy from Harris II. Instead of trying to turn Parliament into a court, the government expanded the Senate to obtain the required majority and then passed the amendment through a joint sitting. The case challenged the validity of this change, arguing that Parliament had manipulated its own composition to secure the constitutional majority.

Issue

Could the court invalidate the Senate changes and constitutional amendments because Parliament had manipulated the Senate to achieve the required majority?

Court reasoning

The Appellate Division upheld the validity of the Senate Act and related constitutional amendments. Its reasoning was strictly formal: Parliament had technically complied with the required manner-and-form procedure by using a joint sitting and securing the necessary two-thirds majority, even though that majority had been achieved through Senate enlargement. Once those formal steps were satisfied, the Court refused to examine political motive or substantive injustice.

Judgment

The court upheld the legislation and amendments.

Constitutional significance

Collins is crucial because it shows the ultimate weakness of manner-and-form review under parliamentary sovereignty. Procedural review could delay constitutional abuse, but it could not prevent it once Parliament mastered the formal procedural steps. If Parliament followed the required procedure, courts could not examine the substance of the law, the political motive behind it or its racially oppressive impact.

Exam use

Use Collins for:

limits of judicial review;

procedural compliance;

no inquiry into political motive;

no substantive review;

court subordination under parliamentary sovereignty.

One-sentence case memory

Collins = once Parliament followed formal procedure, courts could not question the substance, motive or injustice of the law.

H. Harris I vs Harris II vs Collins: do not confuse them

This is the exam danger zone.

CaseWhat happenedCourt’s answerCore principle
Harris IParliament removed voters from the common roll without following entrenched procedure.Invalid.Parliament must comply with manner and form requirements.
Harris IIParliament created the High Court of Parliament to overturn the Appellate Division.Invalid.Parliament cannot turn itself into a court or retroactively set aside court decisions.
CollinsParliament expanded the Senate, secured the majority, and followed formal procedure.Valid.Once formal procedure is followed, courts will not review motive or substance.

The A1 feedback specifically warns that students lost marks by confusing Harris II and Collins. Harris II is about the High Court of Parliament Act and judicial independence; Collins is about the enlargement of the Senate and Parliament complying with formal procedure despite improper political motives.

I. What the three cases collectively show

Together, Harris I, Harris II and Collins show the limited nature of judicial review in the old order.

They show that courts could:

enforce entrenched procedural requirements;

prevent Parliament from turning itself into a court;

protect some basic institutional boundaries.

But they could not:

invalidate legislation because it violated equality or dignity;

review the substance of apartheid legislation;

invalidate laws merely because Parliament’s motive was racially oppressive;

protect rights through a justiciable Bill of Rights;

treat the Constitution as supreme over Parliament.

The lecturer’s feedback says the key thematic elements are parliamentary sovereignty, subordination of the courts, and the inability of courts to review the substance of legislation, focusing instead on limited procedural aspects.

Exam sentence

The 1950s cases show that the courts had enough power to insist on procedure, but not enough power to prevent Parliament from using law as an instrument of racial exclusion.

J. Why this created a constitutional crisis

The crisis was not only legal. It was also political and moral.

The apartheid government wanted to remove vulnerable voters from the common roll. The courts briefly blocked this in Harris I by enforcing procedure. Parliament then attempted to bypass the courts through the High Court of Parliament Act, which failed in Harris II. Finally, Parliament succeeded through the Collins route by manipulating the Senate and then complying with formal procedure.

This revealed the weakness of parliamentary sovereignty:

Procedure could slow Parliament down, but not stop racially oppressive law.

Courts could not review substantive injustice.

Parliament could eventually achieve racial disenfranchisement if it followed the formal steps.

Rights had no strong entrenched protection.

Judicial review lacked the force of constitutional supremacy.

The revision lecture’s summary captures this: Harris I = Parliament must follow the rules; Harris II = Parliament cannot turn itself into a court; Collins = if Parliament follows the rules, courts cannot question what it does.

K. Link to constitutional supremacy

The later move to constitutional supremacy directly responds to this weakness.

Under constitutional supremacy:

  1. the Constitution is the highest law;
  2. legislation must comply with the Constitution;
  3. rights are entrenched and justiciable;
  4. courts can review law and conduct for constitutional validity;
  5. Parliament is no longer legally unlimited.

The Theme 3 slides show that the Interim Constitution brought fundamental changes, including the shift from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy, the creation of a new independent Constitutional Court, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a certification mechanism, multi-party democracy and separation of powers.

The safest precise wording is:

Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect. The 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened constitutional supremacy.

This avoids the imprecise “1993 or 1996” wording that caused problems in the Theme 4 audit.

L. Conclusion

High-value theoretical link: This old-order system reflects what Mureinik later described as a culture of authority: law demanded obedience because it came from Parliament, not because public power had to justify itself through rights and reasons.

Parliamentary sovereignty in pre-1994 South Africa meant that Parliament had the final say over legislation and courts could not invalidate laws merely because they were unjust or rights-violating. The Harris and Collins cases reveal both the limited power and ultimate weakness of judicial review under that system. Harris I shows that courts could enforce manner and form requirements. Harris II shows that Parliament could not turn itself into a court. Collins shows that once Parliament complied with formal procedure, courts would not question the substance or motive of legislation. This constitutional crisis exposed why South Africa needed constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights, an independent Constitutional Court and stronger judicial review.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain parliamentary sovereignty and limited judicial review in the 1950s.”

“What do Harris I, Harris II and Collins show about judicial review?”

Answer:

Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was the supreme law-maker. It could make or unmake law, and there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution allowing courts to strike down legislation for substantive injustice. Courts could only conduct limited procedural review, especially where Parliament had to follow entrenched “manner and form” requirements.

The three key cases show this. Harris I held that Parliament had to follow the special procedure when removing voters from the common roll, so the Act was invalid. Harris II held that Parliament could not create a High Court of Parliament to overturn court judgments. Collins held that once Parliament complied with the formal procedure, courts would not question Parliament’s motives or the substance of the law.

Therefore, judicial review in the 1950s was limited: courts could enforce procedure, but not substantive justice.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Discuss the limited nature of judicial review under parliamentary sovereignty.”

“Explain Harris I, Harris II and Collins in relation to parliamentary sovereignty.”

Answer:

Under South Africa’s pre-1994 system of parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was the highest law-making authority. The revision lecture explains that Parliament could make or unmake law, there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation, and courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament based on substance. Judicial review was limited mainly to checking whether correct procedures had been followed.

The constitutional crisis began when Parliament passed the Separate Representation of Voters Act in 1951 to remove voters from the common roll without complying with the entrenched amendment procedure. The 1910 constitutional framework protected certain entrenched provisions, including language and limited voting rights, which required a special procedure and two-thirds majority to amend.

In Harris I, the Appellate Division held that Parliament had not validly removed the voters because it failed to follow the required manner and form procedure. The case is important because it shows that even under parliamentary sovereignty, courts could enforce procedural constitutional limits.

In Harris II, Parliament attempted to bypass Harris I by passing the High Court of Parliament Act 35 of 1952. This Act created a parliamentary body with power to review and overturn Appellate Division decisions. The Appellate Division declared the Act invalid because Parliament could not create a parliamentary body outside the constitutional framework to function as a court of appeal over Appellate Division judgments or to override judicial decisions.

In Collins, Parliament used a different strategy: it expanded the Senate, secured the required majority and passed the amendments through a joint sitting. The Appellate Division upheld the legislation because Parliament had complied with the formal manner and form requirements. The Court refused to investigate Parliament’s political motives.

Together, the cases show that judicial review under parliamentary sovereignty was procedurally possible but substantively weak. Courts could insist on proper form, but they could not invalidate laws merely because they were racially oppressive or rights-violating. This explains why South Africa later needed constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights and stronger judicial review.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss parliamentary sovereignty and judicial review in South Africa during the 1950s.”

“Explain the constitutional crisis involving Harris I, Harris II and Collins.”

“How did the limits of old-order judicial review help explain South Africa’s later shift to constitutional supremacy?”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: parliamentary sovereignty + limited judicial review
2Define parliamentary sovereignty
3Explain limited judicial review
4Entrenched provisions and Separate Representation of Voters Act
5Harris I
6Harris II
7Collins
8What the three cases collectively show
9Link to constitutional supremacy
10Critical conclusion

15-mark full answer

South Africa’s 1950s constitutional crisis shows the limits of judicial review under parliamentary sovereignty. Under the old order, Parliament was the highest law-making authority and courts could not invalidate legislation merely because it was unjust, oppressive or inconsistent with fundamental rights. However, courts could enforce limited procedural or “manner and form” requirements where the constitutional structure required them.

Parliamentary sovereignty meant that Parliament had ultimate substantive legislative authority, but South Africa’s model was not fully Diceyan because entrenched provisions in the South Africa Act 1909 imposed procedural manner-and-form limits in specific areas. The revision lecture states that Parliament was the supreme law-maker, could make or unmake law, there was no supreme, fully justiciable Constitution controlling the substance of legislation, and courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament based on substance.

Judicial review was therefore narrow. Courts could review whether correct procedures were followed, but not whether the substance of a law violated dignity, equality, freedom or basic rights. This meant apartheid legislation could be legally valid even where it was morally illegitimate. The lecturer’s feedback identifies the key elements as parliamentary sovereignty, subordination of courts and the inability of courts to review the substance of legislation.

The crisis began because the 1910 constitutional framework contained entrenched provisions, including language protections and limited Cape franchise protections. To amend entrenched provisions, Parliament had to comply with a special procedure, including a two-thirds majority. In 1951, Parliament passed the Separate Representation of Voters Act to remove voters from the common roll without complying with the required entrenched procedure.

In Harris I, the Appellate Division held that Parliament had failed to comply with the required manner and form procedure. The Act was declared invalid. The significance of Harris I is that courts could enforce procedural constitutional limits even under parliamentary sovereignty. It did not mean the court had a general power to invalidate apartheid laws for substantive injustice.

After Harris I, Parliament passed the High Court of Parliament Act 35 of 1952. This created a body composed of Members of Parliament with power to review and overturn Appellate Division judgments. In Harris II, the Appellate Division declared this Act invalid. The reasoning was that Parliament could not create a parliamentary body outside the constitutional framework to function as a court of appeal over Appellate Division judgments or to override judicial decisions. Harris II is therefore important for separation of powers and judicial independence.

In Collins, Parliament used a different method. It expanded the Senate to secure the required majority and then passed the amendments through a joint sitting. The Appellate Division upheld the legislation because Parliament had complied with the formal manner and form requirements. The court refused to inquire into Parliament’s political motives.

The three cases must be distinguished carefully. Harris I is about failure to comply with entrenched procedure. Harris II is about Parliament attempting to turn itself into a court through the High Court of Parliament Act. Collins is about Parliament complying with the formal procedure after expanding the Senate. The A1 feedback warns that students often confuse Harris II and Collins, even though they involve different facts and principles.

Together, the cases reveal the weakness of parliamentary sovereignty. Courts could enforce procedure, but not substantive justice. Once Parliament complied with form, the courts could not question the content, motive or rights impact of the law. This meant Parliament could use law to advance racial exclusion while remaining formally valid.

This history explains the later shift to constitutional supremacy. Under the Interim Constitution, South Africa moved from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy, created a new Constitutional Court, adopted a justiciable Bill of Rights and introduced a certification mechanism. The Final Constitution confirmed and deepened that change.

In conclusion, the 1950s constitutional crisis shows why procedural legality without substantive constitutional supremacy was insufficient. Harris I and Harris II show that courts could enforce some structural limits, but Collins shows that those limits could be defeated through formal compliance. The later constitutional revolution replaced this weak model with a supreme Constitution, entrenched rights and meaningful judicial review.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the constitutional significance of the Freedom Charter.”

“How did early resistance shape South Africa’s constitutional vision?”

“Why is Rivonia important for constitutional law?”

Answer:

Early anti-apartheid resistance shaped South Africa’s constitutional vision because it exposed the difference between formal legality and justice. The Defiance Campaign deliberately defied unjust apartheid laws to show their illegitimacy. It also built awareness and helped develop non-racial cooperation in resistance.

The Freedom Charter, adopted at Kliptown in 1955 after a mass democratic process, declared that South Africa belongs to all who live in it and envisioned equality, shared citizenship, universal suffrage, human rights, land reform, housing, education and work. Although not legally binding, it became a foundational ideological document and normative precursor to the 1996 Constitution.

The Rivonia Trial later showed how apartheid law could be weaponised against resistance. It demonstrated why South Africa needed entrenched rights, fair trial protections, constitutional supremacy and limits on legislative power.

90%+Add law vs justice analysis, non-racial constitutional vision, normative precursor language, caution that Freedom Charter was not binding, and clear links to constitutional supremacy and a justiciable Bill of Rights

Final exam warning for this essay

Do not dump every historical event. Pick the events the question asks for and link them to constitutional principles. The lecturer’s assessment guidance says marks are awarded for content, structure and application, and that university answers must show more than memory recall.

Next should be Essay Bank 3: CODESA, Violence and the Two-Stage Transition — Legitimacy, Compromise and Constitutional Design.

Theme 3 essay 2: Theme 3 Essay Bank 2

Liberation Constitutional Vision and Early Resistance: From the Freedom Charter to Rivonia

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

Possible exam questionWhat the examiner wants
“Explain the constitutional significance of the Freedom Charter.”Freedom Charter as normative precursor to the 1996 Constitution
“Discuss how early anti-apartheid resistance shaped South Africa’s constitutional vision.”Defiance Campaign, Congress Alliance, Congress of the People, Freedom Charter
“Explain the relationship between law and justice using the Rivonia Trial.”Apartheid legality vs moral legitimacy

“Why was the liberation movement’s constitutional vision important?”Equality, citizenship, universal suffrage, socio-economic rights
“Discuss the significance of the ANC Constitutional Guidelines.”1988 constitutionalism, justiciable Bill of Rights, non-racial/non-sexist democracy
“How did apartheid law show the need for entrenched constitutional rights?”Repressive laws, detention, sabotage legislation, Rivonia
