2. Core Thesis

Early anti-apartheid resistance was not only political protest; it produced a constitutional vision of non-racial democracy, equal citizenship, universal suffrage, human rights and socio-economic justice. From the Freedom Charter to Sharpeville and Rivonia, the liberation struggle exposed the gap between law and justice under apartheid and helped explain why South Africa later needed constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights and a transformative constitutional order.

Use this as your opening paragraph for a longer answer.

3. Key Authorities and Events to Remember

Authority / eventUse
Defiance Campaign, 1952Resistance to unjust laws; awareness building; non-racial cooperation
Congress Alliance, 1954Coalition of anti-apartheid organisations; non-racial constitutional vision
Congress of the People, 1955Participatory process; ordinary people asked what SA should become
Freedom Charter, 1955Equality, shared citizenship, socio-economic rights, universal suffrage
Sharpeville, 1960State violence, emergency powers, banning of ANC/PAC, international criticism
1961 ConstitutionRepublic, retained parliamentary system and racial exclusion
Rivonia Trial, 1963–1964Apartheid law weaponised against resistance; law vs justice
1983 ConstitutionTricameral Parliament; limited reform; exclusion of Black African majority
ANC Constitutional Guidelines, 1988Future constitutional vision: democracy, non-racism, non-sexism, rights, participation

The Theme 3 lecture places these events on the road to constitutional change: the Freedom Charter, Sharpeville, Rivonia, the 1983 Constitution and the 1988 Constitutional Guidelines are used to explain why South Africa’s later negotiated transition was radical despite being negotiated.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: resistance as constitutional imagination

The liberation struggle must not be treated only as background history. It is part of South Africa’s constitutional development because resistance movements did not merely oppose apartheid; they imagined a different constitutional order. They challenged racial exclusion, parliamentary supremacy, authoritarian law, unequal citizenship and the use of law as an instrument of repression.

This matters because the 1996 Constitution did not appear from nowhere. Its commitments to dignity, equality, freedom, universal suffrage, non-racialism, non-sexism, rights and socio-economic transformation were shaped by decades of resistance, constitutional imagination and struggle.

The lecturer’s slides describe this historical movement as part of South Africa’s “constitutional revolution”: before 1994 there was parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion from citizenship, no entrenched Bill of Rights and an authoritarian security state; after 1994 there was constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage and a justiciable Bill of Rights.

B. Defiance Campaign: resisting legality without justice

The Defiance Campaign showed that a law can be legally valid but morally unjust. The campaign involved volunteers deliberately defying apartheid laws in order to expose their injustice. The lecture notes that the first group of volunteers included Nelson Mandela, Yusuf Dadoo, Moses Kotane and Walter Sisulu, and that their goal was to defy apartheid laws in Johannesburg and other major cities, be arrested, show the unjustness of those laws, and ultimately change or abolish them.

The campaign was not immediately successful in changing the laws, but it was successful in awareness-building. It also led to international recognition, including a United Nations Commission investigating unjust racial policies. Importantly, it marked the beginning of non-racial cooperation in resistance, later cemented by the Congress Alliance.

Constitutional significance

The Defiance Campaign shows the difference between legality and legitimacy. Apartheid laws were formally valid under parliamentary sovereignty, but they lacked moral and democratic legitimacy because they were imposed by a racially exclusive state.

Exam sentence

The Defiance Campaign is constitutionally significant because it exposed the inadequacy of formal legality: law cannot claim legitimate authority merely because Parliament enacted it if the people governed by it are excluded from political power and treated unequally.

C. Congress Alliance: non-racial constitutional cooperation

The Congress Alliance was a coalition of anti-apartheid organisations formed in the early 1950s to coordinate resistance against apartheid laws. It included the African National Congress, South African Coloured Peoples Organization, Congress of Democrats, South African Congress of Trade Unions and others.

Its significance is that it was explicitly non-racial at a time when apartheid enforced racial division. It developed a shared constitutional vision of equality and citizenship and organised the Congress of the People in 1955.

Constitutional significance

The Congress Alliance matters because it anticipated core values later entrenched in the Constitution, especially:

  • non-racialism;
  • equality;
  • shared citizenship;
  • democratic participation;
  • social justice.

Exam sentence

The Congress Alliance challenged apartheid’s racial constitutional order by developing a non-racial vision of citizenship before formal constitutional democracy existed.

D. Congress of the People: participatory constitutional imagination

The Congress of the People was held in Kliptown near Johannesburg on 26 June 1955. About 3000 delegates from across South Africa gathered. The process began with a mass campaign asking ordinary people what kind of South Africa they wanted, and it culminated in the adoption of the Freedom Charter.

This is very important for constitutional law because it shows that constitutional ideas were being generated through popular participation long before the formal constitutional negotiations of the 1990s.

Constitutional significance

The Congress of the People matters because it was a form of popular constitutional imagination. Ordinary people were asked to participate in articulating the values of a future South Africa.

Exam sentence

The Congress of the People is important because it shows that South Africa’s later constitutional democracy was preceded by a participatory liberation vision, not only by elite negotiation.

E. Freedom Charter: normative precursor to the Final Constitution

The Freedom Charter is one of the most important historical documents for Theme 3. It declared that South Africa belongs to all who live in it and envisioned equality, shared citizenship and socio-economic rights. The lecture describes it as the normative precursor to the 1996 Final Constitution.

Its key principles included:

universal suffrage and democratic government;

equality before the law;

human rights and freedoms, including speech, assembly and movement;

redistribution of land and economic opportunity;

access to housing, education and work.

The lecture also stresses that although the Freedom Charter was not legally binding, it became a foundational ideological document for the liberation movement.

Woolman and Swanepoel make the same point more academically: South Africa’s Final Constitution has a history, and an important part of that history is the adoption of the Freedom Charter. They specifically say that the Final Constitution owes a debt to the Freedom Charter as a founding document of South Africa’s liberation movements.

Constitutional significance

The Freedom Charter is not a statute and not a court judgment. Its importance is normative and historical. It gave content to the liberation movement’s constitutional vision and anticipated many themes later found in the Constitution:

Freedom Charter ideaLater constitutional connection
“South Africa belongs to all who live in it”Preamble: South Africa belongs to all who live in it, united in diversity
Universal suffrageConstitutional democracy and political rights
Equality before the lawEquality, non-racialism and non-sexism
Speech, assembly and movementCivil and political rights
Housing, education and workSocio-economic rights and transformation
Land and economic opportunityRedress and substantive equality

Exam sentence

The Freedom Charter was not legally binding, but it was constitutionally significant because it articulated a liberation vision of equal citizenship, democracy, human rights and socio-economic justice that later influenced the values and structure of the Final Constitution.

F. Sharpeville: state violence and the need for rights-limited power

The Sharpeville Massacre is a constitutional turning point because it exposed the violent nature of apartheid legality. The road to Sharpeville began with the anti-pass campaign. In March 1960, Robert Sobukwe and the PAC announced a mass pass-law protest, and PAC activists called on Black South Africans to leave their pass books at home and voluntarily present themselves at police stations for arrest. The aim was to overwhelm the apartheid system and challenge pass laws directly.

On 21 March 1960, thousands marched peacefully to the Sharpeville police station without passbooks. Police opened fire on the crowd. The lecture records that 69 people were killed and hundreds were injured.

The government response intensified repression. A state of emergency was declared on 30 March 1960, used to suppress protest and detain thousands of people. The ANC and PAC were banned under the Unlawful Organisations Act. The lecture states that this marked a shift to a more violent and repressive phase of apartheid and led to international criticism.

Constitutional significance

Sharpeville shows why the later Constitution needed:

  1. entrenched rights;
  2. freedom of assembly;
  3. freedom of expression;
  4. protection against arbitrary detention;
  5. limits on emergency powers;
  6. accountability for state violence;
  7. constitutional supremacy over security-state power.

Exam sentence

Sharpeville is constitutionally significant because it showed that without entrenched rights and limits on public power, the state could respond to peaceful protest with violence, emergency rule and political banning while remaining formally within apartheid legality.

G. 1961 Constitution: republic without democratic transformation

After Sharpeville, South Africa’s constitutional order did not become democratic. The 1961 Constitution removed the British monarch as head of state and made South Africa a republic. The lecture notes that a white-only referendum approved the move to become a republic and that the 1961 Constitution retained the parliamentary system from the 1910 Constitution. It reinforced white minority dominance and continued to exclude Black South Africans from the political process.

Accuracy note: the slide appears to contain a typo saying “32 May 1961”. The historically accepted date for South Africa becoming a republic is 31 May 1961. The legal point for this essay is not the exact date but the constitutional continuity: the republic retained parliamentary structures and racial exclusion.

Constitutional significance

The 1961 Constitution shows that formal constitutional change does not necessarily produce democratic transformation. South Africa became a republic, but not a constitutional democracy.

Exam sentence

The 1961 Constitution changed the symbolic head of state, but it did not cure the deeper constitutional problem of racial exclusion and parliamentary supremacy.

H. Rivonia Trial: law, justice and political legitimacy

Authority precision: when mentioning Rivonia, name the repressive statutes if space allows: the General Law Amendment Act 76 of 1962 (the Sabotage Act) and the Suppression of Communism Act 44 of 1950. This strengthens the point that apartheid used formally valid law to criminalise resistance.

The Rivonia Trial is one of the strongest examples of the difference between law and justice under apartheid.

The lecture explains that the trial was held from 1963–1964 and resulted in life imprisonment for Nelson Mandela and other anti-apartheid leaders. It became a historical turning point and drew international scrutiny to apartheid.

The trialists were charged under laws designed to criminalise resistance to racial oppression. The lecture says the case demonstrates how a legal system can be weaponised by those in power against the very people it should protect, and why constitutions must entrench rights that constrain legislative power, not merely procedural rules.

The People’s Law Journal reading gives more detail: by the time the Rivonia Trial began on 26 November 1963, the apartheid state had created an intimidating legal framework to stamp out political dissent. The trialists were charged under laws including the General Law Amendment Act 1962, commonly known as the Sabotage Act, the Suppression of Communism Act and the Criminal Law Amendment Act.

Rather than mount only a technical criminal defence, the accused put apartheid itself on trial. The lecture explains that Mandela’s “I am prepared to die” statement reframed the proceedings as a question of political legitimacy: can a government that excludes the majority from political participation claim the moral authority to prosecute them for defying its laws?

Constitutional significance

Rivonia matters because it shows:

law can be used to repress rather than protect;

formal legality is not the same as justice;

political legitimacy depends on democratic inclusion;

rights must be entrenched and enforceable;

courts in authoritarian systems may be constrained by unjust legal frameworks;

constitutional design after apartheid had to respond to the abuse of criminal law and emergency powers.

The lecture summarises Rivonia as a foundational case study in the difference between law and justice, the role of courts in authoritarian systems, why entrenched constitutional rights matter, and how historical abuse shapes constitutional design.

Exam sentence

Rivonia shows that apartheid’s legal system could prosecute resistance through valid-looking statutes, but the trial exposed the deeper constitutional problem: a state that excludes the majority from political participation lacks democratic legitimacy when it criminalises their struggle for freedom.

I. 1983 Constitution: limited reform without equal citizenship

The 1983 Constitution is important because it shows that apartheid attempted reform without giving up racial domination. The lecture describes it as a key reform under the apartheid regime intended to entrench apartheid rule while responding to internal protest and international pressure. It created the Tricameral Parliament, with separate houses for white, Coloured and Indian population groups, while completely excluding the Black African majority from central government representation.

The 1983 system also created “own affairs” and “general affairs” categories, abolished the office of Prime Minister, and created an executive State President.

Constitutional significance

The 1983 Constitution shows that partial inclusion is not constitutional democracy. It widened participation for some groups while preserving white control and excluding the majority.

Exam sentence

The 1983 Constitution illustrates the failure of limited racial reform: it created institutional change without universal citizenship, equal political participation or genuine constitutional democracy.

J. ANC Constitutional Guidelines: from resistance to constitutional design

By the late 1980s, the liberation movement’s constitutional vision became more formal. The ANC Constitutional Guidelines for a Democratic South Africa set out the ANC’s plan for a future South Africa and invited people and organisations to discuss it. The document explained that people in South Africa could be part of shaping the future.

The Guidelines were based on democracy, an end to racism and an end to sexism. They spoke about creating a society where people could live as free citizens in a free country, have a say at all levels of government, and address issues such as land, workers, women and family.

Woolman and Swanepoel explain that in 1988 the ANC published the Constitutional Guidelines for a Democratic South Africa as its first public expression of a desire to work towards a negotiated settlement, and that the ANC committed itself to a justiciable Bill of Rights and constitutionalism generally.

Constitutional significance

The Guidelines matter because they show the move from resistance to constitutional design. They connect the Freedom Charter’s broad ideals to a more concrete constitutional programme based on:

democracy;

non-racism;

non-sexism;

participation;

rights;

land reform;

workers’ rights;

women’s equality;

social justice;

constitutionalism.

Exam sentence

The ANC Constitutional Guidelines are significant because they translated liberation ideals into a more concrete constitutional vision, including democracy, non-racism, non-sexism, participation, a justiciable Bill of Rights and constitutionalism.

K. How this history links to the 1996 Constitution

The key is not to write this as disconnected history. Link every event to the later constitutional order.

Historical experienceConstitutional lesson
Defiance CampaignFormal legality can be unjust; people may resist illegitimate laws.
Congress AllianceNon-racial cooperation is necessary in a racially divided society.
Freedom CharterEqual citizenship, universal suffrage and socio-economic justice form the normative foundation.
SharpevillePublic power must be limited by rights, accountability and controls over state violence.
RivoniaCriminal law can be weaponised; entrenched rights and fair trial protections are necessary.
1983 ConstitutionPartial reform without equal citizenship is not democracy.
ANC GuidelinesLiberation vision becomes a concrete constitutional programme.

Exam sentence

These events show that South Africa’s constitutional revolution was not only negotiated in the 1990s; it was prepared by decades of resistance that identified the values and institutional protections the new Constitution would need.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the constitutional significance of the Freedom Charter.”

“How did early resistance shape South Africa’s constitutional vision?”

“Why is Rivonia important for constitutional law?”

Answer:

Early anti-apartheid resistance shaped South Africa’s constitutional vision because it exposed the difference between formal legality and justice. The Defiance Campaign deliberately defied unjust apartheid laws to show their illegitimacy. It also built awareness and helped develop non-racial cooperation in resistance.

The Freedom Charter, adopted at Kliptown in 1955 after a mass democratic process, declared that South Africa belongs to all who live in it and envisioned equality, shared citizenship, universal suffrage, human rights, land reform, housing, education and work. Although not legally binding, it became a foundational ideological document and normative precursor to the 1996 Constitution.

The Rivonia Trial later showed how apartheid law could be weaponised against resistance. It demonstrated why South Africa needed entrenched rights, fair trial protections, constitutional supremacy and limits on legislative power.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Discuss the constitutional significance of early anti-apartheid resistance.”

“Explain how the Freedom Charter, Sharpeville and Rivonia shaped South Africa’s constitutional development.”

Answer:

Early anti-apartheid resistance is constitutionally significant because it produced a vision of a democratic, non-racial and rights-based South Africa before formal constitutional negotiations began. The resistance movement challenged apartheid’s claim that formally valid law was automatically legitimate.

The Defiance Campaign involved volunteers deliberately defying apartheid laws in order to be arrested and expose the injustice of those laws. The lecture notes that it did not immediately change the laws, but it succeeded in awareness-building and helped produce international recognition through a United Nations Commission investigating unjust racial policies. This shows the gap between legality and legitimacy.

The Congress Alliance then developed a non-racial constitutional vision. It brought together organisations such as the ANC, South African Coloured Peoples Organization, Congress of Democrats and South African Congress of Trade Unions. It organised the Congress of the People, held in Kliptown on 26 June 1955, where about 3000 delegates adopted the Freedom Charter after a mass democratic process asking ordinary people what South Africa should become.

The Freedom Charter declared that South Africa belongs to all who live in it and envisioned universal suffrage, equality before the law, human rights, land and economic opportunity, housing, education and work. Although not legally binding, it became a foundational ideological document for the liberation movement and a normative precursor to the Final Constitution.

Sharpeville and Rivonia showed why this constitutional vision was necessary. Sharpeville exposed the violence of the apartheid state, leading to a state of emergency, mass detentions and the banning of the ANC and PAC. Rivonia showed how criminal law could be weaponised against resistance and why entrenched constitutional rights were necessary to constrain legislative and executive power.

Therefore, early resistance shaped the later Constitution by identifying the values and protections that apartheid lacked: equal citizenship, universal suffrage, rights, accountability, non-racialism, social justice and constitutional limits on state power.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss how the liberation struggle shaped South Africa’s constitutional vision.”

“Explain the constitutional significance of the Freedom Charter, Sharpeville and Rivonia.”

“How did early resistance expose the need for constitutional supremacy and a justiciable Bill of Rights?”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: resistance produced constitutional vision
2Defiance Campaign and legality vs justice
3Congress Alliance and non-racial constitutionalism
4Congress of the People and participatory constitutional imagination
5Freedom Charter as normative precursor
6Sharpeville and state violence
71961 Constitution and republic without democracy
8Rivonia Trial and law weaponised
91983 Constitution and failed partial reform
10ANC Constitutional Guidelines
11Link to 1996 Constitution
12Conclusion

15-mark full answer

Early anti-apartheid resistance was not only political protest. It produced a constitutional vision of non-racial democracy, equal citizenship, universal suffrage, human rights and socio-economic justice. This history is important because the 1996 Constitution did not emerge in a vacuum. It responded to apartheid’s use of law as an instrument of exclusion and repression.

The Defiance Campaign showed the difference between legality and justice. Volunteers deliberately defied apartheid laws, intending to be arrested in order to expose the injustice of those laws. The lecture notes that the campaign did not immediately change the laws, but it built awareness and resulted in international recognition, including a United Nations Commission investigating unjust racial policies. This matters constitutionally because it showed that formal legal validity does not automatically create legitimate authority.

The Congress Alliance developed this resistance into a non-racial constitutional vision. It was a coalition of anti-apartheid organisations, including the ANC, South African Coloured Peoples Organization, Congress of Democrats and South African Congress of Trade Unions. It was explicitly non-racial at a time when apartheid enforced racial division and developed a shared constitutional vision of equality and citizenship.

The Congress of the People was held in Kliptown on 26 June 1955. Around 3000 delegates gathered after a mass democratic process asking ordinary people what kind of South Africa they wanted. The result was the adoption of the Freedom Charter. This is constitutionally significant because it shows participatory constitutional imagination before formal constitutional democracy.

The Freedom Charter declared that South Africa belongs to all who live in it and envisioned equality, shared citizenship and socio-economic rights. It included principles such as universal suffrage, equality before the law, freedoms of speech, assembly and movement, land and economic opportunity, and access to housing, education and work. Although it was not legally binding, it became a foundational ideological document for the liberation movement and a normative precursor to the Final Constitution. Woolman and Swanepoel also emphasise that the Final Constitution owes a debt to the Freedom Charter as a founding document of the liberation movements.

The Sharpeville Massacre showed the violence of apartheid public power. On 21 March 1960, thousands marched peacefully to the Sharpeville police station without passbooks, and police opened fire. The lecture records that 69 people were killed and hundreds injured. The government then declared a state of emergency, detained thousands and banned the ANC and PAC. This demonstrated why a future Constitution needed rights protecting assembly, expression, liberty, fair process and accountability for state violence.

The 1961 Constitution did not solve the constitutional problem. It created a republic and removed the British monarch as head of state, but retained the parliamentary system and continued to exclude Black South Africans from the political process. This shows that formal constitutional change is not the same as democratic transformation.

The Rivonia Trial then exposed how apartheid law could be weaponised against resistance. The trial was held from 1963 to 1964 and resulted in life imprisonment for Mandela and other anti-apartheid leaders. The trialists were charged under laws designed to criminalise resistance, including security and sabotage legislation. The People’s Law Journal explains that by the time the trial began on 26 November 1963, the apartheid state had created an intimidating legal framework to stamp out political dissent, including the Sabotage Act and Suppression of Communism Act.

Rather than mount only a technical defence, the accused used the trial to put apartheid itself on trial. Mandela’s statement reframed the proceedings as a question of political legitimacy: whether a government that excluded the majority from political participation could claim the moral authority to prosecute resistance. Rivonia therefore shows why entrenched rights and constitutional review are necessary: without them, law can become a tool of repression.

The 1983 Constitution also showed the limits of partial reform. It created a Tricameral Parliament with houses for white, Coloured and Indian groups, but completely excluded the Black African majority from central government. It was designed to broaden support for white minority rule while fracturing non-racial dissent. This was not genuine constitutional democracy because it lacked universal citizenship and equal political participation.

By 1988, the liberation movement’s constitutional vision had become more concrete through the ANC Constitutional Guidelines for a Democratic South Africa. Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Guidelines were the ANC’s first public expression of a desire to work towards a negotiated settlement and that the ANC committed itself to a justiciable Bill of Rights and constitutionalism generally. The Guidelines also invited broad discussion and were based on democracy, non-racism, non-sexism, rights and participation.

This history links directly to the 1996 Constitution. The Freedom Charter anticipated equal citizenship, universal suffrage and socio-economic justice. Sharpeville showed the need for rights and limits on state violence. Rivonia showed the danger of legal repression and the need for entrenched fair trial and political rights. The 1983 Constitution showed that limited racial inclusion is not democracy. The ANC Guidelines showed the move from liberation ideals to constitutional design.

In conclusion, early resistance shaped South Africa’s constitutional future by exposing the failures of apartheid legality and articulating a vision of democracy, equality, rights and social justice. The later constitutional order was therefore not only the product of elite negotiation in the 1990s. It was also the legal expression of decades of struggle against a system in which law was valid in form but unjust in substance.

Treating Rivonia as just a criminal trialToo thinExplain law vs justice and political legitimacy.

Theme 3 essay 3: Theme 3 Essay Bank 3

CODESA, Violence and the Two-Stage Transition: Legitimacy, Compromise and Constitutional Design

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

“Why was South Africa’s transition negotiated rather than purely revolutionary?”Compromise between majority rule and minority protections
“Critically discuss whether the negotiated settlement was democratic.”Unelected negotiations, but later elected CA + certification + public participation

2. Core Thesis

The transition from apartheid to constitutional democracy was negotiated because neither the apartheid government nor the liberation movement could unilaterally impose a stable constitutional future. CODESA exposed the central tensions of legitimacy, majority rule, minority protection and violence, while the two-stage transition resolved these tensions by allowing unelected negotiators to draft an Interim Constitution and democratically elected representatives to draft the Final Constitution under binding Constitutional Principles.

Use this as your opening paragraph for a 10- or 15-mark answer.

3. Key Authorities and Events to Remember

The slides frame CODESA around the legitimacy problem: negotiators had not been elected in free and verifiable elections, so they lacked the democratic mandate to write the final Constitution.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: negotiation as constitutional design

South Africa’s transition was not a simple revolution where one side defeated the other and imposed a new constitutional order. It was a negotiated constitutional revolution. This means the transition was radical in substance because it moved South Africa from parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion and authoritarian government to constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage and a justiciable Bill of Rights. But it was negotiated in form because the new order emerged through bargaining, compromise, interim arrangements and certification.

The central problem was legitimacy. The apartheid government still controlled the state, but lacked moral and democratic legitimacy. The liberation movements had democratic and moral legitimacy among oppressed communities, but had not yet won a formal national election. This meant the transition had to create a process that was stable enough to govern immediately, but democratic enough to produce a legitimate final Constitution.

B. CODESA I and the legitimacy problem

One of the major issues facing CODESA I was legitimacy. The negotiators had not been elected through free and verifiable elections, so they did not have the democratic mandate to write the final Constitution. The slides make this point directly: because the negotiators were unelected, a properly elected body was required for the drafting of any final Constitution.

This created a constitutional dilemma:

ProblemWhy it mattered
Unelected negotiatorsThey could negotiate a transition, but lacked democratic authority to impose a permanent Constitution.
Need for immediate stabilitySouth Africa needed a legal framework to govern the first democratic election and transition period.
Need for democratic legitimacyThe final Constitution had to be drafted by representatives elected by the people.
Need for legal continuityThe transition still had to use existing legal procedures to avoid state collapse.

Exam sentence

The legitimacy problem at CODESA was that unelected negotiators could design a pathway to democracy, but could not claim full democratic authority to write South Africa’s permanent constitutional settlement.

C. CODESA I: the central disagreement

CODESA I also exposed a major disagreement about how decisions would be made in the new constitutional order.

The apartheid government wanted:

power-sharing agreements;

strong minority protections;

supermajority requirements for constitutional decisions;

mechanisms that would give minority parties something close to veto power.

The ANC argued for:

  • majority rule based on democratic election;
  • a Constitution drafted by an elected body;
  • equal political participation for all citizens.

This was not merely a political disagreement. It was a constitutional disagreement about the meaning of democracy after apartheid.

NP position

The National Party feared that simple majority rule would allow the ANC to dominate the future constitutional order. It therefore wanted strong minority protections and high voting thresholds to protect white and minority interests.

ANC position

The ANC argued that a democratic South Africa had to be based on majority rule, universal suffrage and equal citizenship. From this perspective, giving the minority veto power would reproduce apartheid’s logic of minority control.

Exam sentence

The CODESA dispute was a clash between minority-security constitutionalism and majority-rule constitutionalism.

D. Why CODESA stalled

The disagreement over legitimacy and decision-making meant that CODESA could not easily produce a final settlement. The parties could not agree on the voting thresholds for constitutional decisions or the structure of transitional government.

The slides state that CODESA II, the second round of negotiations in 1992, failed because parties could not agree on voting thresholds for constitutional decisions and the structure of the transitional government, leading to a temporary collapse of negotiations in mid-1992.

Exam sentence

CODESA failed because it could not reconcile the NP’s demand for minority-protective power-sharing with the ANC’s demand for democratically legitimate majority rule.

E. Political violence and crisis during negotiations

The early 1990s were not a peaceful transition. Negotiations occurred alongside significant political violence. The slides mention township violence, political assassinations such as Chris Hani, conflict between political organisations such as the Inkatha Freedom Party, and security force involvement in destabilisation, including Boipatong.

This violence mattered constitutionally because it created mistrust between negotiating parties and made the transition more urgent. Violence threatened to destroy the possibility of a peaceful constitutional settlement.

Constitutional significance

Political violence showed that:

negotiations were fragile;

compromise was necessary to avoid civil conflict;

the new Constitution would need strong rights and accountability protections;

the transition needed a binding legal framework;

democratic legitimacy had to be achieved without allowing the process to collapse.

Exam sentence

Political violence did not merely form the background to negotiations; it shaped the urgency and content of constitutional compromise.

F. Boipatong Massacre: crisis and turning point

The Boipatong Massacre occurred on 17 June 1992. The slides state that more than 40 residents of Boipatong township were killed, that the attack was linked to hostel dwellers aligned with the Inkatha Freedom Party, and that allegations emerged that apartheid security forces had been involved. The massacre shocked the country and led the ANC to withdraw from negotiations.

The lecture gives an important interpretive point:

Boipatong did not end the possibility of negotiation — it clarified for both sides that the cost of failure was higher than the cost of compromise.

This is a beautiful exam sentence because it explains why violence both disrupted and intensified the need for negotiation.

Constitutional significance

Boipatong matters because it:

deepened mistrust;

weakened the NP’s legitimacy;

exposed the danger of uncontrolled state/security force power;

contributed to the collapse of negotiations;

made compromise more urgent;

showed why a rights-based, accountable constitutional order was necessary.

Exam sentence

Boipatong transformed the negotiations by showing that failure to compromise could lead to greater violence and instability than the compromises required to reach a constitutional settlement.

G. The two-stage transition solution

Formal title precision: the “Interim Constitution” is the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993. It was finalised through the MPNF and passed through the old legal order to preserve legal continuity while enabling a radical constitutional break.

The two-stage transition was the key compromise.

The negotiators devised a model with two stages:

StageWhat happenedWhy it mattered
Stage 1Unelected negotiating parties drafted and adopted the Interim Constitution. It governed the country after the first democratic election.Created immediate legal stability and enabled the first democratic election.
Stage 2A democratically elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution after the election.Solved the legitimacy problem by ensuring the final Constitution came from elected representatives.

The slides describe the two-stage transition as follows: Stage 1 involved unelected negotiating parties drafting and adopting the Interim Constitution (Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993), which governed the country after the first democratic election. Stage 2 required the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly as the Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution after the election, subject to the 34 Constitutional Principles and Constitutional Court certification.

Why the two-stage model was brilliant

It balanced competing needs:

NeedHow the two-stage model responded
StabilityThe Interim Constitution governed during the transition.
Democratic legitimacyThe Final Constitution was drafted by an elected Constitutional Assembly.
Minority protectionThe 34 Constitutional Principles bound the Final Constitution.
Majority ruleThe first democratic election determined representation in the Constitutional Assembly.
Legal certaintyThe Constitutional Court certified the Final Constitution against the Principles.

Exam sentence

The two-stage transition was the constitutional compromise that allowed unelected negotiators to create a temporary bridge to democracy while reserving final constitution-making authority for a democratically elected body.

H. MPNF: successor to CODESA

After CODESA stalled, the Multiparty Negotiating Forum became the successor process. The slides describe the MPNF as the successor to CODESA between 1993 and 1994. It included 26 political parties and groups, firmly established the two-stage transitional government, set the date for the first democratic election as 27 April 1994, and finalised the Interim Constitution, which was adopted on 18 November 1993.

The slides also highlight the negotiations of Roelf Meyer for the NP and Cyril Ramaphosa for the ANC as part of the successful finalisation of the Interim Constitution.

Constitutional significance

The MPNF matters because it moved the transition from failed talks to workable constitutional design. It did not produce the permanent Constitution, but it produced the legal structure that made the first democratic election and later Final Constitution possible.

Exam sentence

The MPNF transformed the failed CODESA process into a workable constitutional pathway by finalising the Interim Constitution and setting the election date that would legitimate the final constitution-making process.

I. Interim Constitution as negotiated bridge

The Interim Constitution was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election. It was adopted late in 1993 and became binding after 27 April 1994, the date of the first democratic election. It was adopted using the procedures of the 1983 Constitution.

This is constitutionally important because it shows both continuity and rupture:

ContinuityRupture
It was adopted through procedures of the existing legal order.It replaced parliamentary sovereignty with constitutional supremacy.
It was negotiated by unelected parties.It enabled the first universal democratic election.
It was interim, not final.It created binding Constitutional Principles for the Final Constitution.

The Interim Constitution was therefore a bridge between apartheid legality and democratic constitutionalism.

J. Constitutional Principles as negotiated safeguards

The 34 Constitutional Principles were crucial because they solved the fear that the elected majority might later impose a Constitution without respecting negotiated safeguards.

The slides define the Constitutional Principles as benchmarks that the Final Constitution had to meet. They would be used by the Constitutional Assembly when drafting the Final Constitution, and the newly established Constitutional Court would evaluate and certify the Final Constitution using those Principles. If the Final Constitution did not comply, the Court could not certify it and it would have to be redrafted.

Key principles included:

  • constitutional democracy based on constitutional supremacy and an independent judiciary;
  • universal adult suffrage and regular elections;
  • separation of powers and checks and balances;
  • a justiciable Bill of Rights;
  • one sovereign state structured at national, provincial and local levels;
  • proportional representation and common voters roll;
  • recognition of traditional leadership and customary law;
  • protection of the Constitution from amendment except through special processes.

Constitutional significance

The Principles were not ordinary political promises. They were legally binding standards for the Final Constitution.

Exam sentence

The Constitutional Principles were the legal mechanism that allowed the negotiated settlement to bind the later democratic constitution-making process without allowing unelected negotiators to write the permanent Constitution themselves.

K. Critical evaluation: was the negotiated settlement democratic?

A high-mark answer should evaluate the democratic tension.

Argument that it was not fully democratic

The early negotiation process was not fully democratic because CODESA and the MPNF were not elected through universal suffrage. The negotiators lacked the democratic mandate to write a permanent Constitution.

Argument that it became democratically legitimate

However, the two-stage transition responded to this problem. The Interim Constitution was temporary. The Final Constitution had to be drafted by a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly after the first democratic election. The Constitutional Principles ensured that the final text complied with the negotiated framework, while the certification process gave legal certainty.

Balanced conclusion

The negotiated settlement was therefore not pure direct democracy, but it was a democratically controlled transition. It used negotiation to avoid collapse, election to create legitimacy, and certification to ensure constitutional compliance.

Exam sentence

The two-stage transition converted an initially elite-negotiated process into a democratically authorised and legally controlled constitution-making process.

L. Conclusion

CODESA and the MPNF show why South Africa’s constitutional transition was both negotiated and revolutionary. CODESA failed because of disputes over legitimacy, minority protections, majority rule, voting thresholds and transitional government. Political violence, especially Boipatong, deepened mistrust but also made compromise urgent. The two-stage transition solved the core problem by allowing unelected negotiators to adopt an Interim Constitution while reserving final constitution-making for a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly. The 34 Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism ensured that the Final Constitution would remain faithful to the negotiated settlement. This is why South Africa’s transition can be described as a negotiated constitutional revolution.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the two-stage transition.”

“Why did CODESA fail?”

“What was the significance of the MPNF?”

Answer:

CODESA faced a legitimacy problem because the negotiators had not been elected in free and verifiable elections and therefore lacked the democratic mandate to draft a final Constitution. The ANC wanted majority rule and an elected constitution-making body, while the apartheid government wanted power-sharing, strong minority protections and supermajority requirements that would give minorities near-veto power.

The solution was a two-stage transition. In Stage 1, unelected negotiating parties drafted and adopted the Interim Constitution, which governed the transition. In Stage 2, a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution after the first democratic election. The MPNF later replaced the failed CODESA process, set the election date for 27 April 1994 and finalised the Interim Constitution, adopted on 18 November 1993.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Explain CODESA, the legitimacy problem and the two-stage transition.”

“Discuss why the two-stage transition was necessary.”

Answer:

South Africa’s transition was negotiated because neither the apartheid government nor the liberation movement could unilaterally impose a stable constitutional settlement. CODESA exposed the central legitimacy problem: the negotiators had not been elected through free and verifiable elections, so they lacked the democratic mandate to write the final Constitution.

There was also a major disagreement about the future constitutional order. The apartheid government wanted power-sharing, strong minority protections and supermajority requirements for constitutional decisions. This would give minority parties something close to veto power. The ANC argued for majority rule based on democratic election, a Constitution drafted by an elected body, and equal political participation for all citizens.

CODESA II failed in 1992 because the parties could not agree on voting thresholds for constitutional decisions or the structure of the transitional government. Negotiations also took place in a context of serious political violence, including township violence, political assassinations, conflict involving the Inkatha Freedom Party and allegations of security force destabilisation.

The two-stage transition solved these problems. Stage 1 allowed unelected negotiators to draft and adopt the Interim Constitution so that the country could be governed through the first democratic election. Stage 2 required a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution. The 34 Constitutional Principles then bound that process and the Constitutional Court certified compliance.

The MPNF was the successor to CODESA. It included 26 political parties and groups, set 27 April 1994 as the first democratic election date, and finalised the Interim Constitution adopted on 18 November 1993. Therefore, the negotiated transition balanced stability, legitimacy, majority rule, minority protection and constitutional supremacy.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss the negotiated transition from apartheid to constitutional democracy.”

“Explain the failure of CODESA and the significance of the two-stage transition.”

“How did the legitimacy problem shape South Africa’s constitutional settlement?”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: negotiated constitutional revolution
2Legitimacy problem at CODESA
3ANC vs NP constitutional disagreement
4CODESA II failure
5Political violence and mistrust
6Boipatong Massacre
7Two-stage transition solution
8MPNF and Interim Constitution
9Constitutional Principles
10Critical evaluation of democracy
11Conclusion

15-mark full answer

South Africa’s transition from apartheid to constitutional democracy was a negotiated constitutional revolution. It was revolutionary because it replaced parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion and authoritarian state power with constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage and a justiciable Bill of Rights. It was negotiated because the new order emerged through compromise rather than a unilateral victory by one side.

The first problem at CODESA was legitimacy. The negotiators had not been elected through free and verifiable elections, so they lacked the democratic mandate to write a final Constitution. The slides state that a properly elected body was required for the drafting of any final Constitution.

The second problem was disagreement about the structure of the future constitutional order. The apartheid government wanted power-sharing, strong minority protections and supermajority requirements for constitutional decisions. These would have given minority parties something close to veto power. The ANC argued for majority rule based on democratic elections, a Constitution drafted by an elected body, and equal political participation for all citizens.

CODESA II failed in 1992 because the parties could not agree on voting thresholds for constitutional decisions or the structure of transitional government. This led to a temporary collapse of negotiations in mid-1992.

The negotiation process also occurred in a context of serious political violence. The early 1990s were marked by township violence, political assassinations, conflict between political organisations such as the Inkatha Freedom Party, and allegations of security force involvement in destabilisation. This violence created deep mistrust between negotiating parties and made constitutional compromise urgent.

The Boipatong Massacre was especially important. On 17 June 1992, more than 40 residents of Boipatong township were killed. The attack was linked to hostel dwellers aligned with the Inkatha Freedom Party, and allegations emerged of apartheid security force involvement. The massacre led the ANC to withdraw from negotiations and weakened the NP’s remaining legitimacy.

However, Boipatong did not end the possibility of negotiation. Instead, it clarified that the cost of failure was higher than the cost of compromise. This is important because it shows how violence both destabilised and intensified the need for a negotiated settlement.

The solution was the two-stage transition. Stage 1 allowed unelected negotiating parties to draft and adopt the Interim Constitution, which would govern the country after the first democratic election. Stage 2 required a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution after the election. This balanced immediate stability with democratic legitimacy.

The MPNF became the successor to CODESA between 1993 and 1994. It included 26 political parties and groups, firmly established the two-stage transition, set the date for the first democratic election as 27 April 1994, and finalised the Interim Constitution, which was adopted on 18 November 1993.

The 34 Constitutional Principles were also essential. They acted as benchmarks that the Final Constitution had to meet. The Constitutional Assembly had to draft the Final Constitution in line with these Principles, and the Constitutional Court had to certify compliance. If the Final Constitution did not comply, it could not be certified and had to be redrafted.

Critically, the negotiated settlement was not pure democracy at the start, because unelected parties drafted the Interim Constitution. However, the process became democratically legitimate because the Final Constitution had to be drafted by an elected Constitutional Assembly after the first democratic election. The Constitutional Principles and certification process ensured that the elected majority remained bound by negotiated constitutional safeguards.

In conclusion, CODESA failed because legitimacy, majority rule, minority protections, voting thresholds, transitional government and violence could not be resolved at that stage. The two-stage transition solved these problems by using an Interim Constitution to create stability and an elected Constitutional Assembly to create democratic legitimacy. This is why South Africa’s transition is best described as a negotiated constitutional revolution.

C. The Interim Constitution as a “historic bridge”

Textual anchor: the bridge language appears in the Postamble, titled “National Unity and Reconciliation”. That Postamble also created the textual foundation for amnesty linked to political objectives during past conflict, which later connects to the TRC framework. Keep this as a supporting point; the core bridge idea remains Mureinik’s move from authority to justification.

The Interim Constitution’s postamble described it as a historic bridge between a deeply divided past and a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and development opportunities for all South Africans. The Theme 3 lecture quotes the bridge language and asks how the fundamental changes moved South Africa from a culture of authority to a culture of justification.

The correct legal reference is the postamble to the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993, titled National Unity and Reconciliation.

Mureinik’s bridge idea

Etienne Mureinik explains the bridge as a movement away from a culture of authority and towards a culture of justification. In the apartheid order, parliamentary sovereignty meant that what Parliament said was law without needing to justify itself to courts or to most of the people governed by those laws. Mureinik says this fostered an ethic of obedience, where Parliament commanded the bureaucracy and the bureaucracy commanded the people.

The bridge had to lead to a culture of justification: a culture in which every exercise of power is expected to be justified, and where government leadership rests on reasons rather than coercion. Mureinik also argues that the Bill of Rights is the “chief strut” of that bridge because it gives citizens values and standards through which they can demand justification from those who govern.

Exam sentence

Theme 3 essay 4: Theme 3 Essay Bank 4

The Interim Constitution as a Bridge: Government of National Unity, Bill of Rights, Constitutional Court and Constitutional Principles

FINAL CORRECTION PASS: This version applies the final Theme 3 accuracy fixes from the audit cycle. It preserves the essay-bank format while tightening legal precision, authority wording, dates, certification details and exam-risk phrasing.

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

2. Core Thesis

The Interim Constitution of 1993 was the legal bridge between apartheid and constitutional democracy: although it was negotiated by unelected parties and adopted through the old legal order, it radically transformed South Africa by enabling the first democratic election, replacing parliamentary sovereignty with constitutional supremacy, creating a justiciable Bill of Rights and Constitutional Court, establishing a Government of National Unity, and binding the elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution according to 34 Constitutional Principles.

Use this as your opening paragraph for a 10- or 15-mark answer.

The Theme 3 lecture says the Interim Constitution was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election, adopted late in 1993, became binding after 27 April 1994, and was adopted through the procedures of the 1983 Constitution.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: why the Interim Constitution matters

The Interim Constitution is one of the most important parts of South Africa’s constitutional transition because it made the movement from apartheid legality to constitutional democracy legally possible. It was “interim” because it was not intended to be the permanent Constitution. But it was also revolutionary because it created the legal framework for the first democratic election and introduced constitutional supremacy, rights, judicial review and the Constitutional Court.

The key point is that the Interim Constitution was both a compromise and a break. It was a compromise because it was negotiated by unelected parties and contained power-sharing arrangements such as the Government of National Unity. But it was a break because it replaced the old system of parliamentary sovereignty with a rights-based constitutional order.

B. Origins and process of the Interim Constitution

The Interim Constitution emerged from the failed CODESA process and the later successful Multiparty Negotiating Forum. Negotiations began during CODESA, but the Interim Constitution was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election. The MPNF adopted the Interim Constitution late in 1993, and it became binding after the first democratic election on 27 April 1994.

This creates an important accuracy point:

The Interim Constitution was negotiated before the first democratic election, but it governed the transition after that election.

It was also adopted through the procedures of the 1983 Constitution, which shows the strange but important nature of the transition: South Africa used the old legal order to create the legal foundation for a new constitutional order.

Exam sentence

The Interim Constitution is constitutionally significant because it used legal continuity to produce constitutional rupture.

This is a strong 90% phrase because it captures the paradox of the transition.

C. The Interim Constitution as a “historic bridge”

Textual anchor: the bridge language appears in the Postamble, titled “National Unity and Reconciliation”. That Postamble also created the textual foundation for amnesty linked to political objectives during past conflict, which later connects to the TRC framework. Keep this as a supporting point; the core bridge idea remains Mureinik’s move from authority to justification.

The Interim Constitution’s postamble described it as a historic bridge between a deeply divided past and a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and development opportunities for all South Africans. The Theme 3 lecture quotes the bridge language and asks how the fundamental changes moved South Africa from a culture of authority to a culture of justification.

The correct legal reference is the postamble to the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993, titled National Unity and Reconciliation.

Mureinik’s bridge idea

Etienne Mureinik explains the bridge as a movement away from a culture of authority and towards a culture of justification. In the apartheid order, parliamentary sovereignty meant that what Parliament said was law without needing to justify itself to courts or to most of the people governed by those laws. Mureinik says this fostered an ethic of obedience, where Parliament commanded the bureaucracy and the bureaucracy commanded the people.

The bridge had to lead to a culture of justification: a culture in which every exercise of power is expected to be justified, and where government leadership rests on reasons rather than coercion. Mureinik also argues that the Bill of Rights is the “chief strut” of that bridge because it gives citizens values and standards through which they can demand justification from those who govern.

Exam sentence

The Interim Constitution was a bridge because it moved South Africa from apartheid’s culture of authority, where law demanded obedience, to a culture of justification, where public power had to be justified through rights, reasons and constitutional standards.

D. Key feature 1: Government of National Unity

One of the key transitional compromises in the Interim Constitution was the Government of National Unity.

The Theme 3 lecture says the Interim Constitution established an interim Government of National Unity, meaning both the ANC and NP would be represented in the executive. It also provided for a coalition government among major political parties for a minimum of five years, up to 1999, and provided for two Deputy Presidents, one from the ANC and one from the NP.

Why the GNU mattered

The GNU was constitutionally significant because it:

created political stability during the transition;

reassured minority parties that they would not be immediately excluded;

allowed the first democratic government to govern inclusively;

reduced the risk of violent collapse;

reflected the negotiated nature of the transition.

But do not overstate it

The GNU was not the same as full constitutional democracy. It was a transitional power-sharing mechanism. Its purpose was to stabilise the shift to democracy, not to replace majority rule permanently.

Exam sentence

The Government of National Unity shows that the Interim Constitution balanced democratic transition with political reassurance: it enabled majority rule to begin without immediately excluding former ruling groups from executive power.

E. Key feature 2: Justiciable Bill of Rights

The Interim Constitution contained a Bill of Rights protecting basic human rights. This was a radical change from the old parliamentary sovereignty model, where courts could not strike down Acts of Parliament merely because they violated fundamental rights.

The Bill of Rights was central to the bridge because it made rights legally enforceable. It allowed people to challenge public power through constitutional standards.

Mureinik’s point is vital here: the Bill of Rights was the “chief strut” of the bridge to a culture of justification because it empowered citizens affected by laws or decisions to demand justification.

Constitutional significance

The Bill of Rights mattered because it:

  • entrenched basic rights;
  • created rights-based limits on government;
  • supported judicial review;
  • moved South Africa away from obedience to authority;
  • gave legal content to democracy and equality.

Exam sentence

The justiciable Bill of Rights was revolutionary because it turned rights from political ideals into enforceable constitutional standards.

F. Key feature 3: New Constitutional Court

The Interim Constitution required the Final Constitution to be certified by a newly created Constitutional Court and contained 34 Constitutional Principles regulating the adoption of the Final Constitution.

The lecture explains that because the apartheid judiciary was thoroughly discredited, the Interim Constitution created an entirely new Constitutional Court. In terms of section 71(3) of Act 200 of 1993, the Constitutional Court’s certification decision would be final and binding, so no other court could pronounce on the validity of the constitutional text once certified.

Why this mattered

The Constitutional Court mattered because it:

symbolised a break from the apartheid judiciary;

became guardian of the negotiated constitutional settlement;

ensured the elected Constitutional Assembly complied with the 34 Principles;

strengthened constitutional supremacy and judicial review;

created legal certainty in the transition.

Appointment nuance

The lecture says the democratically elected President appointed the first head of the Court in consultation with the current Chief Justice; the President appointed four more judges in consultation with Cabinet and the Chief Justice; and the remaining six judges were appointed from a list forwarded by the Judicial Service Commission.

This is useful detail for a high-mark answer, but only include it if the question specifically asks about the Constitutional Court or certification.

Exam sentence

The new Constitutional Court gave legal force to the negotiated transition by ensuring that the Final Constitution would not merely be politically agreed, but legally compliant with the Constitutional Principles.

G. Key feature 4: Two-stage transition

The Interim Constitution expressly provided for the two-stage transition. The Theme 3 lecture says Stage 1 involved unelected negotiating parties drafting and adopting the Interim Constitution, which would govern the country after the first democratic election. Stage 2 required a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution after the election.

This solved the legitimacy problem from CODESA. Unelected negotiators could not legitimately impose the permanent Constitution, but they could create a temporary framework to enable democratic election and final constitution-making.

Exam sentence

The two-stage transition allowed unelected negotiators to create the bridge, but required elected representatives to build the permanent constitutional order.

H. Key feature 5: Rules for drafting the Final Constitution

Section 73 precision: if the Constitutional Assembly failed to reach the required two-thirds majority but the draft still had majority support, the deadlock process could involve a panel of constitutional experts and, if necessary, a referendum route. The referendum fallback required at least 60% support from voters. This mechanism was not ultimately used.

The Interim Constitution set out the rules and process for drafting the Final Constitution.

The key rules were:

RuleExplanation
Section 68(1)The National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly.
Two-year deadlineThe Constitutional Assembly had to adopt the constitutional text within two years of the first sitting of the National Assembly.
Two-thirds majorityAdoption required support of two-thirds of all Constitutional Assembly members.
Section 71(2)The new text had no force unless the Constitutional Court certified that it complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles.
Referendum fallbackIf a draft failed to get two-thirds but gained majority support, it could become the Constitution if backed by at least 60% of voters in a referendum; this option was not used.

These rules come directly from the Theme 3 lecture’s “Rules for Drafting the Final Constitution” slide.

Accuracy note

Some lecture slides write “National Assembly and Senate (NCOP)”, but the legally precise term for the Interim Constitution process is Senate, not NCOP. The NCOP was created by the 1996 Constitution. For Theme 3, write:

National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly as the Constitutional Assembly.

Exam sentence

Sections 68 and 71 of the Interim Constitution converted political compromise into a legally structured constitution-making process.

I. Key feature 6: The 34 Constitutional Principles

The 34 Constitutional Principles were benchmarks that the Final Constitution had to meet. The Constitutional Assembly used them when drafting the Final Constitution, and the Constitutional Court used them to certify the text. If the Final Constitution did not comply, the Court could not certify it and the text had to be redrafted.

The principles included:

one sovereign state;

common South African citizenship;

democratic government;

equality between men and women and people of all races;

entrenched and justiciable fundamental rights;

prohibition of discrimination and promotion of equality and national unity;

constitutional supremacy;

equality before the law and equitable legal process;

separation of powers with checks and balances;

independent and impartial judiciary;

multi-party democracy;

regular elections;

universal adult suffrage;

common voters’ roll;

proportional representation;

acknowledgement and protection of language and culture;

recognition of traditional leadership and customary law;

special procedures for constitutional amendments;

national, provincial and local levels of government.

Constitutional significance

The Constitutional Principles are extremely important because they were the legal safeguards of the negotiated settlement. They reassured minority parties that the elected majority could not simply write any Constitution it wanted, while still allowing the final text to be drafted by a democratic Constitutional Assembly.

Exam sentence

The Constitutional Principles were the negotiated bridge between political compromise and legal constitutionalism: they limited the elected Constitutional Assembly, but did so according to agreed democratic and rights-based standards.

J. The Interim Constitution as revolutionary

The Interim Constitution brought fundamental changes. The Theme 3 lecture lists the main changes as:

  1. shift from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy;
  2. creation of a new independent Constitutional Court;
  3. justiciable Bill of Rights;
  4. two-stage transition model;
  5. coalition Government of National Unity;
  6. certification mechanism;
  7. shift to multi-party democracy;
  8. separation of powers.

These features show why the Interim Constitution can be called revolutionary even though it was negotiated.

Why it was revolutionary

It changed the basic logic of public power:

Old orderInterim Constitution
Parliamentary sovereigntyConstitutional supremacy
Rights dependent on ordinary politicsJusticiable Bill of Rights
Apartheid judiciary discreditedNew Constitutional Court
Racial exclusionFirst universal democratic election
Unchecked state powerRights, separation of powers, certification
Majority excluded from Constitution-makingElected Constitutional Assembly for Final Constitution

Exam sentence

The Interim Constitution was revolutionary because it replaced the legal grammar of apartheid with the legal grammar of constitutional democracy.

K. Critical evaluation: why the Interim Constitution was both democratic and undemocratic

A 90% answer should not oversimplify the Interim Constitution.

Why it was not fully democratic at origin

It was negotiated by unelected parties before the first democratic election. This means it lacked the full democratic legitimacy that the Final Constitution later gained through the elected Constitutional Assembly.

Why it was democratically necessary

However, the Interim Constitution enabled the first democratic election, created the Constitutional Assembly and set legally binding rules for the Final Constitution. It was therefore not the permanent democratic settlement, but the legal mechanism that made democratic constitution-making possible.

Balanced exam sentence

The Interim Constitution was democratically imperfect in its origin but democratically essential in its function.

That sentence is very useful.

L. Conclusion

The Interim Constitution was the bridge between apartheid legality and constitutional democracy. It was negotiated before the first democratic election and adopted through the old legal order, but it created a radically new constitutional framework. It established the Government of National Unity, entrenched a justiciable Bill of Rights, created a new Constitutional Court, provided for a two-stage transition and bound the elected Constitutional Assembly through 34 Constitutional Principles and certification. Its significance lies in the fact that it balanced political compromise, legal continuity, democratic legitimacy and transformative constitutional change.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain why the Interim Constitution was important.”

“What does it mean to say the Interim Constitution was a bridge?”

“List key features of the Interim Constitution.”

Answer:

The Interim Constitution was important because it created the legal bridge from apartheid to constitutional democracy. It was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election, adopted late in 1993, and became binding after 27 April 1994.

Its key features were the Government of National Unity, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a newly created Constitutional Court, a two-stage transition and 34 Constitutional Principles. The Government of National Unity created stability through power-sharing, while the Bill of Rights made basic rights enforceable. The Constitutional Court certified whether the Final Constitution complied with the 34 Principles. The Interim Constitution was therefore a temporary but revolutionary framework: it enabled democratic election and controlled the drafting of the Final Constitution.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Discuss the significance of the Interim Constitution.”

“Explain the Interim Constitution as a bridge.”

Answer:

The Interim Constitution was the legal bridge between apartheid and constitutional democracy. It was negotiated by the MPNF before the first democratic election, adopted late in 1993 and became binding after 27 April 1994. It was adopted through the procedures of the 1983 Constitution, which shows that South Africa used legal continuity to create constitutional rupture.

It was a bridge because it moved South Africa from parliamentary sovereignty and apartheid legality towards constitutional supremacy, rights and democracy. Mureinik explains this as a move from a culture of authority to a culture of justification. Under apartheid, Parliament’s word was law without needing to justify itself to those governed by it. Under the new order, power had to be justified through rights and reasons. The Bill of Rights was the chief strut of this bridge.

The Interim Constitution also established a Government of National Unity. The ANC and NP were represented in the executive, major political parties were included in a coalition government, and there were two Deputy Presidents, one from the ANC and one from the NP. This created stability and minority reassurance during the transition.

The Interim Constitution contained a justiciable Bill of Rights and created a new Constitutional Court because the apartheid judiciary was discredited. It also set the rules for drafting the Final Constitution. Under s 68(1), the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly. Under s 71(2), the new text had no force unless certified by the Constitutional Court as complying with the 34 Constitutional Principles.

Therefore, the Interim Constitution was temporary but foundational. It enabled the first democratic election, structured the transition and ensured that the Final Constitution would be democratically drafted and legally controlled.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss the Interim Constitution as a bridge between apartheid and constitutional democracy.”

“Explain the role and significance of the Interim Constitution in South Africa’s transition.”

“Discuss the key features of the Interim Constitution and their constitutional importance.”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: IC as bridge
2Origins and process

4Government of National Unity

6Constitutional Court
7Two-stage transition
