9Constitutional Principles
10Revolutionary changes
11Critical evaluation
12Conclusion

15-mark full answer

The Interim Constitution of 1993 was the legal bridge between apartheid and constitutional democracy. It was not intended to be South Africa’s permanent Constitution, but it made the transition legally possible. It was negotiated by unelected parties and adopted through the old legal order, yet it radically changed South Africa by enabling the first democratic election, introducing constitutional supremacy, entrenching a justiciable Bill of Rights, creating a new Constitutional Court and regulating the drafting of the Final Constitution.

The Interim Constitution emerged from the MPNF after the failure of CODESA. Negotiations began during CODESA, but the Interim Constitution was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election. It was adopted late in 1993 and became binding after 27 April 1994. It was adopted through the procedures of the 1983 Constitution. This shows the paradox of the transition: South Africa used the old legal order to create a new constitutional order.

The bridge idea comes from the Interim Constitution’s postamble, which described the Constitution as a historic bridge between a deeply divided past and a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and development opportunities for all. The correct legal reference is the postamble to the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993, titled National Unity and Reconciliation.

Mureinik explains this bridge as a movement from a culture of authority to a culture of justification. Under apartheid parliamentary sovereignty, what Parliament said was law without needing to justify itself to courts or to most of the governed population. This produced obedience rather than accountability. Mureinik argues that the new Constitution had to create a culture in which every exercise of power must be justified, and that the Bill of Rights was the chief strut of that bridge.

One key feature of the Interim Constitution was the Government of National Unity. The Interim Constitution established an interim government in which both the ANC and NP were represented in the executive. It provided for a coalition government among major political parties for at least five years and for two Deputy Presidents, one from the ANC and one from the NP. This was a transitional compromise designed to create stability and reassure minority parties during the move to democracy.

A second key feature was the justiciable Bill of Rights. This was revolutionary because rights were no longer merely political ideals or ordinary legislative choices. They became enforceable constitutional standards. This directly broke from parliamentary sovereignty, under which courts could not invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive rights violations. The Bill of Rights therefore enabled people to demand justification from public power.

A third key feature was the creation of a new Constitutional Court. The lecture explains that because the apartheid judiciary was thoroughly discredited, the Interim Constitution created an entirely new Constitutional Court. Its certification decision would be final and binding, and no other court could pronounce on the validity of the constitutional text. This was important because the Court became guardian of the negotiated settlement and legal supervisor of the Final Constitution’s compliance with the 34 Principles.

The Interim Constitution also expressly provided for the two-stage transition. Stage 1 involved unelected negotiating parties drafting and adopting the Interim Constitution, which governed the country after the first democratic election. Stage 2 required a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution after the election. This solved the legitimacy problem: unelected negotiators could create the temporary framework, but elected representatives had to draft the permanent Constitution.

The Interim Constitution set out the rules for drafting the Final Constitution. Under s 68(1), the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly. The Assembly had to adopt the text within two years of the first sitting of the National Assembly. Adoption required support of two-thirds of all Constitutional Assembly members. Under s 71(2), the new text had no force unless the Constitutional Court certified that it complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles.

The 34 Constitutional Principles were benchmarks that the Final Constitution had to meet. They included one sovereign state, common citizenship, democratic government, equality between men and women and people of all races, entrenched and justiciable rights, constitutional supremacy, equality before the law, separation of powers, an independent judiciary, multi-party democracy, universal adult suffrage, a common voters’ roll, proportional representation, cultural and language protection, recognition of traditional leadership, special amendment procedures and multi-level government.

The Interim Constitution was therefore revolutionary in substance. The Theme 3 lecture lists the fundamental changes it brought about: the shift from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy, creation of a new independent Constitutional Court, justiciable Bill of Rights, two-stage transition, Government of National Unity, certification mechanism, multi-party democracy and separation of powers.

Critically, the Interim Constitution was not fully democratic in its origin because it was negotiated by unelected parties before the first democratic election. However, it was democratically essential in its function because it enabled the first democratic election and required the Final Constitution to be drafted by an elected Constitutional Assembly. It was therefore democratically imperfect at the beginning, but it created the conditions for democratic constitution-making.

In conclusion, the Interim Constitution was the bridge between apartheid and constitutional democracy. It combined legal continuity with radical constitutional change. Through the Government of National Unity, Bill of Rights, Constitutional Court, two-stage transition and Constitutional Principles, it balanced compromise, stability, legitimacy and transformation. Its significance lies in the fact that it made South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution legally possible.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the Constitutional Principles.”

“What was the certification mechanism?”

“Why did the Final Constitution have to be certified?”

Answer:

The Constitutional Principles were 34 binding benchmarks in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution. They controlled the drafting of the Final Constitution and included principles such as constitutional supremacy, an independent judiciary, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, separation of powers, a justiciable Bill of Rights, proportional representation and protection of the Constitution through special amendment procedures.

The certification mechanism required the Constitutional Court to certify that the Final Constitution complied with those Principles. Under s 71(2) of the Interim Constitution, the new constitutional text had no force or effect unless the Constitutional Court certified compliance. The Court’s certification decision was final and binding under s 71(3). Therefore, the Principles ensured that the Final Constitution was democratically drafted but legally controlled.

11. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton

Constitutional Principles = 34 binding benchmarks in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution; a solemn pact limiting the Final Constitution.

Purpose = control Final Constitution.

Why needed = legitimacy + minority safeguards.

s 68 = NA + Senate = Constitutional Assembly.

s 71(1) = text must comply with Principles.

s 71(3) = certification final and binding.

s 73 = two-year deadline + two-thirds majority + referendum fallback.

Court’s role = legal, not political.

Solemn pact = negotiated parties bound the final process.

First Certification = first text not certified.

Second Certification = Amended Text certified on 4 Dec 1996.

Final Constitution = democratic drafting + legal certification.

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

A major 90% point is that the Constitutional Court’s certification role was legal, not political.

Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Court said it had a judicial, not political mandate. The wisdom or correctness of the New Text was left to the Constitutional Assembly. The Court had no power and no right to express a view on the political choices made by the Constitutional Assembly. Its role was only to decide whether the text complied with the Constitutional Principles.

This prevents a dangerous mistake: do not say the Court rejected the first text because it disliked the political choices. It rejected the text only where it found non-compliance with the Principles.

Exam sentence

The Court’s role was not to choose the best Constitution, but to decide whether the Constitution chosen by the elected Assembly complied with the agreed Principles.

I. Holistic interpretation, not technical rigidity

The Theme 3 lecture says that in the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Court determined that the Constitutional Principles had to be interpreted holistically and not in a technically rigid way, to ensure citizens could exercise their fundamental rights and freedoms.

This matters because the Principles were broad and sometimes open-textured. The Court had to interpret them carefully, without turning certification into a mechanical technical exercise.

Exam sentence

Theme 3 essay 5: Theme 3 Essay Bank 5

Constitutional Principles and the Certification Mechanism: The Solemn Pact Behind the Final Constitution

2. Core Thesis

The 34 Constitutional Principles were binding benchmarks negotiated into the Interim Constitution to control the drafting of the Final Constitution. They formed a solemn pact between the negotiating parties: the elected Constitutional Assembly had democratic authority to draft the Final Constitution, but the Constitutional Court had the legal duty to certify that the text complied with the agreed principles before it could have force and effect.

Use this as the opening paragraph for a longer answer.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

Theme 3 lecturesCPs as benchmarks for Final Constitution

The Theme 3 lecture defines the Constitutional Principles as benchmarks that the Final Constitution had to meet. The Constitutional Assembly had to draft with them in mind, and the Constitutional Court had to certify compliance; if the text did not comply, it could not be certified and had to be redrafted.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: why the Constitutional Principles mattered

The Constitutional Principles were central to South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. They were the mechanism that allowed South Africa to solve a difficult problem: unelected negotiators could not legitimately impose a permanent Constitution, but minority parties and former ruling groups also wanted guarantees that an elected majority would not write any Constitution it wanted.

The solution was to let the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly draft the Final Constitution, but to bind it to 34 agreed Constitutional Principles contained in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution. The Constitutional Court would then certify whether the Final Constitution complied with those Principles.

The Constitutional Principles therefore sat at the heart of the balance between democracy, compromise, legality and constitutional supremacy.

B. What were the Constitutional Principles?

The Constitutional Principles were binding legal standards that the Final Constitution had to satisfy. They were not merely political promises or moral ideals. They were placed in the Interim Constitution and used as the legal yardstick for certification.

The Theme 3 lecture says the Principles were benchmarks that would shape the nature of the new Constitution. Key examples included:

  1. constitutional democracy based on the supremacy of the Constitution and protected by an independent judiciary;
  2. universal adult suffrage and regular elections;
  3. separation of powers with checks and balances;
  4. a justiciable Bill of Rights protecting fundamental rights and civil liberties;
  5. one sovereign state structured at national, provincial and local levels;
  6. proportional representation and a common voters’ roll;
  7. recognition of traditional leadership and customary law;
  8. protection of the Constitution from amendment except through special processes.

Exam sentence

The Constitutional Principles were the negotiated constitutional DNA of the Final Constitution: they did not write the final text, but they determined the minimum features it had to contain.

C. Why were the Principles needed?

The Principles were needed because the transition had to solve two problems at the same time.

1. The legitimacy problem

The Interim Constitution was negotiated before the first democratic election by unelected parties. Those negotiators could create a temporary framework, but they could not claim full democratic authority to write South Africa’s permanent Constitution.

2. The fear of majoritarian domination

After the first election, the ANC was expected to be the dominant political force. Minority parties feared that an elected majority could draft a Constitution that ignored negotiated safeguards, minority interests, provincial powers, language/cultural concerns, independent institutions or rights protections.

The Constitutional Principles solved this by allowing:

democratic drafting by the elected Constitutional Assembly; but

legal control through agreed principles and court certification.

Exam sentence

The Principles solved the transition’s legitimacy dilemma by combining elected constitution-making with negotiated constitutional safeguards.

D. The Constitutional Assembly and democratic drafting

The Final Constitution was not drafted by CODESA or the MPNF. It was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, formed after the first democratic election.

Under s 68(1) of the Interim Constitution, the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly. The Assembly had to draft and adopt the new constitutional text according to the Interim Constitution’s rules.

This detail matters because some students incorrectly say the NCOP formed part of the Constitutional Assembly. That is inaccurate. The NCOP belongs to the 1996 Constitution. Under the Interim Constitution process, the correct term is Senate.

Exam sentence

The Constitutional Assembly gave the Final Constitution democratic legitimacy because it was made up of representatives chosen in South Africa’s first democratic election.

E. Section 71: certification as a legal gatekeeper

Statutory map: s 68(1) created the Constitutional Assembly as the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly; s 71(1) required the new text to comply with the 34 Constitutional Principles; s 71(2) stated that the text had no force or effect unless certified; and s 71(3) made certification final and binding. Section 73 then regulated adoption, including the two-year deadline, two-thirds majority and fallback process.

Section 71 of the Interim Constitution is the core provision.

Section 71(1) required the new constitutional text to comply with the Constitutional Principles in Schedule 4 and to be passed in accordance with the Interim Constitution.

Section 71(2) provided that the new constitutional text, or any provision of it, would have no force or effect unless the Constitutional Court certified that all provisions complied with the Constitutional Principles.

Section 71(3) made the Court’s certification decision final and binding, and no court could later enquire into or pronounce upon the validity of the text or any provision once certified.

Exam sentence

Certification was the legal gatekeeper: without Constitutional Court certification, the Final Constitution had no force and effect.

F. Section 73: adoption rules and fallback mechanisms

Section 73 of the Interim Constitution regulated adoption of the new constitutional text.

Important points:

RuleExplanation
Two-year deadlineThe Constitutional Assembly had to pass the new text within two years of the first sitting of the National Assembly.
Two-thirds majorityAdoption required support of at least two-thirds of all members of the Constitutional Assembly.
Provincial provisionsText relating to provincial boundaries, powers and functions also required two-thirds approval of all Senate members.
Expert panel fallbackIf the text failed to get two-thirds but had majority support, it could be referred to a panel of constitutional experts for advice.
Referendum fallbackA text supported by majority but not two-thirds could, after certification, be put to a national referendum and become the Constitution if approved by at least 60% of votes cast.
This option was not usedThe Final Constitution was ultimately adopted through the Constitutional Assembly route.

The Interim Constitution’s text confirms the two-year deadline, two-thirds requirement, and referendum route requiring at least 60% of votes cast if the referendum process was triggered.

Exam sentence

Section 73 shows that the Final Constitution had to be democratically adopted, but within a carefully structured and legally controlled process.

G. Certification and the “solemn pact”

Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Constitutional Court treated certification as a legal duty arising from the Interim Constitution. The Court emphasised that the Interim Constitution created a “solemn pact” between the negotiating parties to create a Final Constitution that adhered to the specified Constitutional Principles.

This phrase is extremely useful in an exam.

It means the Principles were not optional. They were the binding terms of the negotiated transition. Even if the Constitutional Assembly had strong democratic support, the Court had to send the text back if it did not comply.

Exam sentence

The Constitutional Principles were a solemn pact: they bound the democratic constitution-making process because they were the legal price of the negotiated transition.

H. The Constitutional Court’s role: legal, not political

A major 90% point is that the Constitutional Court’s certification role was legal, not political.

Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Court said it had a judicial, not political mandate. The wisdom or correctness of the New Text was left to the Constitutional Assembly. The Court had no power and no right to express a view on the political choices made by the Constitutional Assembly. Its role was only to decide whether the text complied with the Constitutional Principles.

This prevents a dangerous mistake: do not say the Court rejected the first text because it disliked the political choices. It rejected the text only where it found non-compliance with the Principles.

Exam sentence

The Court’s role was not to choose the best Constitution, but to decide whether the Constitution chosen by the elected Assembly complied with the agreed Principles.

I. Holistic interpretation, not technical rigidity

The Theme 3 lecture says that in the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Court determined that the Constitutional Principles had to be interpreted holistically and not in a technically rigid way, to ensure citizens could exercise their fundamental rights and freedoms.

This matters because the Principles were broad and sometimes open-textured. The Court had to interpret them carefully, without turning certification into a mechanical technical exercise.

Exam sentence

Certification required careful constitutional interpretation: the Principles had to be applied legally, but holistically and without technical rigidity.

J. First Certification Judgment: the first text was not certified

The Constitutional Assembly adopted a first draft of the Final Constitution and submitted it to the Constitutional Court. The Theme 3 slides record that the first draft was submitted on 10 May 1996 and was not certified because the Court found several provisions did not align with the Constitutional Principles.

The First Certification Judgment, formally Ex parte Chairperson of the Constitutional Assembly: In re Certification of the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC), is the key case.

The lecture identifies three major objection categories:

inclusion of socio-economic rights in a justiciable Bill of Rights;

lack of complete separation between executive and legislature;

other instances of non-compliance with Constitutional Principles.

The Court rejected some objections but upheld others. This is important: the first draft did not fail completely. The Court was careful to say that most of the text complied, but certain provisions did not.

Woolman and Swanepoel emphasise how extraordinary this was: a judicial body declared a constitution “unconstitutional” even though the text had been adopted by 86% of the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly.

Exam sentence

The First Certification Judgment was extraordinary because the Constitutional Court refused to certify a constitutional text adopted by an overwhelming democratic majority, not for political reasons, but because parts of it failed to comply with the solemn pact contained in the Constitutional Principles.

K. Why this did not undermine democracy

At first, certification may look counter-majoritarian: unelected judges could prevent a Constitution adopted by elected representatives from coming into force.

But the better answer is that certification protected the negotiated democratic process because the elected Constitutional Assembly was never free to ignore the Principles. Those Principles were part of the Interim Constitution and were accepted as the legal framework for drafting the Final Constitution.

Certification therefore:

gave legal certainty;

protected negotiated safeguards;

prevented opportunistic constitution-making;

ensured compliance with supremacy, rights, democracy and separation of powers;

strengthened public confidence in the Final Constitution.

Exam sentence

Certification did not replace democracy; it enforced the legal conditions under which democratic constitution-making had been authorised.

L. Second Certification Judgment: final legal hurdle

After the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Assembly amended the text. The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles. The lecture describes this as the final legal hurdle for South Africa’s transition to full democracy.

Following certification, President Nelson Mandela signed the Constitution into law on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville, and the Constitution took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

Exam sentence

The Second Certification Judgment completed the legal transition from negotiated interim constitutionalism to the Final Constitution.

M. Relationship between the Principles and the Final Constitution’s content

The Constitutional Principles explain why the Final Constitution contains many of its major features.

Constitutional Principle themeFinal Constitution feature
Constitutional supremacys 2 and founding value in s 1(c)
Justiciable Bill of RightsChapter 2
Universal suffrage and regular electionsPolitical rights and democratic system
Separation of powersLegislative, executive and judicial authority
Independent judiciaryChapter 8
Multi-sphere governmentNational, provincial and local government
Traditional leadership and customary lawRecognition in the constitutional framework
Special amendment proceduress 74 amendment process
Language and cultureOfficial languages and cultural rights

Exam sentence

The Final Constitution’s major doctrines were not accidental; they were the legal product of the Constitutional Principles and the certification process.

N. Critical evaluation: legal certainty vs democratic tension

A strong answer should evaluate the certification mechanism.

Strengths

The certification mechanism:

protected the negotiated settlement;

ensured the Final Constitution complied with basic democratic and rights-based commitments;

reassured minority parties;

prevented one party from dominating the constitutional text;

gave the Constitution legal legitimacy;

strengthened constitutional supremacy.

Weakness / tension

It also created a democratic tension because an unelected Court reviewed a constitutional text adopted by an elected Constitutional Assembly. That tension is real.

Balanced answer

However, the Court’s mandate was legal, not political. It did not choose the best Constitution. It only tested compliance with the agreed Principles. This makes the certification mechanism unusual but constitutionally defensible.

Exam sentence

The certification mechanism was counter-majoritarian in form but democratic-constitutional in function because it enforced the agreed limits that made democratic constitution-making possible.

O. Conclusion

The Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism were central to South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. They allowed unelected negotiators to create binding safeguards without writing the permanent Constitution themselves. They allowed the elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution, but only within the framework of agreed principles. Section 71 made certification a legal precondition for the Final Constitution to have force, while section 73 regulated democratic adoption. The Constitutional Court’s role was legal, not political: it enforced the solemn pact between the negotiating parties. This ensured that the Final Constitution was both democratically drafted and legally legitimate.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the Constitutional Principles.”

“What was the certification mechanism?”

“Why did the Final Constitution have to be certified?”

Answer:

The Constitutional Principles were 34 binding benchmarks in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution. They controlled the drafting of the Final Constitution and included principles such as constitutional supremacy, an independent judiciary, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, separation of powers, a justiciable Bill of Rights, proportional representation and protection of the Constitution through special amendment procedures.

The certification mechanism required the Constitutional Court to certify that the Final Constitution complied with those Principles. Under s 71(2) of the Interim Constitution, the new constitutional text had no force or effect unless the Constitutional Court certified compliance. The Court’s certification decision was final and binding under s 71(3). Therefore, the Principles ensured that the Final Constitution was democratically drafted but legally controlled.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Explain the Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism.”

“How did the Constitutional Principles balance democracy and compromise?”

Answer:

The 34 Constitutional Principles were binding benchmarks contained in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution. They were created as part of the negotiated transition to control the drafting of the Final Constitution. Their purpose was to ensure that the elected Constitutional Assembly could draft the permanent Constitution, but only within agreed constitutional safeguards.

The Principles included constitutional democracy based on constitutional supremacy and an independent judiciary, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, separation of powers with checks and balances, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a sovereign state structured at national, provincial and local levels, proportional representation, a common voters’ roll, recognition of traditional leadership and protection of the Constitution from amendment except through special processes.

The certification mechanism gave these Principles legal force. Under s 71(1) of the Interim Constitution, the new constitutional text had to comply with the Constitutional Principles. Under s 71(2), it had no force or effect unless certified by the Constitutional Court. Under s 71(3), certification was final and binding.

This mechanism balanced democracy and compromise. The Final Constitution was drafted by the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly, giving it democratic legitimacy. But the Assembly was bound by the Principles, which protected the negotiated settlement and reassured parties that the elected majority could not ignore agreed constitutional fundamentals.

The Constitutional Court’s role was legal, not political. Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Court said it had a judicial mandate, not a political one, and that it could not express a view on the wisdom of the Constitutional Assembly’s political choices. Its role was only to test compliance with the Principles.

Therefore, the Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism made the Final Constitution both democratically authorised and legally legitimate.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss the Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism.”

“Explain how the Constitutional Principles controlled the drafting of the Final Constitution.”

“Was certification democratic? Discuss.”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: Principles as solemn pact
2What Principles were
3Why they were needed
4Constitutional Assembly and s 68
5s 71 certification
6s 73 adoption rules
7Court’s legal, not political role
8First Certification Judgment
9Second Certification Judgment
10Relationship to Final Constitution content
11Critical evaluation
12Conclusion

15-mark full answer

The 34 Constitutional Principles were central to South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. They were binding benchmarks contained in Schedule 4 of the Interim Constitution and controlled the drafting of the Final Constitution. Their purpose was to ensure that the Final Constitution would be democratically drafted by an elected Constitutional Assembly, but would still comply with the agreed fundamentals of the negotiated settlement.

The Principles included constitutional democracy based on constitutional supremacy and an independent judiciary, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, separation of powers, checks and balances, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a sovereign state structured at national, provincial and local levels, proportional representation, a common voters’ roll, traditional leadership and protection of the Constitution from amendment except through special processes.

The Principles were needed because of the transition’s legitimacy problem. Unelected negotiators could not write the permanent Constitution themselves. At the same time, minority parties feared that an elected majority might ignore negotiated safeguards. The solution was to allow an elected Constitutional Assembly to draft the Final Constitution, but to bind it to the 34 Principles.

The Constitutional Assembly was created under the Interim Constitution. Under s 68(1), the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly. This is important because the Final Constitution was not drafted by CODESA or the MPNF. It was drafted by elected representatives after the first democratic election.

Section 71 created the certification mechanism. Section 71(1) required the new constitutional text to comply with the Constitutional Principles and to be passed in accordance with the Interim Constitution. Section 71(2) provided that the new text had no force or effect unless the Constitutional Court certified compliance. Section 71(3) made the certification decision final and binding.

Section 73 regulated adoption of the new text. The Constitutional Assembly had to pass the text within two years of the first sitting of the National Assembly. Adoption required a majority of at least two-thirds of all Constitutional Assembly members, and provisions relating to provincial boundaries, powers and functions also required two-thirds approval of all Senate members. If the two-thirds threshold was not met, the Interim Constitution contained fallback mechanisms, including possible expert-panel advice and a referendum route requiring at least 60% of votes cast.

The Constitutional Court’s certification role was legal, not political. Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Court said it had a judicial mandate, not a political mandate. It had no power or right to comment on the wisdom of the Constitutional Assembly’s political choices. Its role was not to select the best Constitution, but to determine whether the chosen text complied with the Principles.

The Court treated the Principles as a solemn pact between the negotiating parties. This meant that even overwhelming democratic support in the Constitutional Assembly could not cure non-compliance with the agreed Principles. The Court therefore had to enforce the pact by refusing certification where necessary.

The First Certification Judgment shows this in practice. The first text was submitted to the Constitutional Court in 1996, but the Court refused certification because several provisions did not comply with the Principles. The lecture identifies important issues including socio-economic rights, separation of powers between legislature and executive, and other instances of non-compliance. Woolman and Swanepoel describe the decision as extraordinary because a judicial body effectively declared a constitutional text unconstitutional despite its adoption by 86% of the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly.

After the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Assembly amended the text. The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996, and the Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Principles. The lecture describes this as the final legal hurdle in South Africa’s transition to full democracy.

The Constitutional Principles also explain the content of the Final Constitution. Features such as constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal suffrage, separation of powers, independent courts, multi-sphere government, special amendment procedures and recognition of traditional leadership were all connected to the Principles. This means the Final Constitution’s structure was not accidental; it was shaped by legally binding negotiated commitments.

Critically, certification created a democratic tension because an unelected Constitutional Court reviewed a text adopted by an elected Constitutional Assembly. However, this tension is justified because the Court was not substituting its political preferences for those of the Assembly. It was enforcing the legal conditions that made the democratic drafting process possible. Certification was therefore counter-majoritarian in form, but democratic-constitutional in function.

In conclusion, the Constitutional Principles and certification mechanism made the Final Constitution both democratic and legally legitimate. The elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the text, but the Constitutional Court ensured that it complied with the agreed Principles. This protected the negotiated settlement, strengthened constitutional supremacy and completed the movement from apartheid legality to constitutional democracy.

Common mistakes / warnings

One of the most important objections concerned the inclusion of socio-economic rights in the justiciable Bill of Rights.

The rights included, among others:

housing under s 26;

healthcare, food, water and social security under s 27;

basic education under s 29;

children’s rights under s 28.

There were three main objections.

2. Core Thesis

The First Certification Judgment shows that South Africa’s Final Constitution was not merely politically negotiated, but legally controlled. The Constitutional Court refused to certify the first New Text because some provisions failed to comply with the binding Constitutional Principles, while also confirming that socio-economic rights, flexible separation of powers and judicial certification were compatible with South Africa’s constitutional democracy. The Second Certification Judgment then completed the transition by certifying the Amended Text after the defects were corrected.

Use this as your opening paragraph for a longer answer.

Theme 3 Lecture 12/13Key objections: socio-economic rights, separation of powers, non-compliance

The lecture says that once the final draft was submitted, all political parties other than the ANC and PAC lodged objections to provisions in the New Text, and the Court interpreted the Constitutional Principles holistically rather than with technical rigidity.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: what certification meant

The certification process was the legal mechanism that made the negotiated transition enforceable. The Constitutional Assembly had democratic authority to draft the Final Constitution, but the text could not come into force unless the Constitutional Court certified that it complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles.

The First Certification Judgment is therefore important because it shows that even the making of the Final Constitution was subject to law. The Court did not simply approve the text because it had political support. It tested the text against the Principles and refused certification where the New Text failed to comply.

The Theme 3 lecture records that the first draft of the Final Constitution was submitted to the Constitutional Court on 10 May 1996, but was not certified because several provisions did not align with the Constitutional Principles.

B. The Court’s certification approach

The Constitutional Court evaluated the text in two ways:

  • Structural enquiry: did the basic structure and premises of the New Text accord with the Constitutional Principles?
  • Substantive enquiry: did the contents of the provisions comply with the Constitutional Principles?

The lecture explains that if the basic structure had not aligned, the Court would not have needed to continue with the substantive enquiry. However, the structure did align, so the judgment dealt mostly with substantive objections.

The Court’s task was legal, not political. It did not ask whether the New Text was the best possible Constitution. It asked whether the New Text complied with the agreed Constitutional Principles. This distinction is important because it protects you from saying the Court “rewrote” or “politically rejected” the Constitution.

Exam sentence

The Court’s certification role was not to choose the ideal Constitution, but to decide whether the Constitution chosen by the elected Constitutional Assembly complied with the binding Constitutional Principles.

C. Holistic interpretation of the Constitutional Principles

The Court interpreted the Constitutional Principles holistically and not with technical rigidity. This meant the Court did not apply the Principles like narrow checklist items detached from the purpose of the transition. Instead, it considered whether the text as a whole allowed citizens to exercise fundamental rights and freedoms.

Exam sentence

Certification required legal interpretation, but not mechanical literalism: the Principles had to be read holistically in light of the purpose of constitutional democracy.

D. Objection 1: socio-economic rights in a justiciable Bill of Rights

Exam warning: do not say socio-economic rights caused non-certification. The Court rejected that objection. It accepted that socio-economic rights could form part of a justiciable Bill of Rights and that their budgetary implications did not automatically breach separation of powers.

One of the most important objections concerned the inclusion of socio-economic rights in the justiciable Bill of Rights.

The rights included, among others:

housing under s 26;

healthcare, food, water and social security under s 27;

basic education under s 29;

children’s rights under s 28.

There were three main objections.

E. Socio-economic rights objection 1: not “universally accepted” rights

The first argument was that socio-economic rights should not be included in a justiciable Bill of Rights because they were not “universally accepted” fundamental rights in the same way as traditional civil and political rights.

The Court rejected this. The lecture explains that CP II allowed the inclusion of additional rights beyond universally accepted rights. The Constitution was not limited to traditional civil and political rights; it embraced a broader transformative vision of rights protection.

Exam sentence

The Court rejected the idea that the Bill of Rights had to be limited to traditional civil and political rights; socio-economic rights were compatible with the broader transformative vision permitted by the Constitutional Principles.

F. Socio-economic rights objection 2: separation of powers and budgeting

The second argument was that socio-economic rights would violate separation of powers because courts would interfere with budgeting and policy, which are usually matters for the executive and legislature.

The Court rejected this too. The lecture notes that courts already make orders with budgetary implications, such as legal aid and equality remedies, and socio-economic rights were not fundamentally different.

This is an important precision point: the Court did not say courts may freely run budgets or take over government policy. It said the mere fact that a right has budgetary implications does not make it non-justiciable or unconstitutional.

Exam sentence

The Court accepted that socio-economic rights may have budgetary consequences, but held that budgetary consequences alone do not make a right non-justiciable or incompatible with separation of powers.

G. Socio-economic rights objection 3: non-justiciability

The third argument was that courts could not enforce rights tied to resources.

The Court rejected the objection. The lecture says socio-economic rights are at least partially justiciable, and at minimum can be negatively enforced, meaning the state may not unjustifiably interfere with existing access to those rights.

The key takeaway from the lecture is:

socio-economic rights are constitutionally valid and enforceable;

their inclusion does not breach separation of powers;

courts play a legitimate role in their protection;

their inclusion was consistent with the Constitutional Principles.

Exam sentence

The First Certification Judgment confirmed that socio-economic rights are justiciable, although their enforcement requires sensitivity to institutional competence and resources.

H. Objection 2: separation of powers between legislature and executive

The second major objection concerned separation of powers. The challenge was based on Constitutional Principle VI, which required separation between the legislature, executive and judiciary, with checks and balances to ensure accountability.

The objectors argued that the New Text allowed members of the executive also to sit in the legislature. They said this weakened separation of powers, enhanced executive dominance and undermined democratic accountability.

The comparative argument was that countries such as the United States, France and the Netherlands used stricter models of separation, so South Africa’s model was defective.

I. The Court’s response: no single universal model

The Court rejected the argument that CP VI required strict or complete separation between the executive and legislature.

The Court’s starting point was that there was no issue with separation involving the judiciary; the dispute concerned only executive-legislature overlap. The lecture explains the Court’s response:

there is no single model of separation of powers;

systems differ between countries;

separation is always partial, not absolute;

the core idea is balance between functional independence and checks and balances;

branches are partly interacting, not wholly disjointed.

South Africa deliberately adopted overlap between the legislature and executive. The executive being drawn from the legislature could enhance accountability, because Cabinet is accountable to Parliament under s 92(2), Cabinet members must report to Parliament under s 92(3), and Parliament can remove the President under s 89.

The Court also recognised that powers were formally separated in the constitutional design: legislative authority under s 43, executive authority under ss 85 and 125, and judicial authority under s 165. The final finding was that CP VI did not require strict separation, and the New Text’s hybrid model was constitutionally compliant.

Exam sentence

The First Certification Judgment confirms that South Africa’s separation of powers is flexible and contextual: CP VI requires separation with checks and balances, not a rigid American-style separation.

J. Objection 3: immunising legislation from constitutional scrutiny

The Court did, however, find real non-compliance in other parts of the New Text.

One problem was that some provisions attempted to shield ordinary legislation from constitutional review. For example, NT 241(1) provided that the Labour Relations Act would remain valid despite the Constitution. The problem was that this attempted to insulate ordinary legislation from constitutional scrutiny.

This violated the logic of constitutional supremacy. Under CP IV, the Constitution had to be the supreme law. If Parliament could protect ordinary legislation from constitutional review, then constitutional supremacy and justiciability would be undermined.

Woolman and Swanepoel also note that section 241(1) attempted to place the Labour Relations Act beyond constitutional scrutiny, which was impermissible because CP IV required constitutional supremacy and CP II and CP VII required a justiciable and enforceable Bill of Rights. They also note that a similar schedule provision attempted to place the Promotion of National Unity and Reconciliation Act beyond constitutional scrutiny.

Exam sentence

The Court refused to certify provisions that attempted to place ordinary legislation beyond constitutional review because that would undermine constitutional supremacy and the justiciability of the Bill of Rights.

K. Objection 4: constitutional amendment procedures and Bill of Rights entrenchment

Entrenchment precision: the problem was that the Bill of Rights did not receive sufficiently distinct protection from amendment when compared with ordinary constitutional provisions. CP II required entrenched and justiciable fundamental rights, and CP XV required special procedures and special majorities for constitutional amendment.

Another important non-compliance issue concerned amendment of the Constitution.

CP XV required special majorities and special procedures for constitutional amendments. The purpose was to protect the Constitution from rapid or opportunistic amendment. The New Text provided supermajorities, such as two-thirds of the National Assembly for general amendments and 75% for foundational provisions, but it did not provide distinct special procedures. The lecture says the Court reasoned that “special procedures” must mean more than higher voting thresholds; it required additional deliberative and procedural safeguards.

The Bill of Rights was also not sufficiently entrenched. CP II required rights to be entrenched and justiciable. The objection was that the Bill of Rights was not more protected than other constitutional provisions. The Court stated that entrenchment requires a higher level of protection than ordinary constitutional provisions, and the New Text did not sufficiently protect the Bill of Rights because it only required a two-thirds majority in the National Assembly.

Woolman and Swanepoel confirm this: the New Text’s amendment provision failed to comply with CP XV, which required special procedures as well as special majorities, and also failed to comply with CP II because it did not give the Bill of Rights greater protection from amendment than the rest of the Constitution.

Exam sentence

The Court required both numerical and procedural entrenchment: a supreme Constitution must be harder to amend than ordinary law, and the Bill of Rights must receive special protection.

L. Other grounds of non-certification

Woolman and Swanepoel list several additional grounds on which the First Certification Judgment found non-compliance. These are useful for 90% answers, but do not overload a short answer with all of them.

The key examples include:

Defect in the New TextWhy it mattered
Collective bargainingSection 23 gave collective bargaining rights to employers’ associations but not individual employers; the Court held CP XXVIII required individual employers to have a constitutional right to engage in collective bargaining.
Public Protector and Auditor-General independenceSection 194 did not adequately safeguard their independence, contrary to CP XXIX.
Public Service CommissionSection 196 did not specify the PSC’s powers and functions clearly enough to certify its independence and its relationship to provincial administration.
Local governmentChapter 7 did not create an adequate framework for local government structures and powers.
Provincial powersThe Court found problems linked to whether provincial powers and functions were substantially less or inferior compared to the Interim Constitution.

Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Court rejected the New Text on nine discrete grounds, many of which concerned institutions designed to protect the rule of law and constitutional democracy.

Exam sentence

The First Certification Judgment was not a general political rejection of the Constitution; it identified specific legal defects in the text’s compliance with the Constitutional Principles.

M. Why the First Certification Judgment mattered

The First Certification Judgment mattered because it proved that certification was real. The Court did not rubber-stamp the New Text. It enforced the negotiated constitutional pact even against a text adopted by the elected Constitutional Assembly.

Woolman and Swanepoel describe this as extraordinary: a judicial body declared a constitutional text “unconstitutional” even though it had been adopted by 86% of the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly.

The case showed:

Constitutional Principles were binding;

constitutional supremacy was real;

the Bill of Rights had to be genuinely entrenched and justiciable;

ordinary legislation could not be immunised from review;

separation of powers did not require strict separation;

the Court could enforce the settlement while respecting democratic choices.

Exam sentence

The First Certification Judgment showed that South Africa’s Constitution was born under law: even the elected constitution-making body had to comply with the binding legal conditions of the transition.

N. Critical nuance: the Court’s role was legal, but not value-neutral

A strong answer should add nuance.

The Court said its role was legal, not political. However, Woolman and Swanepoel note that the Court’s methodology created interpretive difficulties because future interpretations of the Final Constitution had to be consistent with the Court’s certification interpretations, and the Court sometimes placed its own gloss on provisions that were susceptible to multiple interpretations. They argue that despite the Court’s claim that its role was purely legal, some interpretations reflected a preferred political reading of the text.

This does not mean the Court acted illegitimately. It means certification involved constitutional interpretation, and constitutional interpretation can never be entirely mechanical.

Exam sentence

The certification role was formally legal, but it inevitably involved constitutional judgment because the Court had to interpret broad Constitutional Principles and apply them to contested constitutional choices.

O. Second Certification Judgment: final legal hurdle

After the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Assembly amended the text. The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles, and the judgment focused on confirming that the shortcomings identified in the First Certification Judgment had been sufficiently addressed.

The lecture describes the Second Certification Judgment as the final legal hurdle for South Africa’s transition to full democracy. After certification, President Nelson Mandela signed the Constitution into law on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville. The 1996 Constitution took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the 1993 Interim Constitution.

Woolman and Swanepoel add that although the entire constitution-making process could theoretically have reopened after non-certification, the major parties confined themselves to amendments designed to satisfy the Court’s concerns.

Exam sentence

The Second Certification Judgment completed the transition by confirming that the Amended Text had cured the defects identified in the First Certification Judgment.

P. Conclusion

The First and Second Certification Judgments show the legal discipline behind South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. The First Certification Judgment confirmed that socio-economic rights could be included in a justiciable Bill of Rights and that separation of powers did not require strict separation between executive and legislature. But it refused certification where provisions failed to comply with the Constitutional Principles, especially where they threatened constitutional supremacy, rights entrenchment, institutional independence, local government structure or provincial powers. The Second Certification Judgment then certified the Amended Text after those defects were addressed. Together, the cases show that the Final Constitution was not merely politically negotiated; it was legally certified as compliant with the solemn pact of the Constitutional Principles.

6. 5-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Briefly explain the First Certification Judgment.”

“Why was the first constitutional text not certified?”

“What was the Second Certification Judgment?”

Answer:

The First Certification Judgment concerned whether the New Text of the Final Constitution complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles. The Constitutional Court evaluated whether the basic structure of the text and its specific provisions aligned with the Principles. The structure did align, but several substantive provisions did not.

The Court rejected objections to socio-economic rights, holding that they could be included in a justiciable Bill of Rights and were at least partially enforceable. It also rejected the argument that separation of powers required strict separation between executive and legislature. However, the Court refused certification because some provisions failed to comply with the Principles, including problems with constitutional amendment procedures, Bill of Rights entrenchment, institutional independence and provisions attempting to immunise legislation from constitutional review.

The Second Certification Judgment, delivered on 4 December 1996, unanimously certified the Amended Text after the defects were corrected.

7. 10-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Discuss the First and Second Certification Judgments.”

“Explain the main objections in the First Certification Judgment.”

Answer:

The First Certification Judgment tested whether the New Text of the Final Constitution complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles. The Court considered the text structurally and substantively. Structurally, the text aligned with the Principles, so the judgment focused mainly on substantive objections.

One objection concerned socio-economic rights in the justiciable Bill of Rights. Objectors argued that rights such as housing, healthcare, food, water, social security, basic education and children’s rights were not universally accepted, would violate separation of powers, and were not justiciable. The Court rejected these arguments. It held that CP II allowed additional rights beyond traditional civil and political rights, that courts already make orders with budgetary implications, and that socio-economic rights are at least partially justiciable, including through negative enforcement.

A second objection concerned separation of powers. The challenge was that members of the executive could also sit in the legislature, allegedly weakening accountability. The Court rejected a rigid model. It held that there is no universal model of separation of powers, that separation is partial rather than absolute, and that South Africa’s overlap between executive and legislature can strengthen accountability through parliamentary responsibility. CP VI did not require strict separation.

However, the Court still refused certification because other provisions did not comply with the Principles. These included attempts to immunise ordinary legislation from constitutional review, inadequate amendment procedures, insufficient entrenchment of the Bill of Rights, and insufficient protection of some independent institutions.

The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Court unanimously certified the Amended Text after confirming that the shortcomings identified in the First Certification Judgment had been addressed. This was the final legal hurdle before Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996 and it took effect on 4 February 1997.

8. 15-Mark Version

Use this for:

“Critically discuss the First and Second Certification Judgments.”

“Explain why the first constitutional text was not certified.”

“Discuss the Court’s approach to socio-economic rights and separation of powers in the First Certification Judgment.”

15-mark answer structure

ParagraphFunction
1Thesis: certification as legal control
2Court’s two-stage certification approach
3Holistic interpretation
4Socio-economic rights objection
5Separation of powers objection
6Immunising legislation from review
7Amendment and Bill of Rights entrenchment
8Other non-compliance grounds
9Significance of non-certification
10Critical nuance: legal but not value-neutral
11Second Certification Judgment
12Conclusion

15-mark full answer

The First and Second Certification Judgments were central to South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. They show that the Final Constitution was not merely politically negotiated, but legally controlled by the 34 Constitutional Principles in the Interim Constitution. The First Certification Judgment refused to certify the first New Text because some provisions did not comply with the Principles. The Second Certification Judgment later certified the Amended Text once the defects were corrected.

The Constitutional Court’s certification approach had two parts. First, it asked whether the basic structure and premises of the New Text aligned with the Constitutional Principles. Secondly, it asked whether the contents of specific provisions complied with the Principles. The structure did align, so the judgment dealt mostly with substantive objections.

The Court interpreted the Constitutional Principles holistically rather than with technical rigidity. This was important because the Principles were broad constitutional standards, not narrow technical clauses. The Court had to apply them in a way that allowed citizens to exercise fundamental rights and freedoms.

The first major objection concerned socio-economic rights. Objectors argued that rights such as housing, healthcare, food, water, social security, basic education and children’s rights should not be included in a justiciable Bill of Rights. They argued that these rights were not universally accepted, would interfere with budgeting and policy, and were not justiciable.

The Court rejected those objections. It held that CP II allowed rights beyond traditional civil and political rights, and that the Constitution could embrace a broader transformative vision of rights protection. It also held that budgetary consequences did not make socio-economic rights constitutionally improper, because courts already make orders with budgetary implications. Finally, the Court held that socio-economic rights are at least partially justiciable and can at minimum be negatively enforced.

The second major objection concerned separation of powers between the legislature and executive. Objectors argued that allowing members of the executive also to sit in the legislature weakened separation of powers and enhanced executive dominance. This objection was based on CP VI, which required separation of powers with checks and balances ensuring accountability.

The Court rejected a rigid approach. It held that there is no single universal model of separation of powers. Separation is partial rather than absolute, and the core question is whether the system balances functional independence with checks and balances. In South Africa, executive-legislative overlap was deliberate and could support accountability because Cabinet is accountable to Parliament, must report to Parliament, and Parliament can remove the President.

The Court therefore held that CP VI did not require strict separation. The constitutional design formally separated legislative authority under s 43, executive authority under ss 85 and 125, and judicial authority under s 165. South Africa’s hybrid model of partial separation and strong accountability through overlap was constitutionally compliant.

However, the Court did refuse certification on other grounds. One major problem was that some provisions attempted to place ordinary legislation beyond constitutional review. NT 241(1), for example, attempted to protect the Labour Relations Act from constitutional scrutiny. This undermined CP IV, which required constitutional supremacy, and also undermined the justiciability and enforceability of the Bill of Rights.

Another problem concerned constitutional amendment procedures. CP XV required special majorities and special procedures to protect the Constitution from rapid or opportunistic amendment. The New Text provided supermajorities, but not distinct special procedures. The Court reasoned that special procedures must mean more than higher voting thresholds. Constitutional amendments required both numerical and procedural entrenchment.

The Bill of Rights was also not sufficiently entrenched. CP II required rights to be entrenched and justiciable, but the Bill of Rights was not protected at a higher level than ordinary constitutional provisions. The Court held that entrenchment required greater protection than ordinary constitutional provisions.

Woolman and Swanepoel identify other grounds of non-certification, including the collective bargaining provision, inadequate protection of the independence of the Public Protector and Auditor-General, insufficient entrenchment of the Public Service Commission’s functions and powers, defects in the local government chapter, and issues with provincial powers. They explain that the Court rejected the New Text on nine discrete grounds, many involving institutions and mechanisms designed to protect the rule of law and constitutional democracy.

The significance of the First Certification Judgment is that certification was real. The Court did not rubber-stamp the work of the Constitutional Assembly. It enforced the Constitutional Principles even against a text adopted by a strong democratic majority. Woolman and Swanepoel describe this as extraordinary because a judicial body effectively declared a constitutional text unconstitutional despite its adoption by 86% of the democratically elected Constitutional Assembly.

Critically, the Court’s role was formally legal, not political. It had to test the New Text against the Constitutional Principles, not decide whether it liked the political choices of the Constitutional Assembly. However, Woolman and Swanepoel note that certification still involved interpretive judgment, and that the Court sometimes placed its own gloss on provisions capable of multiple interpretations. This means certification was legal, but not purely mechanical.

After the First Certification Judgment, the Constitutional Assembly amended the text. The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles and confirmed that the shortcomings identified in the First Certification Judgment had been addressed.

This completed the legal transition. Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville, and the 1996 Constitution took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

In conclusion, the First and Second Certification Judgments show how South Africa combined democracy with legality. The elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution, but the Constitutional Court enforced the binding Constitutional Principles. The First Certification Judgment confirmed socio-economic rights and a flexible separation of powers, but refused certification where the text failed to protect constitutional supremacy, rights entrenchment, institutional independence and local/provincial government requirements. The Second Certification Judgment then certified the corrected Amended Text, completing the legal foundation of South Africa’s constitutional democracy.

9. How to Adapt This in the Exam

If the question asks “why was the first text not certified?”

Use:

  • First text submitted but not certified;
  • some provisions did not comply with Constitutional Principles;
  • immunising legislation from review;
  • amendment procedures;
  • Bill of Rights entrenchment;
  • independence of institutions;
  • local government and provincial powers;
  • avoid saying “because socio-economic rights were included” — that objection was rejected.

If the question asks about socio-economic rights

Use:

housing, healthcare, food, water, social security, education, children;

objections: not universally accepted, separation of powers, not justiciable;

Court rejected all three;

at least partially justiciable;

inclusion compatible with Constitutional Principles.

If the question asks about separation of powers

Use:

CP VI;

objection to executive sitting in legislature;

no universal model;

strict separation not required;

hybrid model allowed;

accountability through Parliament;

formal separation under ss 43, 85, 125 and 165.

If the question asks about Second Certification

Use:

after defects corrected;

delivered 4 December 1996;

Amended Text unanimously certified;

final legal hurdle;

Mandela signed 10 December 1996;

Constitution took effect 4 February 1997.

If the question asks critically about certification

Use:

tension: unelected Court reviewing elected Assembly;

answer: Court enforced agreed Principles;

legal, not political role;

but interpretation was not mechanical;

strengthened legitimacy and constitutional supremacy.

10. Common Mistakes to Avoid

MistakeWhy it loses marksBetter version
Saying the first text was certifiedWrongThe First Certification Judgment refused certification.
Saying socio-economic rights caused non-certificationWrongThe Court rejected objections to socio-economic rights.
Saying CP VI required strict separationWrongThe Court held no strict universal model was required.
Saying the Court acted politicallyToo broadThe Court’s mandate was legal, though interpretation involved judgment.
Forgetting that the structure compliedMisses methodStructure aligned; substantive defects caused non-certification.
Saying all objections succeededWrongSome were rejected, some upheld.
Forgetting Bill of Rights entrenchmentMajor high-mark pointCP II required stronger protection for the Bill of Rights.
Forgetting constitutional amendment proceduresMajor high-mark pointCP XV required special majorities and special procedures.
Confusing First and Second CertificationSerious errorFirst = not certified; Second = Amended Text certified.
Saying Second Certification was the first time the Court considered the textWrongIt responded to defects identified in First Certification.

11. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton

First Certification = New Text tested against 34 Principles.

Court’s method = structure first, then substance.

Structure complied; some substance did not.

Socio-economic rights objection rejected.

Socio-economic rights = valid, enforceable, at least partially justiciable.

Separation of powers objection rejected.

CP VI = no strict universal model.

Non-certification grounds = review immunity, amendments, Bill of Rights entrenchment, institutional independence, local government, provincial powers.

First text not certified.

Second Certification = 4 Dec 1996, Amended Text certified.

Mandela signed = 10 Dec 1996, Sharpeville.

Constitution effective = 4 Feb 1997.

One-sentence memory trigger

First Certification says “socio-economic rights and flexible separation are fine, but this text still fails because some provisions undermine constitutional supremacy, entrenchment and institutional independence”; Second Certification says “the corrected text now passes.”

12. 70 / 80 / 90+ Upgrade Guide

Mark rangeWhat your answer must include
70%First text not certified; Second text certified; basic reasons; socio-economic rights and separation of powers objections
80%Add Court’s two-stage method, CP II, CP IV, CP VI, CP XV, key non-certification grounds
90%+Add holistic interpretation, legal-not-political role, Woolman/Swanepoel critique, democratic tension, and precise distinction between rejected objections and successful objections

Final exam warning for this essay

The biggest trap is saying:

“The first text was rejected because socio-economic rights were included.”

That is wrong. The Court accepted socio-economic rights. The first text failed because of other non-compliance issues such as amendment procedures, Bill of Rights entrenchment, institutional independence, local government/provincial powers and attempts to immunise legislation from constitutional review.

Thought for 11s

Yes, Nix — for Theme 3, we should not create too many more. We already have the main legal backbone.

5/10/15-mark versions

5. 5-Mark Version

The 1996 Constitution completed South Africa’s transition from apartheid legality to constitutional democracy. It was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996, is now referred to as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, certified by the Constitutional Court and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

It is revolutionary because it entrenched a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, non-racialism, non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages. It is negotiated because it emerged from compromise, democratic drafting and certification under the Constitutional Principles.

6. 10-Mark Version

The 1996 Constitution is the final product of South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. It was negotiated because it emerged from CODESA, the MPNF, the Interim Constitution, the elected Constitutional Assembly and the certification process. It was revolutionary because it replaced apartheid parliamentary sovereignty and racial exclusion with constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage and enforceable rights.

After the first democratic election, the Constitutional Assembly had to draft a new Constitution within two years and in line with the Constitutional Principles. The ANC had the majority but not the two-thirds majority required to pass the Constitution alone, so consensus with other parties remained necessary. The Assembly also launched a large public participation process to help ensure popular acceptance of the Final Constitution.

The text was then certified by the Constitutional Court. The first text was not certified, but the Amended Text was unanimously certified in the Second Certification Judgment on 4 December 1996. Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville, and it took effect on 4 February 1997.

Its key features include a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy and rule of law, non-racialism and non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages. Therefore, the 1996 Constitution is both legally legitimate and transformative.

7. 15-Mark Version: Structure

Common mistakes / warnings

Theme 3 Essay Bank 7

The 1996 Final Constitution as a Negotiated Constitutional Revolution

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

2. Core Thesis

The 1996 Constitution was a negotiated constitutional revolution because it emerged through compromise, democratic drafting and Constitutional Court certification, yet it radically replaced apartheid’s parliamentary sovereignty and racial exclusion with constitutional supremacy, universal adult suffrage, a justiciable Bill of Rights, separation of powers, cooperative government, Chapter 9 accountability institutions and a transformative commitment to dignity, equality and freedom.
