The Theme 3 lecture states that the Constitution was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996, is now referred to as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, certified by the Constitutional Court and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the 1993 Interim Constitution.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: negotiated but revolutionary

The 1996 Constitution should be understood as the culmination of South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. It was negotiated because it arose from CODESA, the MPNF, the Interim Constitution, the 34 Constitutional Principles, the elected Constitutional Assembly and certification by the Constitutional Court. It was revolutionary because it fundamentally changed the legal and political order: South Africa moved from parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion and authoritarian state power to constitutional supremacy, universal adult suffrage, a justiciable Bill of Rights and democratic constitutionalism.

The Final Constitution was not imposed by one victorious side. The ANC won the first democratic election and held the majority in the National Assembly, but it did not have the two-thirds majority required to pass the Final Constitution alone. The Theme 3 lecture records that the ANC received 63.66% of the vote and 252 seats, which was not quite two-thirds, meaning it had to seek consensus with other parties, especially the NP.

B. Democratic legitimacy: the Constitutional Assembly

The legitimacy of the Final Constitution came from the fact that it was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, a body made up after the first democratic election. This was the solution to the legitimacy problem that had troubled CODESA: unelected negotiators could create an Interim Constitution, but the permanent Constitution had to be drafted by elected representatives.

The Theme 3 lecture says that after the first democratic election, in which all persons could vote for the first time, Mandela became South Africa’s first democratically elected President, and the Constitutional Assembly had the mammoth task of drafting a new Constitution in line with all the Constitutional Principles within two years.

C. Public participation and popular legitimacy

The Final Constitution also gained legitimacy through public participation. The Theme 3 lecture notes that an enormous public participation process was launched to aid popular acceptance of the final document; if people felt they had a hand in shaping the Final Constitution, they would feel a greater connection to it.

This point matters because the Final Constitution was not only a legal document drafted by elites. Its legitimacy also depended on the broader public being invited into the constitution-making process.

Exam sentence: The public participation process helped transform the Final Constitution from a negotiated elite settlement into a constitutional text with broader democratic ownership.

D. Certification as legal legitimacy

The Final Constitution also had to pass a legal test. The first draft was submitted to the Constitutional Court on 10 May 1996, but it was not certified because several provisions did not align with the Constitutional Principles.

The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles. The lecture describes this as the final legal hurdle for South Africa’s transition to full democracy.

This means the Final Constitution had two kinds of legitimacy:

Type of legitimacySource
Democratic legitimacyDrafted by elected Constitutional Assembly after universal suffrage election
Legal legitimacyCertified by the Constitutional Court as compliant with the Constitutional Principles

E. Formal enactment and coming into force

Citation precision: historically, the Final Constitution was enacted as Act 108 of 1996. For modern legal writing, it should be cited as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, without the Act number. It was signed by President Mandela on 10 December 1996 at Sharpeville and took effect on 4 February 1997.

The Final Constitution was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996. It is now correctly cited as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, without the Act number. It was signed into law by President Nelson Mandela on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the 1993 Interim Constitution.

The location of signing — Sharpeville — was symbolically powerful because Sharpeville represented apartheid state violence. Signing the Constitution there helped mark the move from repression to rights-based democracy.

F. Key feature 1: Justiciable Bill of Rights

One of the most important features of the 1996 Constitution is a justiciable Bill of Rights containing basic rights for everyone. The Theme 3 lecture identifies this as one of the important aspects of the 1996 Constitution.

This was revolutionary because apartheid-era rights were not entrenched in a supreme Constitution. Under the new order, rights could be enforced in courts, and law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution could be declared invalid.

G. Key feature 2: Universal adult suffrage

The Constitution entrenched universal adult suffrage and democratic elections. This directly responded to apartheid’s exclusion of the majority from political power. The Theme 3 lecture lists universal adult suffrage as a key aspect of the 1996 Constitution.

Exam sentence: Universal adult suffrage is revolutionary because it replaced racially restricted political membership with equal citizenship.

H. Key feature 3: Constitutional supremacy and rule of law

High-value link: section 1 contains the founding values and is the most strongly entrenched part of the Constitution. Amending section 1 requires the highest threshold under section 74, which reinforces the idea that the break from the old order is structurally protected.

The 1996 Constitution entrenched constitutional supremacy and the rule of law. The Theme 3 lecture identifies these as key features of the Constitution.

This is the clearest break from parliamentary sovereignty. Parliament is no longer the highest law-maker in the old sense. All law and conduct must comply with the Constitution.

I. Key feature 4: Non-racialism and non-sexism

The Constitution also entrenched non-racialism and non-sexism, which directly responded to apartheid racial hierarchy and patriarchal inequality. These are not decorative values. They form part of the constitutional foundation for equality, transformation and inclusive citizenship.

The Theme 3 lecture lists non-racialism and non-sexism among the important aspects of the 1996 Constitution.

J. Key feature 5: Cooperative government

The Constitution created a system of cooperative government across national, provincial and local spheres. The Theme 3 lecture expressly lists cooperative government at national, provincial and local levels as a key aspect of the 1996 Constitution.

This responded to the negotiated settlement’s concern with provincial powers and multi-sphere government. It also avoided a purely centralised state.

K. Key feature 6: Separation of powers

The Constitution entrenched separation of legislative, executive and judicial authority. The Theme 3 lecture lists separation of powers as a key aspect of the 1996 Constitution.

This limited public power by dividing authority between institutions and creating checks and balances.

L. Key feature 7: Chapter 9 institutions

The Constitution created institutions supporting constitutional democracy, including Chapter 9 institutions. The Theme 3 lecture lists these institutions as a key aspect of the 1996 Constitution.

These institutions add accountability beyond the classic legislature-executive-judiciary model.

M. Key feature 8: Traditional leaders and languages

The Constitution also recognised traditional leaders and languages. The Theme 3 lecture lists traditional leaders and languages as important aspects of the 1996 Constitution.

This matters because South Africa’s constitutional order had to be democratic and rights-based, while also recognising cultural and linguistic diversity.

N. Transformative nature of the Constitution

The 1996 Constitution is also transformative. The Theme 3 lecture states that both the Interim Constitution and Final Constitution addressed the injustices of apartheid by incorporating revolutionary provisions such as a justiciable Bill of Rights, and that the 1996 Constitution in particular has been described as transformative.

Transformative constitutionalism means the Constitution provides a framework and normative direction for changing South Africa’s legal, political and social order. The lecture uses Karl Klare’s famous definition: transformative constitutionalism is a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa’s political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

The lecture also links this to Langa CJ’s idea that the Constitution is a bridge from an unjust, divided past to a future based on rights, equality and dignity.

O. Critical evaluation: revolution through law, not revolution outside law

A strong answer must explain the paradox: the Final Constitution was revolutionary, but it did not come through violent overthrow or legal rupture outside the system. It came through negotiation, elections, legal continuity, Constitutional Principles and certification.

This creates the best 90% sentence:

The 1996 Constitution was revolutionary in substance, but negotiated and legal in form.

It radically changed the basis of state power, but it did so through structured legal mechanisms.

P. Conclusion

The 1996 Constitution completed South Africa’s transition to constitutional democracy. It was drafted by a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly, subjected to public participation, certified by the Constitutional Court and brought into force on 4 February 1997. Its key features — a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, non-racialism, non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, recognition of traditional leadership and languages — show why it was revolutionary. But because it emerged through negotiation and certification, it was also a carefully structured constitutional settlement. This is why it is best described as a negotiated constitutional revolution.

5. 5-Mark Version

It was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996, is now referred to as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, certified by the Constitutional Court and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

It is revolutionary because it entrenched a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, non-racialism, non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages. It is negotiated because it emerged from compromise, democratic drafting and certification under the Constitutional Principles.

6. 10-Mark Version

The 1996 Constitution is the final product of South Africa’s negotiated constitutional revolution. It was negotiated because it emerged from CODESA, the MPNF, the Interim Constitution, the elected Constitutional Assembly and the certification process. It was revolutionary because it replaced apartheid parliamentary sovereignty and racial exclusion with constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage and enforceable rights.

After the first democratic election, the Constitutional Assembly had to draft a new Constitution within two years and in line with the Constitutional Principles. The ANC had the majority but not the two-thirds majority required to pass the Constitution alone, so consensus with other parties remained necessary. The Assembly also launched a large public participation process to help ensure popular acceptance of the Final Constitution.

The text was then certified by the Constitutional Court. The first text was not certified, but the Amended Text was unanimously certified in the Second Certification Judgment on 4 December 1996. Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996 in Sharpeville, and it took effect on 4 February 1997.

Its key features include a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy and rule of law, non-racialism and non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages. Therefore, the 1996 Constitution is both legally legitimate and transformative.

5. 5-Mark Version

The Constitution is supreme, but it is not frozen. Amendments allow it to be updated, refined and strengthened in line with changing societal needs and norms.

The amendment process is governed by section 74: section 1 requires a 75% National Assembly majority; Chapter 2 requires a two-thirds National Assembly majority plus support of at least six provinces in the NCOP; and other provisions generally require a two-thirds National Assembly majority, with NCOP support where provincial interests are affected. The amendment Bill must be gazetted at least 30 days before voting, may not contain unrelated provisions, must go through public participation and must be referred to the President for assent. This protects constitutional supremacy by making the Constitution harder to amend than ordinary legislation.

6. 10-Mark Version

The 1996 Constitution is supreme and stable, but it remains capable of amendment. This is necessary because constitutional law must respond to changing social and institutional needs. The Theme 3 lecture explains that amendments update, refine and strengthen the supreme law and align it with changing societal needs and norms.

However, constitutional amendment cannot be as easy as ordinary legislation. The First Certification Judgment confirms this principle because the Court objected to amendment provisions that provided higher voting thresholds without sufficiently distinct special procedures. The Court held that special procedures must mean more than higher majorities.

The amendment process therefore includes the relevant section 74 thresholds, gazetting the Bill at least 30 days before voting, excluding unrelated provisions, public participation and presidential assent.

Examples show how amendments keep the Constitution alive. The 1st Amendment extended TRC amnesty cut-off dates; the 12th altered provincial boundaries; the 17th overhauled the court system; and the 18th recognised South African Sign Language as the 12th official language. These examples show that constitutional amendment can support reconciliation, institutional development, cooperative government and inclusion.

Theme 3 Essay Bank 8

Constitutional Amendments and the Living Constitution: Stability, Change and Constitutional Supremacy

FINAL CORRECTION PASS: This version applies the final Theme 3 accuracy fixes from the audit cycle. It preserves the essay-bank format while tightening legal precision, authority wording, dates, certification details and exam-risk phrasing.

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

Possible exam questionWhat the examiner wants
“Explain how the Constitution may be amended.”Special majorities, process, public participation, assent
“Why are constitutional amendment procedures important?”Protect supremacy, prevent opportunistic change
“Is the Constitution fixed or capable of change?”Living Constitution, controlled amendments
“Discuss examples of constitutional amendments.”1st, 12th, 17th, 18th amendments
“How do amendments show the Constitution is a living document?”Updating language, courts, boundaries, TRC
“Why are special amendment procedures necessary?”Higher law should be harder to amend than ordinary law

2. Core Thesis

The 1996 Constitution is supreme and stable, but not frozen. Constitutional amendments allow the supreme law to be updated, refined and strengthened in response to changing social and institutional needs, while special amendment procedures, public participation and presidential assent protect the Constitution from ordinary political manipulation.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

Authority / exampleUse
s 74 of the 1996 ConstitutionAmendment process and special majorities
Special majorities in NA and NCOPHigher threshold than ordinary legislation
Gazette requirementPublic notice before vote
Public participationDemocratic legitimacy of amendments
Presidential assentFinal formal step
1st Amendment Act 1997Extended TRC amnesty cut-off dates
12th Amendment Act 2005Altered provincial boundaries
17th Amendment Act 2012 / assented to 2013Overhauled court system
18th Amendment Act 2023Recognised South African Sign Language as 12th official language

The Theme 3 lecture states that constitutional amendments are used to update, refine and strengthen the supreme law, and that they align the Constitution with changing societal needs and norms.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: stable but not frozen

The Constitution is the supreme law, but it is not unchangeable. A democratic Constitution must be stable enough to protect rights, institutions and constitutional values, but flexible enough to respond to new social, political and institutional needs.

The Theme 3 lecture explains that amendments are used to update, refine and strengthen the supreme law, and to align the Constitution with changing societal needs and norms.

B. Why amendment procedures matter

Certification link: the First Certification Judgment treated amendment design as a real constitutional issue. The first New Text failed partly because the Bill of Rights was not given sufficiently distinct protection from amendment compared with ordinary constitutional provisions. That is why amendment procedure is not technical detail; it protects constitutional supremacy.

Constitutional amendment procedures matter because a supreme Constitution must be harder to amend than ordinary legislation. If Parliament could amend the Constitution as easily as ordinary law, constitutional supremacy would be weakened.

This point also links back to the First Certification Judgment. One reason the first New Text was not certified was that it did not provide sufficiently special procedures for amendment. The Court held that “special procedures” must mean more than higher voting thresholds; constitutional amendments require additional deliberative and procedural safeguards.

C. Amendment process

Section 74 architecture: amendments are deliberately tiered. Section 1 and section 74(1) require at least 75% of the National Assembly. Chapter 2 amendments require a two-thirds National Assembly majority plus supporting votes of at least six provinces in the NCOP. Other constitutional provisions generally require a two-thirds National Assembly majority, with NCOP concurrence where provincial boundaries, powers, functions or institutions are affected. This gives practical effect to CP XV’s demand for special procedures and special majorities.

The Theme 3 lecture lists the amendment process as including:

  1. the strict section 74 amendment thresholds: 75% of the National Assembly for section 1, two-thirds of the National Assembly plus six provinces for the Bill of Rights, and two-thirds of the National Assembly for ordinary constitutional amendments, with NCOP support required where provincial interests are affected;
  2. a Bill must be gazetted in the Government Gazette at least 30 days before voting;
  3. the Bill may not contain provisions unrelated to the constitutional amendment;
  4. the amendment must go through public participation;
  5. the Bill must be referred to the President for assent.

D. Example 1: First Amendment Act 1997

The Theme 3 lecture identifies the 1st Amendment Act 1997 as an example and notes that it extended amnesty cut-off dates related to the Truth and Reconciliation Commission.

This shows how amendments can be used to support transitional justice and reconciliation processes.

E. Example 2: Twelfth Amendment Act 2005

The 12th Amendment Act 2005 altered provincial boundaries, affecting local government structures and provincial management.

This shows how amendments can adjust the territorial and institutional design of government.

F. Example 3: Seventeenth Amendment Act

The 17th Amendment Act, 2012, assented to in 2013, overhauled the court system. The lecture lists its effects as establishing a single High Court division structure, confirming that the Constitutional Court is the highest court in all matters, designating the Chief Justice as head of the judiciary and redefining the jurisdiction of the Supreme Court of Appeal.

This amendment is important because it shows how the Constitution can evolve institutionally while preserving constitutional supremacy.

G. Example 4: Eighteenth Amendment Act 2023

The 18th Amendment Act 2023 officially recognised South African Sign Language as the 12th official language.

This shows how constitutional amendments can respond to inclusion, dignity and recognition.

H. Critical evaluation

Amendment powers are necessary but potentially dangerous. They allow adaptation, but if used too easily, they can undermine constitutional stability. This is why constitutional amendment requires special procedures, special majorities, public notice and public participation.

The best answer is balanced:

A living Constitution must be capable of change, but constitutional supremacy requires that change be legally difficult, publicly visible and democratically justified.

I. Conclusion

Constitutional amendments show that the 1996 Constitution is stable but living. Amendments allow the constitutional order to respond to transitional justice, institutional reform, provincial boundaries and language recognition. However, because the Constitution is supreme, it may not be amended through ordinary political convenience. Special procedures, special majorities, public participation and presidential assent protect the Constitution from opportunistic change.

The amendment process is governed by section 74: section 1 requires a 75% National Assembly majority; Chapter 2 requires a two-thirds National Assembly majority plus support of at least six provinces in the NCOP; and other provisions generally require a two-thirds National Assembly majority, with NCOP support where provincial interests are affected. The amendment Bill must be gazetted at least 30 days before voting, may not contain unrelated provisions, must go through public participation and must be referred to the President for assent. This protects constitutional supremacy by making the Constitution harder to amend than ordinary legislation.

6. 10-Mark Version

South Africa’s transition is best described as a negotiated constitutional revolution. It was negotiated because the new order emerged through compromise, legal continuity and certification. It was revolutionary because it fundamentally changed the source and limits of public power.

Under apartheid parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was supreme and courts could not invalidate legislation simply because it was unjust. This exposed the weakness of formal legality. The liberation movement then developed a constitutional vision through the Defiance Campaign, Freedom Charter, Sharpeville, Rivonia and ANC Constitutional Guidelines.

CODESA exposed the legitimacy problem: unelected negotiators lacked a democratic mandate to write a permanent Constitution. The two-stage transition solved this. Unelected negotiators drafted the Interim Constitution, while the elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution.

The Interim Constitution created a Government of National Unity, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a new Constitutional Court and 34 Constitutional Principles. The Principles acted as binding benchmarks for the Final Constitution.

The First Certification Judgment refused certification of the first text, while the Second Certification Judgment certified the Amended Text on 4 December 1996. Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996, and it took effect on 4 February 1997.

Common mistakes / warnings

Exam warning: the First Certification Judgment did not reject the first text because of socio-economic rights or flexible separation of powers. The Court accepted both. Non-certification resulted from other defects, including amendment/entrenchment problems, institutional independence issues, local government/provincial powers concerns and attempts to immunise ordinary legislation from constitutional review.

The First Certification Judgment showed that the Constitutional Principles were real. The first text was not certified because some provisions failed to comply with the Principles. The Court rejected objections to socio-economic rights and strict separation of powers, but found defects relating to constitutional review, amendment procedures, Bill of Rights entrenchment, institutional independence, local government and provincial powers.

The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Principles. This was the final legal hurdle for South Africa’s transition to full democracy.

I. Final Constitution

Citation and timing precision: the Final Constitution was historically enacted as Act 108 of 1996, but is now cited as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996. It was signed by President Mandela on 10 December 1996 and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

The Final Constitution was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996, is now referred to as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, certified by the Constitutional Court and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

Its key features include a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law, non-racialism and non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages.

J. Constitutional amendments and the living Constitution

The Constitution is supreme but not frozen. Amendments are used to update, refine and strengthen the supreme law and align it with changing societal needs and norms. Examples include the 1st Amendment linked to TRC amnesty cut-off dates, the 12th Amendment altering provincial boundaries, the 17th Amendment overhauling the court system and the 18th Amendment recognising South African Sign Language as the 12th official language.

Memory triggers

9. What to Memorise

Theme 3 chain: Parliamentary sovereignty → limited judicial review → liberation constitutional vision → CODESA legitimacy problem → violence and compromise → Interim Constitution → 34 Constitutional Principles → First Certification failure → Second Certification success → Final Constitution → living Constitution through amendments.

Memory trigger: South Africa used negotiation and law to escape apartheid legality and build constitutional democracy.

Integrated Theme 3 Final Boss Essay: From Apartheid Legality to Constitutional Democracy

“Critically discuss the legitimacy of South Africa’s Constitution-making process.”Unelected negotiation, elected CA, public participation, certification

2. Core Thesis

South Africa’s transition was a negotiated constitutional revolution: it was negotiated because the new constitutional order emerged through compromise, CODESA, the MPNF, the Interim Constitution, the Constitutional Principles and certification; but it was revolutionary because it replaced apartheid parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion and authoritarian legality with constitutional supremacy, universal adult suffrage, a justiciable Bill of Rights, democratic accountability, separation of powers and a transformative constitutional order.

3. Key Authorities and Events to Remember

4. Full Integrated Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: the big Theme 3 story

Theme 3 is the story of how South Africa moved from apartheid legality to constitutional democracy. The transition was not purely revolutionary in the sense of one side violently overthrowing the old order and imposing a new one. It was negotiated through political compromise and legal continuity. But it was revolutionary in substance because it fundamentally changed the source, structure and limits of public power.

The old order was based on parliamentary sovereignty. The new order is based on constitutional supremacy, universal adult suffrage, a justiciable Bill of Rights, separation of powers, democracy and transformation.

B. Old order: parliamentary sovereignty and limited review

Precision point: South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan. Courts could enforce manner-and-form requirements under the South Africa Act 1909, but this procedural review could only delay, not prevent, substantive constitutional abuse once Parliament complied with the formal rules.

Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was the highest law-maker for substantive purposes. Courts could not invalidate Acts of Parliament merely because they were unjust or rights-violating. However, the old South African model was not fully Diceyan: the South Africa Act 1909 contained enforceable manner-and-form limits for entrenched provisions. Harris I showed that courts could enforce those procedures; Harris II showed Parliament could not create an artificial parliamentary court to override judicial decisions; and Collins showed that once Parliament complied formally, courts would not examine motive or substantive injustice.

This exposed the weakness of apartheid legality: law could be valid in form but unjust in substance.

C. Liberation constitutional vision

The liberation movement did not only resist apartheid. It imagined a different constitutional order. The Defiance Campaign exposed the gap between legality and legitimacy. The Congress of the People and Freedom Charter articulated a vision of equal citizenship, universal suffrage, democracy, rights and socio-economic justice. The lecture identifies the Freedom Charter as a normative precursor to the Final Constitution.

Sharpeville exposed the violence of unchecked state power. Rivonia showed how criminal law could be weaponised against resistance and why entrenched rights were necessary. The ANC Constitutional Guidelines later translated liberation ideals into a more concrete constitutional vision, including constitutionalism and a justiciable Bill of Rights. Woolman and Swanepoel note that the 1988 Guidelines were the ANC’s first public expression of a desire to work towards a negotiated settlement and a commitment to constitutionalism generally.

D. Negotiations: CODESA, violence and compromise

The move to democracy required negotiation because neither side could impose a stable settlement alone. CODESA exposed the legitimacy problem: unelected negotiators lacked the democratic mandate to draft the permanent Constitution. The ANC wanted majority rule and an elected constitution-making body, while the NP wanted power-sharing, strong minority protections and supermajorities.

CODESA II failed in 1992 over voting thresholds and transitional government structure. Negotiations also took place amid violence, including Boipatong, township violence, political assassinations and conflict involving the IFP. The Boipatong Massacre led the ANC to withdraw from negotiations, but the lecture notes that Boipatong clarified that the cost of failure was higher than the cost of compromise.

E. Two-stage transition

The key compromise was the two-stage transition. In Stage 1, unelected negotiating parties drafted and adopted the Interim Constitution. In Stage 2, a democratically elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution after the first democratic election.

This solved the legitimacy problem. Unelected negotiators could create a temporary legal bridge, while the permanent Constitution had to be drafted by elected representatives.

F. Interim Constitution as bridge

Legal-continuity point: the Interim Constitution, the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 200 of 1993, was adopted through the procedures of the old legal order. This is the paradox of the transition: legal continuity was used to create constitutional rupture.

The Interim Constitution was finalised by the unelected MPNF before the first democratic election, adopted late in 1993, became binding after 27 April 1994 and was adopted through the procedures of the 1983 Constitution.

Its key features included the Government of National Unity, a justiciable Bill of Rights, a new Constitutional Court, the two-stage transition and the 34 Constitutional Principles.

The Interim Constitution was a bridge because it used legal continuity to create constitutional rupture. It moved South Africa from apartheid’s culture of authority to a culture of justification.

G. Constitutional Principles and certification

Statutory map: s 68(1) established the Constitutional Assembly as the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly; s 71(1) required compliance with the 34 Constitutional Principles; s 71(2) stated that the text had no force or effect unless certified by the Constitutional Court; and s 71(3) made that certification final and binding.

The 34 Constitutional Principles were binding benchmarks and a solemn pact for the Final Constitution. They included constitutional democracy based on constitutional supremacy, universal suffrage, regular elections, separation of powers, a justiciable Bill of Rights, multi-sphere government, proportional representation, a common voters’ roll, traditional leadership and special amendment procedures.

Under the Interim Constitution, the National Assembly and Senate sitting jointly formed the Constitutional Assembly, which had to adopt the new text within two years and by a two-thirds majority. The new text had no force unless certified by the Constitutional Court as complying with the Principles.

Certification therefore transformed political compromise into legal control.

H. First and Second Certification Judgments

Exam warning: the First Certification Judgment did not reject the first text because of socio-economic rights or flexible separation of powers. The Court accepted both. Non-certification resulted from other defects, including amendment/entrenchment problems, institutional independence issues, local government/provincial powers concerns and attempts to immunise ordinary legislation from constitutional review.

The First Certification Judgment showed that the Constitutional Principles were real. The first text was not certified because some provisions failed to comply with the Principles. The Court rejected objections to socio-economic rights and strict separation of powers, but found defects relating to constitutional review, amendment procedures, Bill of Rights entrenchment, institutional independence, local government and provincial powers.

The Second Certification Judgment was delivered on 4 December 1996. The Constitutional Court unanimously certified that the Amended Text complied with the 34 Principles. This was the final legal hurdle for South Africa’s transition to full democracy.

I. Final Constitution

Citation and timing precision: the Final Constitution was historically enacted as Act 108 of 1996, but is now cited as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996. It was signed by President Mandela on 10 December 1996 and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

The Final Constitution was formally enacted as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa Act 108 of 1996, is now referred to as the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996, was drafted by the Constitutional Assembly, certified by the Constitutional Court and took effect on 4 February 1997, replacing the Interim Constitution.

Its key features include a justiciable Bill of Rights, universal adult suffrage, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law, non-racialism and non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages.

J. Constitutional amendments and the living Constitution

The Constitution is supreme but not frozen. Amendments are used to update, refine and strengthen the supreme law and align it with changing societal needs and norms. Examples include the 1st Amendment linked to TRC amnesty cut-off dates, the 12th Amendment altering provincial boundaries, the 17th Amendment overhauling the court system and the 18th Amendment recognising South African Sign Language as the 12th official language.

This shows that the Constitution is living, but amendment remains legally controlled through special procedures.

K. Critical evaluation: was it truly revolutionary?

Mureinik/Langa distinction: Mureinik’s “culture of authority to culture of justification” explains the Interim Constitution’s bridge function. Langa’s later transformative constitutionalism develops the idea in relation to the 1996 Constitution’s broader egalitarian project. Keep them linked, but not conflated.

The transition was revolutionary in substance because it changed the source of law, expanded citizenship, entrenched rights and subjected public power to constitutional control. It replaced parliamentary sovereignty with constitutional supremacy.

But it was negotiated and legally structured in form. The old legal order was used to adopt the Interim Constitution, compromise was necessary, and minority safeguards shaped the Final Constitution. This creates the central Theme 3 conclusion:

South Africa did not have a clean break from law; it used law to break from apartheid legality.

L. Conclusion

South Africa’s transition was a negotiated constitutional revolution. It began with the failure of parliamentary sovereignty and apartheid legality, was challenged by decades of liberation constitutional vision, was negotiated through CODESA and the MPNF, was legally bridged by the Interim Constitution, was controlled by the Constitutional Principles and certification, and culminated in the 1996 Final Constitution. The result was a supreme, democratic, rights-based and transformative constitutional order.

5. 5-Mark Version

It was revolutionary because it replaced parliamentary sovereignty, racial exclusion and apartheid legality with constitutional supremacy, universal adult suffrage, a justiciable Bill of Rights, separation of powers and democratic accountability.

The two-stage transition was central. Unelected negotiators drafted the Interim Constitution, while the elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution. The Final Constitution had no force unless certified by the Constitutional Court as complying with the 34 Constitutional Principles.

6. 10-Mark Version

South Africa’s transition is best described as a negotiated constitutional revolution. It was negotiated because the new order emerged through compromise, legal continuity and certification. It was revolutionary because it fundamentally changed the source and limits of public power.

Under apartheid parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was supreme and courts could not invalidate legislation simply because it was unjust. This exposed the weakness of formal legality. The liberation movement then developed a constitutional vision through the Defiance Campaign, Freedom Charter, Sharpeville, Rivonia and ANC Constitutional Guidelines.

CODESA exposed the legitimacy problem: unelected negotiators lacked a democratic mandate to write a permanent Constitution. The two-stage transition solved this. Unelected negotiators drafted the Interim Constitution, while the elected Constitutional Assembly drafted the Final Constitution.

The First Certification Judgment refused certification of the first text, while the Second Certification Judgment certified the Amended Text on 4 December 1996. Mandela signed the Constitution on 10 December 1996, and it took effect on 4 February 1997.

The Final Constitution entrenched universal suffrage, constitutional supremacy, a justiciable Bill of Rights, rule of law, non-racialism, non-sexism, cooperative government, separation of powers, Chapter 9 institutions, traditional leaders and languages.

5. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Constitutionalism means that government power is derived from and limited by a constitution. In Theme 4, constitutionalism is described as requiring a constitution to both structure and constrain state power: it creates government institutions and gives them powers, but also limits those powers to prevent abuse.

In South Africa, constitutionalism reflects the move from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect; the 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened this shift. Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was treated as the highest legal authority, and courts generally had no power to invalidate legislation for substantive injustice, although they could enforce procedural or manner-and-form limits where applicable. Under constitutional supremacy, the Constitution is the highest law. Section 1(c) makes constitutional supremacy and the rule of law founding values, and section 2 provides that law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution is invalid. Courts may enforce this through judicial review under section 172. Therefore, all public power, including Parliament and the executive, must comply with the Constitution.

Mark focus: definition + old system + new system + ss 1(c), 2, 172.

2. Core Thesis

South Africa's move from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy marks the central break from apartheid legality: public power is no longer valid merely because Parliament authorises it, but only if it complies with the supreme, justiciable, rights-based and value-based Constitution.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: the big idea

South African constitutionalism must be understood as a response to the abuse of law under colonialism and apartheid. Under the old order, Parliament was treated as the highest legal authority, which meant that oppressive laws could be valid even when they were deeply unjust. The 1993 Interim Constitution formally abolished parliamentary sovereignty in South Africa for the first time, and the 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened constitutional supremacy. The key point is that public power is now only valid if it is authorised by, and consistent with, the Constitution.

The lecturer's feedback makes this historical shift important: parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect, and the 1996 Final Constitution consolidated that break. Students must understand the move from sovereignty to supremacy rather than merely memorising labels.

B. Definition of constitutionalism

Constitutionalism is the principle that governmental authority is derived from, structured by and limited by a constitution. In Theme 4, constitutionalism is described as the theory of constitutional law and as the philosophy that rulers must act according to binding constitutional boundaries rather than personal will.

The most important phrase to memorise is:

Constitutionalism means the Constitution must both structure and constrain state power.

This means two things:

The Constitution must structure power by creating institutions such as Parliament, the executive, the courts, provinces, municipalities and Chapter 9 institutions.

It must constrain power by limiting what those institutions may do, subjecting them to rights, legality, accountability and judicial review.

The Theme 4 slides expressly state that constitutionalism requires a constitution to establish institutions and allocate powers for effective government, while also limiting and dispersing power to prevent abuse.

C. Characteristics of constitutionalism

A strong answer should not stop at "constitutionalism limits government". It should explain how.

Constitutionalism includes:

FeatureExplanationInstitutionsIt identifies and establishes structures of government.Distribution of powerIt legally allocates power to those institutions.Relationship between institutionsIt regulates how branches and spheres interact.Binding rulesIt creates binding legal rules for government and the governed.Limits on public powerIt prevents state power from becoming arbitrary or abusive.Rights and accountabilityIt links government power to rights, values and constitutional duties.

D. Descriptive vs prescriptive constitutionalism

This is a very useful 80%+ distinction.

Descriptive constitutionalism merely describes how government institutions are arranged. It focuses on form: what institutions exist, how they interact, and how power is distributed. The problem is that a state may have a constitution in this descriptive sense but still be oppressive. This is why colonial and apartheid South Africa can be discussed as having constitutional arrangements in a descriptive sense, but not true democratic constitutionalism in the stronger sense. Theme 4 describes descriptive constitutionalism as a factual account of structures and institutions without reference to normative values.

Prescriptive constitutionalism asks whether the system complies with constitutional principles and values. It is not satisfied by the mere existence of a constitution. It asks whether power is limited by constitutional supremacy, rule of law, separation of powers, democracy, human rights, judicial independence and transformation. Theme 4 expressly states that this is the approach taken by democratic South Africa, and it identifies constitutional supremacy, limited government, rule of law, separation of powers, democratic self-government, human rights, an independent judiciary and transformative constitutionalism as key aspects of prescriptive constitutionalism.

Exam sentence: South Africa's 1996 Constitution reflects prescriptive constitutionalism because it does not merely organise public power; it evaluates and limits public power according to supreme constitutional values, rights and institutional checks.

E. Parliamentary sovereignty: the old order

Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was the highest legal authority. Courts could interpret legislation, but they generally could not invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive injustice simply because legislation was oppressive or contrary to fundamental rights. However, where applicable, courts could still enforce procedural or 'manner and form' requirements, such as special procedures attached to entrenched provisions. This mattered in apartheid South Africa because Parliament could use ordinary law to entrench racial domination.

The A1 feedback on the Westminster system is important here: the South Africa Act's Westminster model did not treat all citizens equally; it favoured wealthy white males and excluded Black and African voices from the legislature.

The key criticism is not simply that Parliament had power. The key criticism is that Parliament had power without sufficient constitutional limits, and the majority of people were excluded from the democratic process that created those laws.

F. Constitutional supremacy: the new order

Constitutional supremacy means the Constitution is the highest law of the Republic. Section 1(c) makes supremacy of the Constitution and the rule of law a founding value, while section 2 gives the doctrine legal force by providing that law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution is invalid and constitutional obligations must be fulfilled.

Theme 4 identifies constitutional supremacy as the most definitive feature of South Africa's post-apartheid constitutional system and as the foundation of the democratic constitutional dispensation. It is both a founding value in section 1(c) and a binding rule in section 2.

This means Parliament is no longer legally unlimited. The executive, legislature, judiciary, provinces, municipalities and all organs of state must act within the Constitution. Even democratically supported law may be invalid if it violates the Constitution.

G. Judicial review: making supremacy real

Constitutional supremacy would be weak if no institution could enforce it. That is why judicial review is essential. Judicial review is the power of courts to test law or conduct against the Constitution and declare it invalid if it is inconsistent. Section 172 provides that courts must declare any law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution invalid to the extent of the inconsistency.

Theme 4 states that constitutional supremacy renders the Constitution justiciable: any law or conduct can be tested against constitutional provisions and declared unconstitutional and invalid if it fails to comply. This is the practical difference between old and new South Africa. Under parliamentary sovereignty, courts were often limited to procedural review. Under constitutional supremacy, courts may review the constitutional validity of law and conduct, including whether they are substantively consistent with constitutional rights, values and obligations.

H. Constitutional supremacy vs parliamentary sovereignty

IssueParliamentary sovereigntyConstitutional supremacyHighest authorityParliamentConstitutionRole of courtsCourts interpret law but generally cannot invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive injustice, although manner-and-form limits may be enforced where applicableCourts may declare inconsistent law or conduct invalidRights protectionRights depend mainly on ordinary legislation and political willRights are entrenched and justiciableHistorical associationWestminster-style colonial/apartheid orderPost-apartheid democratic constitutional orderRiskParliament can legalise oppressionCourts may invalidate democratically supported laws, creating counter-majoritarian tensionKey sectionsNo equivalent supremacy clauseConstitution ss 1(c), 2 and 172

I. Does constitutional supremacy undermine democracy?

A strong critical answer must deal with this. Constitutional supremacy does create a counter-majoritarian difficulty because unelected judges may declare laws supported by elected representatives invalid. Theme 4's separation of powers lecture notes this problem: judicial review under section 172 may require declarations of invalidity and remedies, meaning judges can affect laws supported by a majority.

But in South Africa, judicial review is usually justified as part of constitutional democracy rather than as a rejection of democracy. Majority rule is not the same as unlimited power. The Constitution itself is democratic: it protects universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. At the same time, it prevents majorities from destroying dignity, equality, freedom and rights.

The correct argument is: Constitutional supremacy does not replace democracy; it constitutionalises democracy. It ensures that democracy operates within the limits of the Constitution.

J. Case plug-in: EFF v Speaker

In Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action against President Zuma was binding unless set aside by a court. The President's failure to comply was inconsistent with section 83(b), read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and invalid. The National Assembly's resolution absolving the President was also inconsistent with sections 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside. Note: section 96 forms part of the broader ethical-obligations context in EFF, but the Constitutional Court's final order found the President's failure inconsistent with section 83(b), read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c).

The Court linked the case to the need to break from unchecked abuse of state power and resources, and emphasised accountability, rule of law and constitutional supremacy as foundational values.

Exam use: EFF shows that constitutional supremacy is not abstract. Even the President and National Assembly must comply with constitutional obligations, and unconstitutional conduct may be declared invalid.

K. Conclusion

South African constitutionalism is best understood as a move from a system in which Parliament could authorise oppression to a system in which all public power must be justified under the Constitution. Constitutional supremacy, section 2, judicial review and the Bill of Rights ensure that law and conduct are no longer valid merely because they come from powerful institutions. They must comply with the supreme Constitution. This is why constitutionalism in South Africa is not only structural, but also prescriptive, value-based and transformative.

In Theme 4, constitutionalism is described as requiring a constitution to both structure and constrain state power: it creates government institutions and gives them powers, but also limits those powers to prevent abuse.

In South Africa, constitutionalism reflects the move from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect; the 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened this shift. Under parliamentary sovereignty, Parliament was treated as the highest legal authority, and courts generally had no power to invalidate legislation for substantive injustice, although they could enforce procedural or manner-and-form limits where applicable. Under constitutional supremacy, the Constitution is the highest law. Section 1(c) makes constitutional supremacy and the rule of law founding values, and section 2 provides that law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution is invalid. Courts may enforce this through judicial review under section 172. Therefore, all public power, including Parliament and the executive, must comply with the Constitution.

6. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

South African constitutionalism must be understood against the historical background of apartheid parliamentary sovereignty. Under the old order, Parliament was treated as the highest legal authority, and courts were generally unable to invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive injustice simply because they were unjust or violated fundamental rights, although procedural or manner-and-form limits could still be enforced where applicable. This allowed law to be used as an instrument of racial domination, especially because the Westminster-style system excluded the majority of South Africans from meaningful political power. The A1 feedback stresses that the old Westminster system did not operate equally and favoured a narrow white minority.

Constitutionalism means that government authority is derived from and limited by a constitution. Theme 4 explains that a constitution must both structure and constrain state power: it must establish institutions and allocate powers, but also limit and disperse power to prevent abuse.

Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect, and the 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened constitutional supremacy. Section 1(c) identifies supremacy of the Constitution and the rule of law as founding values, while section 2 gives this legal force by providing that inconsistent law or conduct is invalid and constitutional obligations must be fulfilled. Theme 4 describes constitutional supremacy as the definitive feature of South Africa's post-apartheid constitutional order.

7. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

South Africa's move from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy is one of the central breaks from the apartheid legal order. Under apartheid, oppressive laws could be legally valid because Parliament was treated as the highest authority. Parliamentary sovereignty was formally abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect. The 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened this by making the Constitution supreme and justiciable. The result is that law and public power are no longer valid merely because they are authorised by Parliament; they must comply with the Constitution.

Constitutionalism means that government authority is derived from and limited by a constitution. Theme 4 describes constitutionalism as the theory of constitutional law and the doctrine that rulers must act according to binding constitutional boundaries rather than personal will. The key point is that a constitution must both structure and constrain state power: it establishes institutions and allocates powers for effective government, but also limits and disperses power to prevent abuse.

This also requires distinguishing between descriptive and prescriptive constitutionalism. Descriptive constitutionalism merely describes state institutions and how they function. By contrast, prescriptive constitutionalism evaluates whether the system complies with constitutional values and principles. Democratic South Africa adopts prescriptive constitutionalism because its Constitution is based on constitutional supremacy, limited government, rule of law, separation of powers, democratic self-government, protection of human rights, an independent judiciary and transformative constitutionalism.

The old order was based on parliamentary sovereignty. In that system, courts could not generally invalidate Acts of Parliament for substantive injustice merely because they were unjust, although procedural or manner-and-form requirements could still be enforced where applicable. This was dangerous in South Africa because the political system itself excluded most South Africans. The A1 feedback emphasises that the Westminster system under the South Africa Act did not treat all citizens equally and marginalised Black and African voices from the legislature.

The new order is based on constitutional supremacy. Section 1(c) identifies constitutional supremacy and the rule of law as founding values. Section 2 gives supremacy direct legal force by providing that the Constitution is supreme, inconsistent law or conduct is invalid, and constitutional obligations must be fulfilled. This places the Constitution above Parliament, the executive and all organs of state. Theme 4 describes constitutional supremacy as the definitive feature of South Africa's post-apartheid constitutional system.

The practical mechanism of constitutional supremacy is judicial review. Section 172 allows courts to declare unconstitutional law or conduct invalid to the extent of its inconsistency. This means that courts may test legislation, executive conduct and other public power against the Constitution. Without judicial review, constitutional supremacy would risk becoming symbolic. With judicial review, the Constitution becomes enforceable.

There is, however, a critical tension. Judicial review may create a counter-majoritarian difficulty because unelected judges can invalidate laws supported by elected representatives. Theme 4's separation of powers lecture recognises that judicial review may require declarations of invalidity and remedies, and judges can declare law supported by a majority invalid even though they are not elected representatives.

However, this does not mean constitutional supremacy is anti-democratic. South Africa is a constitutional democracy, not a system of unlimited majority rule. The Constitution itself protects democracy through universal adult suffrage, regular elections, a common voters' roll, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Constitutional supremacy ensures that democratic power remains consistent with dignity, equality, freedom and rights.

The case of Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC) illustrates the practical force of constitutional supremacy. The Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action against President Zuma was binding unless set aside by a court, that the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with section 83(b) read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and that the National Assembly's resolution absolving him was inconsistent with sections 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside. Note: section 96 forms part of the broader ethical-obligations context in EFF, but the Constitutional Court's final order found the President's failure inconsistent with section 83(b), read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c). The case shows that even the President and National Assembly must comply with constitutional obligations, and that constitutional supremacy, rule of law and accountability prevent impunity.

In conclusion, South African constitutionalism is not satisfied by the existence of a constitution alone. It requires a supreme, rights-based, value-based and enforceable constitutional order. The shift from parliamentary sovereignty to constitutional supremacy ensures that all public power is subject to constitutional control. This is the foundation of South Africa's post-apartheid constitutional democracy.

8. Common Mistakes to Avoid

New order = constitutional supremacy — confirmed and deepened by 1996 Constitution.

Section 2 = inconsistent law/conduct invalid.

Judicial review makes supremacy real.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Transformative constitutionalism means that the Constitution is a long-term project aimed at changing South Africa from an unjust colonial and apartheid past into a democratic society based on dignity, equality, freedom and social justice. Karl Klare defines it as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

The Constitution is transformative because the Preamble recognises past injustices and commits South Africa to healing divisions, while section 1 protects founding values such as dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) also confirms that the Bill of Rights affirms the values of dignity, equality and freedom. Transformation therefore requires more than formal equality; it requires substantive change in law, society, institutions and power relations.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Transformative constitutionalism is the idea that the Constitution is not only a legal document, but a long-term project of social, political, economic and legal change. Karl Klare's definition, used in Theme 4, describes it as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

A 90% answer must not only praise transformative constitutionalism. It must also evaluate it.

1. Persistent inequality and slow pace of change

One major criticism is that large-scale social and economic transformation remains unfulfilled despite the transformative constitutional framework. Theme 4 identifies persistent racialised poverty and inequality as evidence critics use to argue that the project has failed to meet popular expectations. Critics also argue that transformative constitutionalism cannot solve deep structural problems such as land reform.

Evaluation: This criticism is powerful, but it should not lead to the conclusion that the Constitution itself is meaningless. It may show that constitutional ideals require stronger implementation, political will and institutional capacity.

2. Too court-centric

Another criticism is that transformative constitutionalism places too much weight on courts and judges. Theme 4 notes that critics argue the project relies too heavily on the judiciary to achieve social change, placing expectations on courts that cannot be met through adjudication alone. S Sibanda argues that transformative constitutionalism has "not translated into much more than rights or law-based approaches to driving change, with little evidence of other work within the discourse directed at inculcating social, economic or cultural institutional or structural power shifts or innovations beyond the courts" ('Not Yet Uhuru' (2018) 224–225). He contends that TC as a strictly legal and court-centred concept is unable to solve complex questions about structural inequality, such as those raised during the #FeesMustFall protests.

Evaluation: Courts are important because they enforce rights and constitutional obligations, but transformation cannot be achieved only through litigation. Parliament, the executive, local government, civil society and private actors must also drive change.

3. The liberal compromise

A more radical criticism is that the South African constitutional settlement represents an unacceptable compromise that preserves the apartheid-era socio-economic order rather than achieving a true structural break. From a decolonial perspective, some argue that transformative constitutionalism may still be rooted in Western legal traditions and may not adequately respond to the need to decolonise law, institutions and pedagogy.

Transformative Constitutionalism: From an Unjust Past to a Just Future

2. Core Thesis

Transformative constitutionalism means that the Constitution is not merely a legal document that limits government power; it is a long-term project of legal, political, social and economic change aimed at moving South Africa from colonial and apartheid injustice towards a democratic society based on dignity, equality, freedom, participation and substantive justice.
