4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: what is transformative constitutionalism?

Transformative constitutionalism is the idea that the Constitution is not only a supreme legal framework, but also a project of deep social change. It responds to the fact that colonialism and apartheid did not merely create bad laws; they created structural inequality, racial exclusion, economic disadvantage, symbolic domination and a legal culture that often demanded obedience without justification. The 1996 Constitution therefore provides a framework and normative direction for transforming society, not merely a different set of legal rules.

Theme 4 describes the 1996 Constitution as inherently transformative because its central aim is to move from an unjust past to a just future, and because the founding values in section 1 confirm the intention to break from the past.

B. Definition: Karl Klare's long-term project

The most important definition comes from Karl Klare. In Theme 4, transformative constitutionalism is defined as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

This definition has several parts:

Part of Klare definitionMeaning in an exam answerLong-term projectTransformation will not happen overnight. It is a continuing constitutional process.Constitutional enactmentThe constitutional text itself is part of the project of change.InterpretationCourts and legal actors must interpret law in light of constitutional values.EnforcementRights and duties must become practical, not merely symbolic.Historical contextTransformation must be understood against colonialism, apartheid and structural inequality.Political and social institutionsTransformation is not only about courts; it includes Parliament, the executive, universities, private actors and society.Power relationshipsThe Constitution aims to change unequal power relations created by the past.Democratic, participatory and egalitarian directionTransformation must deepen democracy, participation and equality.

Exam sentence: Transformative constitutionalism is therefore not a once-off legal event; it is a continuing project of changing institutions, legal interpretation and social power relations in line with constitutional values.

C. Why the Constitution is transformative

The Constitution is transformative because it was adopted after a deeply unequal and violent history. Apartheid law did not only discriminate formally; it organised land, labour, movement, citizenship, political power, public space and social recognition around racial hierarchy. The Constitution therefore had to do more than create elections. It had to build a new society based on dignity, equality, freedom, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability and social justice.

Section 1 is central here. It entrenches values such as human dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal suffrage, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Theme 4 specifically teaches that transformation is foundational to constitutional interpretation because section 1 values confirm the intention to break from the past. Section 7(1) also matters because it says the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy and affirms the democratic values of human dignity, equality and freedom.

D. The bridge metaphor: Mureinik and Langa

One of the best ways to explain transformative constitutionalism is through the bridge metaphor.

Mureinik explained the Interim Constitution as a bridge away from a culture of authority towards a culture of justification. Under apartheid's culture of authority, law often operated on the basis that Parliament had spoken and people had to obey. Because Parliament was elected only by a minority, the law did not need to justify itself to most of the people governed by it. A culture of justification is the opposite. It requires every exercise of public power to be justified with reasons, rights and values. Mureinik argued that the Bill of Rights is central to this shift because it gives people standards by which they can demand justification from those who govern them.

It is important to note that Mureinik wrote in the context of the Interim Constitution and the Interim Bill of Rights. His argument was about the rights framework as a tool for demanding justification of public power.

Langa CJ develops a related but distinct idea. Writing in the transformative constitutionalism context, Langa argues that under a transformative constitution, judges bear the ultimate responsibility to justify their decisions not only by reference to authority, but also by reference to constitutional ideas and values. He states that it is no longer sufficient for judges to rely on the say-so of Parliament or technical readings of legislation. He also sees the Constitution as a bridge from a deeply divided past to a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and opportunities for all. For Langa, the core idea of transformative constitutionalism is that South Africa must change.

The culture of justification, as Langa develops it, therefore goes beyond the rights framework: it requires a change in legal culture itself, so that legal reasoning becomes value-driven, not merely authority-driven.

Exam sentence: The bridge metaphor shows that the Constitution is both backward-looking and forward-looking: it recognises the injustice of the past, but also commits South Africa to a future based on dignity, equality, freedom and substantive justice.

E. Substantive equality: transformation is more than formal equality

A major feature of transformative constitutionalism is substantive equality. Formal equality means treating people the same on paper. Substantive equality asks whether people can actually enjoy rights and opportunities in real life, given historical disadvantage and material inequality.

This distinction is crucial in South Africa because apartheid produced systemic inequality. Treating everyone "the same" after apartheid would often preserve the unequal effects of the past. Transformative constitutionalism therefore supports measures that address structural inequality, including affirmative action, socio-economic rights, land reform and broader redistributive justice.

Langa explains that transformative constitutionalism includes the establishment of a truly equal society and the provision of basic socio-economic rights to all. He links transformation to access to housing, food, water, healthcare, electricity, education and opportunities.

Exam sentence: Transformative constitutionalism is therefore not satisfied by formal legal equality; it requires substantive equality that confronts the social and economic effects of colonialism and apartheid.

F. Socio-economic rights and transformation

Transformative constitutionalism is closely linked to socio-economic rights because apartheid created material deprivation, not only political exclusion. The Constitution therefore protects rights such as access to housing in section 26, healthcare, food, water and social security in section 27, and education in section 29.

The case of Soobramoney v Minister of Health (KwaZulu-Natal) 1998 (1) SA 765 (CC) is important because the Court recognised that South Africa inherited deep poverty, unemployment, inadequate social security, and lack of access to clean water and healthcare. Theme 4 uses Soobramoney to show that the Constitution's commitment to transform society into one based on dignity, freedom and equality lies at the heart of the new constitutional order.

However, Soobramoney also shows the limits of courts. The applicant, who had chronic renal failure and needed dialysis, did not succeed because the hospital's criteria for rationing a scarce resource were found to be reasonable. This makes the case useful for both explaining transformation and criticising the limits of legal enforcement.

G. Transformation of legal culture: from authority to justification

Transformative constitutionalism does not only require changing material conditions. It also requires changing legal culture. Under a culture of authority, legal actors rely on status, hierarchy or formal authority. Under a culture of justification, legal decisions must be defended through constitutional reasons.

Langa states that it is no longer enough for judges to rely only on Parliament's say-so or technical readings of legislation. Under a transformative Constitution, judges must justify decisions by reference to both authority and constitutional ideas and values. This links directly to section 39. Courts must interpret the Bill of Rights, legislation, common law and customary law in a way that promotes the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. That means interpretation is not mechanical. It must be value-based and transformation-conscious.

Exam sentence: Transformative constitutionalism changes the legal question from "is there legal authority?" to "can the exercise of power be justified in terms of constitutional rights, values and purpose?"

H. Transformative constitutionalism and symbolic redress: City of Tshwane

City of Tshwane Metropolitan Municipality v Afriforum 2016 (6) SA 279 (CC) is a useful case if the question asks about transformation, the Preamble, belonging, public symbols, ubuntu or historical redress.

The case concerned the City's decision to change old street names in Pretoria. Afriforum argued that the removal of the old names harmed Afrikaner culture, heritage and belonging. The Constitutional Court placed the dispute in historical context, explaining that colonialism and apartheid privileged white history and largely excluded Black leaders, institutions and histories from public recognition.

The Court treated the Preamble as important because it recognises past injustices, commits South Africa to healing divisions, and affirms that South Africa belongs to all who live in it, united in diversity. Mogoeng CJ held that the constitutional project requires moving from exclusivity and racial domination towards inclusivity, social cohesion and equitable access to opportunities. The Court also invoked ubuntu, explaining that belonging is relational and requires appreciation, accommodation and respect for others.

Exam use: City of Tshwane shows that transformation is not only economic or legal. It is also symbolic and cultural. Public spaces, names and symbols can either reproduce exclusion or help create constitutional belonging.

I. Criticisms of transformative constitutionalism

A 90% answer must not only praise transformative constitutionalism. It must also evaluate it.

1. Persistent inequality and slow pace of change

One major criticism is that large-scale social and economic transformation remains unfulfilled despite the transformative constitutional framework. Theme 4 identifies persistent racialised poverty and inequality as evidence critics use to argue that the project has failed to meet popular expectations. Critics also argue that transformative constitutionalism cannot solve deep structural problems such as land reform.

Evaluation: This criticism is powerful, but it should not lead to the conclusion that the Constitution itself is meaningless. It may show that constitutional ideals require stronger implementation, political will and institutional capacity.

2. Too court-centric

Another criticism is that transformative constitutionalism places too much weight on courts and judges. Theme 4 notes that critics argue the project relies too heavily on the judiciary to achieve social change, placing expectations on courts that cannot be met through adjudication alone. S Sibanda argues that transformative constitutionalism has "not translated into much more than rights or law-based approaches to driving change, with little evidence of other work within the discourse directed at inculcating social, economic or cultural institutional or structural power shifts or innovations beyond the courts" ('Not Yet Uhuru' (2018) 224–225). He contends that TC as a strictly legal and court-centred concept is unable to solve complex questions about structural inequality, such as those raised during the #FeesMustFall protests.

Evaluation: Courts are important because they enforce rights and constitutional obligations, but transformation cannot be achieved only through litigation. Parliament, the executive, local government, civil society and private actors must also drive change.

3. The liberal compromise

A more radical criticism is that the South African constitutional settlement represents an unacceptable compromise that preserves the apartheid-era socio-economic order rather than achieving a true structural break. From a decolonial perspective, some argue that transformative constitutionalism may still be rooted in Western legal traditions and may not adequately respond to the need to decolonise law, institutions and pedagogy.

Evaluation: This critique highlights the limits of law-based transformation, but it does not negate the value of constitutional rights protection. The question is how transformation goes beyond courts and legislation.

4. Constitutional fetishism

Some critics identify "constitutional fetishism" or "constitutional worship": treating the Constitution as if it were a quasi-religious document, with its own foundational theology (constitutional values), priests (judges) and legal protocols. The concern is that treating the Constitution as the exclusive horizon for social and political change may silence alternative political and radical ideas for transformation.

Evaluation: This critique warns against intellectual closure, but it does not mean the Constitution is unimportant. It means constitutional ideas must be engaged critically, not treated as beyond question.

5. Counter-majoritarian concern

Transformative constitutionalism can raise a counter-majoritarian issue where unelected judges invalidate laws or government conduct adopted by elected representatives. Theme 4 links this criticism to the tension between democratic majority rule and judicial review.

Evaluation: In South Africa, this concern is answered by the idea of constitutional democracy. Courts do not replace democracy; they enforce the democratic Constitution. But courts must still respect separation of powers and institutional limits.

6. The limits of judicial law-making

Langa himself recognises that judges do not have a free hand to decide what the law means. The text of the Constitution still matters, and courts must avoid becoming legislators. He warns that the difficulty lies in finding the line between transformation and legislation.

Evaluation: This is the best balanced critique: transformation requires active interpretation, but not unlimited judicial power.

5. Case-Law Blocks

Case 1: Soobramoney v Minister of Health (KwaZulu-Natal) 1998 (1) SA 765 (CC)

Transformative constitutionalism means that the Constitution is a long-term project aimed at changing South Africa from an unjust colonial and apartheid past into a democratic society based on dignity, equality, freedom and social justice. Karl Klare defines it as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

The Constitution is transformative because the Preamble recognises past injustices and commits South Africa to healing divisions, while section 1 protects founding values such as dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. 7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Transformative constitutionalism is the idea that the Constitution is not only a legal document, but a long-term project of social, political, economic and legal change. Karl Klare's definition, used in Theme 4, describes it as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

The 1996 Constitution is transformative because it responds to colonialism and apartheid. The Preamble recognises the injustices of the past and commits South Africa to healing divisions and building a society based on democratic values, social justice and fundamental human rights. Section 1 entrenches values such as dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) affirms that the Bill of Rights is a cornerstone of democracy and reflects dignity, equality and freedom.

Mureinik, writing in the Interim Constitution context, explained the shift as a movement from a culture of authority to a culture of justification: public power must be justified by reference to rights and values, not merely because Parliament said so. Langa CJ later developed this idea in the transformative constitutionalism context, arguing that judges must justify their decisions by reference to constitutional values and ideas, not only authority. He describes the Constitution as a bridge from a deeply divided past to a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and opportunities for all.

Transformative constitutionalism also requires substantive equality. It is not enough to treat everyone the same on paper, because apartheid created structural inequality. Langa explains that transformation requires a truly equal society and the provision of basic socio-economic rights to all.

However, transformative constitutionalism faces criticism. S Sibanda argues it has not produced structural change beyond the courts. Critics also point to the slow pace of social and economic change, persistent inequality, land reform problems and overreliance on courts. Still, the Constitution provides the normative framework for transformation, while implementation must come from courts, Parliament, the executive, civil society and ordinary people.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

Transformative constitutionalism is one of the central ideas of South Africa's post-apartheid constitutional order. It means that the Constitution is not merely a legal document that organises government and limits power, but a long-term project of changing South Africa's political, social, economic and legal order. It must be understood against colonialism and apartheid, which created racial exclusion, political domination, material inequality and a legal culture of obedience. The 1996 Constitution therefore aims to move South Africa from an unjust past to a just future.

Karl Klare's definition is the best starting point. Theme 4 defines transformative constitutionalism as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction. This shows that transformation is not immediate, court-only, or purely symbolic. It involves the constitutional text, interpretation, enforcement, institutions, society and power relations.

The text of the Constitution supports this. The Preamble recognises the injustices of the past, honours those who suffered for justice and freedom, affirms that South Africa belongs to all who live in it, and commits the country to healing divisions and establishing a society based on democratic values, social justice and human rights. Section 1 protects dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) describes the Bill of Rights as the cornerstone of democracy and affirms dignity, equality and freedom. These provisions show that transformation is built into the Constitution's identity.

Mureinik, writing in the context of the Interim Constitution and Interim Bill of Rights, explained the shift from a culture of authority to a culture of justification: under apartheid, Parliament's word was treated as law without needing to justify itself to most of the people governed by it. The new constitutional order must require every exercise of public power to be justified through reasons, rights and values. Langa CJ later developed this idea further in the transformative constitutionalism context: he argues that under a transformative constitution, judges must justify decisions not only by reference to authority, but also by reference to constitutional ideas and values. For Langa, transformation also requires a changed legal culture, not just changed laws. He describes the Constitution as a bridge from a deeply divided past to a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and opportunities for all.

The case of Soobramoney v Minister of Health (KwaZulu-Natal) 1998 (1) SA 765 (CC) shows the socio-economic dimension of transformation. The applicant had chronic renal failure and required dialysis but was excluded under the hospital's rationing criteria. Theme 4 uses Soobramoney to show that South Africa inherited poverty, unemployment, inadequate social security and lack of access to water and healthcare, and that the commitment to address these conditions lies at the heart of the new constitutional order. The case is also useful critically because the applicant did not succeed, showing that courts may recognise transformative commitments but remain limited by resource constraints and institutional competence.

The case of City of Tshwane Metropolitan Municipality v Afriforum 2016 (6) SA 279 (CC) shows the symbolic and cultural dimension of transformation. The Court placed the street-name dispute in historical context, explaining that colonialism and apartheid gave public honour mainly to white history while excluding Black leaders, institutions and histories. It emphasised the Preamble, ubuntu, belonging, inclusivity and the duty to transform public space. This shows that transformation is not only about money or formal rights; it also concerns memory, recognition and belonging.

However, transformative constitutionalism faces serious criticism. S Sibanda argues that it has "not translated into much more than rights or law-based approaches to driving change, with little evidence of other work within the discourse directed at inculcating social, economic or cultural institutional or structural power shifts or innovations beyond the courts" ('Not Yet Uhuru' (2018) 224–225). This critique captures the broader concern that TC as a court-centred concept is unable to solve complex questions of structural inequality. A related radical critique — the liberal compromise — argues that the constitutional settlement preserves the apartheid-era socio-economic order rather than achieving a true structural break, and that from a decolonial perspective, TC may remain rooted in Western legal traditions. Some critics also identify "constitutional fetishism": treating the Constitution as the exclusive horizon for change, which may silence alternative political and radical ideas. Furthermore, transformation raises counter-majoritarian concerns because unelected judges may invalidate laws or decisions taken by elected representatives. Finally, Langa warns that judges do not have unlimited power to make the law mean whatever they want; the line between transformation and legislation must be respected.

Despite these criticisms, transformative constitutionalism remains essential to South African constitutional law. It does not claim that courts or legal texts alone can fix society. Rather, it provides the constitutional direction for change and requires all institutions and people to justify power, promote substantive equality and dismantle the continuing effects of the past. In conclusion, the Constitution is transformative because it demands not only a new legal order, but a new society based on dignity, equality, freedom, participation, accountability and substantive justice.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Value-based constitutionalism means that the Constitution is not only a set of legal rules, but a normative order based on foundational values. Section 1 lists values such as human dignity, achievement of equality, advancement of human rights and freedoms, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness.

These values guide how law is interpreted and limited. Section 7(1) says the Bill of Rights affirms the democratic values of dignity, equality and freedom. Section 36 requires rights limitations to be reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom. Section 39 requires courts to interpret rights, legislation, common law and customary law in a way that promotes constitutional values. Therefore, South African law is not mechanical or morally neutral; it must be justified through constitutional values.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Value-based constitutionalism means that the Constitution is a supreme normative legal order based on foundational values. It rejects a purely formal approach to law where the only question is whether a rule exists. Instead, South African law must be interpreted, applied and limited in a way that promotes constitutional values.

Section 1 is the starting point. It entrenches values such as human dignity, the achievement of equality, the advancement of human rights and freedoms, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. These values show that the Constitution is democratic, rights-based, accountable and transformative.

10. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton:

Value-based constitutionalism = Constitution as normative order, not just rules.

s 1 = founding values.

s 7(1) = Bill of Rights affirms dignity, equality and freedom.

s 36 = rights limitation must be reasonable and justifiable in a society based on dignity, equality and freedom.

s 39 = interpretation must promote constitutional values.

Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) = values + proportionality + death penalty unconstitutional — decided under s 33 of Interim Constitution; central to 1996 Constitution because s 36 repeats the approach (First Certification Judgment para 90).

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

A 90% answer should include a balanced critique. Value-based constitutionalism is powerful because it prevents law from becoming a morally empty technical system. It helps courts interpret law in a way that promotes dignity, equality, freedom, transformation, openness and accountability.

But there is also a danger. Values can be broad and contested. Judges may disagree about what dignity, equality or freedom require in a particular case. This can raise concerns about judicial discretion and the counter-majoritarian difficulty, especially where courts invalidate laws supported by elected representatives or public opinion.

The answer is not to abandon values. The Constitution itself requires value-based reasoning through sections 1, 7, 36 and 39. The better view is that courts must use values carefully, transparently and with reasons. This fits the idea of a culture of justification: values must not be used as slogans, but as reasons that justify constitutional interpretation and outcomes.

Exam sentence: The critical issue is not whether courts may use values, because the Constitution requires them to do so; the issue is whether courts justify their use of values in a principled, transparent and context-sensitive way.

M. Conclusion

Value-based constitutionalism is central to South African constitutional law. The Constitution does not merely create institutions and procedures; it establishes a supreme normative order founded on dignity, equality, freedom, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness, openness, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law. These values guide the interpretation of rights under section 39, the limitation of rights under section 36 and the broader transformation of law and society. Cases such as Makwanyane (decided under the Interim Constitution but central to the 1996 framework through section 36), Daniels and City of Tshwane show that constitutional values are not abstract decorations. They shape legal reasoning, constitutional remedies and South Africa's movement from apartheid legality to substantive constitutional justice.

THEME 4 ESSAY BANK 3

Foundational Values and Value-Based Constitutionalism: The Constitution as a Normative Legal Order

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

2. Core Thesis

Value-based constitutionalism means that the South African Constitution is not only a set of legal rules and institutions, but a supreme normative order rooted in foundational values such as dignity, equality, freedom, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness, openness, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law. These values guide how law is interpreted, limited, applied and justified.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: the Constitution is not morally neutral

South Africa's Constitution is a value-based Constitution. This means it is not merely a technical rulebook that creates institutions and procedures. It is a supreme legal order built around moral, political and social commitments. These values matter because South Africa's previous legal order often had formal legality without substantive justice: apartheid laws were "legal" in a narrow sense, but they were unjust, exclusionary and oppressive.

Theme 4 explains that the 1996 Constitution is a supreme, value-based, democratic and transformative constitutional framework created against the background of colonialism, apartheid, parliamentary sovereignty and the abuse of law as an instrument of oppression. Public power must now be exercised lawfully, rationally, accountably and consistently with dignity, equality and freedom.

B. Definition of value-based constitutionalism

Value-based constitutionalism means that the Constitution is rooted in core values that guide the interpretation, application, limitation and development of law. Courts and legal actors do not only ask, "What does the law literally say?" They must also ask, "Which interpretation best promotes the Constitution's text, structure, rights, principles and values?"

The Theme 4 Final Boss defines value-based constitutionalism as the idea that constitutions are not merely legal or procedural documents, but are rooted in core ethical, moral and social values that guide the interpretation of rights and laws.

Exam sentence: Value-based constitutionalism marks a break from apartheid legality because it rejects the idea that law is valid simply because it was properly enacted; law and public power must now be justified through the constitutional text, structure, rights, principles and values.

C. The founding values in section 1

Section 1 is the foundation of value-based constitutionalism. It provides that South Africa is founded on values including:

Section 1 valueMeaningExam useHuman dignityEvery person has inherent worth and must be treated as an end, not an object.Use in Makwanyane, equality, rights interpretation and limitation.Achievement of equalityEquality is not only formal sameness; it includes redress and substantive equality.Use in transformative constitutionalism and s 9.Advancement of human rights and freedomsRights are central to the constitutional order.Use with the Bill of Rights and s 7(1).Non-racialism and non-sexismThe Constitution rejects apartheid and patriarchy.Use in equality, transformation and redress questions.Supremacy of the ConstitutionThe Constitution is above all law and conduct.Use in constitutional supremacy and judicial review.Rule of lawPublic power must be lawful, rational and non-arbitrary.Use in legality and EFF.Universal adult suffrageEvery adult citizen has the vote.Use in democracy questions.Common voters roll and regular electionsDemocracy is institutionalised through elections.Use in representative democracy.Multi-party democratic governmentPolitical pluralism is protected.Use in democracy and political rights.Accountability, responsiveness and opennessGovernment must answer to the people and act transparently.Use in public participation, oversight and separation of powers.

D. Section 7(1): dignity, equality and freedom as Bill of Rights values

Section 7(1) says the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy in South Africa and affirms the democratic values of human dignity, equality and freedom. These three values are often the most important values in rights questions. They work together:

ValueMeaningWhy it mattersDignityHuman worth, respect and personhoodPrevents people from being treated as objects or instrumentsEqualityEqual concern, respect and substantive redressResponds directly to apartheid's racial hierarchyFreedomAutonomy, choice and participationProtects people from domination and coercion

A strong exam answer should say that dignity, equality and freedom are not isolated values. They reinforce each other. Apartheid denied dignity by denying equality and freedom. The Constitution therefore uses these values together to guide rights interpretation and limitation.

E. Section 39: values guide interpretation

Section 39 is the clearest textual proof that South African interpretation is value-based. Section 39(1) provides that when interpreting the Bill of Rights, courts must promote the values that underlie an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom. Section 39(2) provides that when interpreting legislation or developing the common law or customary law, every court must promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Theme 4 explains that section 39 directs courts to interpret the Bill of Rights, legislation, common law and customary law in ways that promote constitutional values and the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Exam sentence: Section 39 prevents courts from interpreting law mechanically. It requires interpretation to be consistent with the values and purposes of the Constitution.

F. Daniels v Campbell: value-based statutory interpretation

Daniels v Campbell 2004 (5) SA 331 (CC) is a useful case for section 39(2) and value-based interpretation. The case concerned whether the word "spouse" in succession and maintenance legislation could include a surviving spouse in a Muslim marriage. Sachs J reasoned that constitutional values such as equality, tolerance and respect for diversity supported a broad and inclusive interpretation. The Court interpreted the legislation to include the surviving spouse in a Muslim marriage.

Exam use: Daniels shows that ordinary legislation must be interpreted through constitutional values. A narrow interpretation may reproduce exclusion, while a value-based interpretation can promote equality, dignity, tolerance and diversity.

G. Section 36: values guide limitation of rights

Section 36 is the limitation clause. It provides that rights in the Bill of Rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom.

This means rights are not absolute, but they also cannot be limited casually. The state must justify the limitation by showing that it is proportionate and consistent with constitutional values. The Theme 4 Final Boss explains the section 36 two-stage enquiry:

StageQuestionStage 1: infringementHas a right in the Bill of Rights been limited or infringed?Stage 2: justificationIs the limitation reasonable and justifiable under section 36?

It also lists the section 36 factors: the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and less restrictive means.

Exam sentence: Section 36 shows that rights limitation is value-based because the question is not simply whether the state has power, but whether the limitation can be justified in a society committed to dignity, equality and freedom.

H. S v Makwanyane: the best case for values

S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) is the strongest case for value-based constitutionalism, especially if the question asks about dignity, values, public opinion, proportionality or limitation.

Important note on constitutional context: Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993, not under section 36 of the 1996 Constitution. It remains central to the 1996 constitutional order because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) of the 1996 Constitution substantially repeats the proportionality approach formulated in Makwanyane.

Facts: Makwanyane and Mchunu had been convicted in the Witwatersrand Local Division on four counts of murder each and sentenced to death under section 277(1)(a) of the Criminal Procedure Act 51 of 1977. The Constitutional Court had to decide whether the statutory provision authorising capital punishment was constitutional.

Issue: Whether the death penalty could be justified in a constitutional order based on rights and values such as life, dignity, equality and freedom.

Court reasoning: Chaskalson P held that the limitation of constitutional rights involves weighing up competing values and applying a proportionality analysis. The inquiry must consider the nature and importance of the right, the purpose of the limitation, the extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and whether less restrictive means are available.

The state relied on deterrence, prevention, retribution and public safety. But the Court held that these purposes had to be balanced against the rights and values at stake. The death penalty destroys life, assaults dignity, risks arbitrariness and error, and operates in a society shaped by racial and social inequality. The Court was not persuaded that capital punishment was materially more effective than life imprisonment as a deterrent. The Court also made an important value-based point about public opinion: even if public opinion supports the death penalty, public opinion cannot replace the Court's duty to uphold the Constitution.

Judgment: The death penalty was unconstitutional.

Constitutional significance: Makwanyane shows that values are not decorative. They determine constitutional meaning, guide limitation analysis and may require courts to protect constitutional commitments even against popular sentiment.

Exam use: Use Makwanyane whenever the question asks about dignity, equality, freedom, section 36 (noting it was decided under s 33 IC), proportionality, values, public opinion, constitutional democracy or the difference between law and justice.

I. First Certification Judgment: section 36 and proportionality

The First Certification Judgment — Certification of the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) is useful because it confirms the link between Makwanyane and the 1996 Constitution's limitation clause. At paragraph 90, the Court explained that limitation analysis involves weighing competing values and ultimately applying proportionality, and confirmed that section 36(1) substantially repeats the approach formulated in Makwanyane.

Exam use: Use this if you want to sound precise: Makwanyane was decided under the Interim Constitution, but its proportionality approach remains central because section 36 of the 1996 Constitution substantially repeats that approach (First Certification Judgment para 90).

J. Preamble and City of Tshwane: values, belonging and transformation

The Preamble is not just decorative. In City of Tshwane Metropolitan Municipality v Afriforum 2016 (6) SA 279 (CC), Mogoeng CJ described a preamble as a statement that sets out a constitution's purpose and underlying philosophy. He linked the Preamble to South Africa's commitment to recognise the injustices of the past, heal divisions, build a society based on democratic values, social justice and human rights, and affirm that South Africa belongs to all who live in it, united in diversity.

The case involved a dispute about changing street names associated with colonial and apartheid history. The Court stressed that public symbols are not neutral because they can either reproduce exclusion or promote recognition and belonging. Mogoeng CJ emphasised that South Africans must take seriously the foundational values of dignity, equality, advancement of human rights, non-racialism and non-sexism.

Exam use: City of Tshwane shows that constitutional values guide not only court interpretation, but also how society understands belonging, public space, memory, redress and transformation.

K. The five characteristics of value-based constitutionalism

The Theme 4 Final Boss identifies five useful characteristics:

CharacteristicMeaningNormativeThe Constitution is based on moral values, not just legal rules.InterpretiveValues guide how courts understand and apply rights.TransformativeThe Constitution aims to change society and address injustice.ContextualValues must be understood in South Africa's historical and social context.Counter-majoritarianCourts may uphold constitutional values even against public opinion or majority sentiment.

L. Critical engagement: the danger of vague values

A 90% answer should include a balanced critique. Value-based constitutionalism is powerful because it prevents law from becoming a morally empty technical system. It helps courts interpret law in a way that promotes dignity, equality, freedom, transformation, openness and accountability.

But there is also a danger. Values can be broad and contested. Judges may disagree about what dignity, equality or freedom require in a particular case. This can raise concerns about judicial discretion and the counter-majoritarian difficulty, especially where courts invalidate laws supported by elected representatives or public opinion.

The answer is not to abandon values. The Constitution itself requires value-based reasoning through sections 1, 7, 36 and 39. The better view is that courts must use values carefully, transparently and with reasons. This fits the idea of a culture of justification: values must not be used as slogans, but as reasons that justify constitutional interpretation and outcomes.

Exam sentence: The critical issue is not whether courts may use values, because the Constitution requires them to do so; the issue is whether courts justify their use of values in a principled, transparent and context-sensitive way.

M. Conclusion

Value-based constitutionalism is central to South African constitutional law. The Constitution does not merely create institutions and procedures; it establishes a supreme normative order founded on dignity, equality, freedom, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness, openness, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law. These values guide the interpretation of rights under section 39, the limitation of rights under section 36 and the broader transformation of law and society. Cases such as Makwanyane (decided under the Interim Constitution but central to the 1996 framework through section 36), Daniels and City of Tshwane show that constitutional values are not abstract decorations. They shape legal reasoning, constitutional remedies and South Africa's movement from apartheid legality to substantive constitutional justice.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Value-based constitutionalism means that the Constitution is not only a set of legal rules, but a normative order based on foundational values. Section 1 lists values such as human dignity, achievement of equality, advancement of human rights and freedoms, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Value-based constitutionalism means that the Constitution is a supreme normative legal order based on foundational values. It rejects a purely formal approach to law where the only question is whether a rule exists. Instead, South African law must be interpreted, applied and limited in a way that promotes constitutional values.

Section 1 is the starting point. It entrenches values such as human dignity, the achievement of equality, the advancement of human rights and freedoms, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. These values show that the Constitution is democratic, rights-based, accountable and transformative.

Section 7(1) confirms that the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy and affirms dignity, equality and freedom. Section 39 then gives values an interpretive role: courts must interpret the Bill of Rights, legislation, common law and customary law in a way that promotes constitutional values and the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Section 36 gives values a justificatory role. Rights may be limited only by law of general application and only if the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom. In S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) — decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution but central to section 36 of the 1996 Constitution because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36 substantially repeats the Makwanyane approach — the Court used proportionality and constitutional values to hold that the death penalty was unconstitutional. The case shows that values are not merely symbolic; they guide whether the state can justify limiting rights.

Therefore, value-based constitutionalism ensures that law is not only formally valid, but also justified in light of dignity, equality, freedom, transformation and democratic accountability.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

Value-based constitutionalism means that the South African Constitution is not merely a technical legal document, but a supreme normative order founded on values. These values guide the creation, interpretation, application and limitation of law. The Constitution must therefore be understood as a rights-based, democratic and transformative framework that requires public power and legal interpretation to be justified through values such as dignity, equality, freedom, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness, openness, constitutional supremacy and the rule of law.

This is especially important in South Africa because apartheid law often had formal legality without substantive justice. Laws were valid in a narrow positivist sense, but they entrenched racial domination and exclusion. Theme 4 explains that the 1996 Constitution was created against the background of colonialism, apartheid, parliamentary sovereignty and the abuse of law as an instrument of oppression. Its doctrines are designed to ensure that public power is exercised lawfully, rationally, accountably and consistently with dignity, equality and freedom.

Section 1 is the foundation of value-based constitutionalism. It provides that South Africa is founded on values including human dignity, the achievement of equality, the advancement of human rights and freedoms, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. These values are not merely symbolic. They guide how the Constitution is interpreted and how state power must be exercised.

Section 7(1) confirms that the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy and affirms the democratic values of human dignity, equality and freedom. These three values are especially important because they respond directly to apartheid's denial of equal personhood, autonomy and freedom. Dignity protects inherent human worth, equality requires substantive redress and equal concern, and freedom protects autonomy and participation.

Section 39 gives constitutional values an interpretive role. Section 39(1) requires courts, when interpreting the Bill of Rights, to promote the values underlying an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom. Section 39(2) requires every court, when interpreting legislation or developing common law or customary law, to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. Theme 4 explains that section 39 directs courts to interpret rights and law in ways that promote constitutional values.

The case of Daniels v Campbell 2004 (5) SA 331 (CC) illustrates value-based statutory interpretation. The Court had to decide whether the word "spouse" in succession and maintenance legislation included a surviving spouse in a Muslim marriage. Sachs J reasoned that constitutional values of equality, tolerance and respect for diversity supported a broad and inclusive interpretation. The Court therefore interpreted the legislation to include the surviving spouse in a Muslim marriage. This shows that ordinary legislation must be read through constitutional values.

Section 36 gives values a justificatory role in rights limitation. It provides that rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom. This means that the state must justify rights limitations through proportionality. The main section 36 factors are the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and less restrictive means.

The leading case is S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC). It is important to note that Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993, not section 36 of the 1996 Constitution. However, it remains central to the current constitutional order because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) of the 1996 Constitution substantially repeats the proportionality approach formulated in Makwanyane. In that case, Chaskalson P held that limitation analysis involves weighing competing values and applying proportionality. The Court considered the state's purposes — deterrence, prevention, retribution and public safety — but held that these had to be balanced against life, dignity and equality. The death penalty destroys life, assaults dignity, risks arbitrariness and error, and operates within a society shaped by racial and social inequality. Crucially, the Court held that public opinion cannot replace the Court's duty to uphold the Constitution. Makwanyane therefore shows that values shape both constitutional interpretation and the justification of rights limitations.

The Preamble and City of Tshwane Metropolitan Municipality v Afriforum 2016 (6) SA 279 (CC) show the broader transformative role of values. In City of Tshwane, Mogoeng CJ explained that the Preamble sets out the Constitution's purpose and underlying philosophy. It recognises past injustices, commits South Africa to healing divisions, and affirms that South Africa belongs to all who live in it, united in diversity. The Court linked these ideas to dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, ubuntu, public memory and inclusive belonging. The case shows that values guide not only court reasoning, but also how public space, history and belonging are transformed.

Critically, value-based constitutionalism can raise concerns. Constitutional values are broad and sometimes contested. Judges may disagree about what dignity, equality or freedom require in a particular case. This creates a risk of judicial discretion and raises counter-majoritarian concerns where courts reject laws or policies supported by elected branches or public opinion. However, the answer is not to abandon values. Sections 1, 7, 36 and 39 require courts to reason through values. The real requirement is that courts must use values transparently, carefully and with reasons. This fits South Africa's broader move from a culture of authority to a culture of justification.

In conclusion, value-based constitutionalism is central to South African constitutional law. It means that law must be interpreted and applied in light of the Constitution's foundational commitments. Values such as dignity, equality, freedom, rule of law, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness and openness are not decorative. They shape rights interpretation under section 39, rights limitation under section 36, and the broader project of transforming South Africa from apartheid legality to substantive constitutional justice.

Constitutional subsidiarity warning

Where legislation has been enacted to give effect to a constitutional right, a litigant generally may not bypass that legislation and rely directly on the constitutional right, unless the litigant challenges the constitutionality of the legislation itself. This matters especially in horizontal/application questions involving equality legislation such as PEPUDA and labour legislation such as the LRA. Direct constitutional reliance is not always available merely because a right is implicated.

5/10/15-mark versions

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

The application of the Bill of Rights concerns who is bound by rights and how those rights operate. Vertical application concerns the relationship between the state and private persons. Section 8(1) provides that the Bill of Rights applies to all law and binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. Section 7(2) also requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights.

Horizontal application concerns relationships between private persons or private entities. Section 8(2) provides that a right may bind a natural or juristic person if, and to the extent that, it is applicable, considering the nature of the right and the nature of the duty.

Direct application means a person relies directly on a constitutional right. Indirect application means ordinary law — such as legislation, common law or customary law — is interpreted or developed in light of the Bill of Rights under s 39(2).

Mark focus: s 8(1) + s 8(2) + s 39(2) + one example.

Answer:

The Bill of Rights is broad in application because it gives effect to South Africa's transformative constitutional order. The correct approach is to identify the right, ask whether the application is direct or indirect, ask whether the relationship is vertical or horizontal, and then identify the relevant constitutional section: s 8(1), s 8(2) or s 39(2).

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

The First Certification Judgment — Certification of the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) is useful for a 90% answer because it shows that horizontal application was controversial during certification.

Objectors argued that horizontal application would let courts interfere with private law and legislative functions. The Constitutional Court rejected this. It reasoned that courts have always developed the common law, so there is no separation of powers objection to courts continuing to do so in a constitutional era. The Court also rejected the argument that balancing competing rights is not a proper judicial role.

Most importantly, the Court confirmed that horizontal application is not unqualified. Section 8(2) is conditioned by the phrase "if, and to the extent that, it is applicable", taking into account the nature of the right and the nature of the duty. It also linked the development of customary law to s 39(2), requiring courts to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Exam sentence: The First Certification Judgment shows that horizontal application is constitutionally legitimate, but carefully controlled by the nature of the right, the nature of the duty and the court's ordinary role in developing common law and customary law.

THEME 4 ESSAY BANK 4

Application of the Bill of Rights: Direct, Indirect, Vertical and Horizontal Application

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

Possible exam questionWhat the examiner wants"Distinguish between vertical and horizontal application of the Bill of Rights."s 8(1) vs s 8(2), examples"Distinguish between direct and indirect application of the Bill of Rights."s 8 vs s 39(2), examples"Explain horizontal application of the Bill of Rights."private parties, nature of right and duty, transformative role"Explain how the Bill of Rights applies to private relationships."s 8(2), s 8(3), s 39(2), PEPUDA/common law"Discuss the transformative significance of horizontal application."private power, equality, dignity, common law development"How does the Bill of Rights apply to the state?"s 8(1), s 7(2), vertical application"Explain the difference between direct horizontal and indirect horizontal application."direct constitutional reliance vs ordinary-law development"Apply the Bill of Rights to a scenario involving a private actor."identify right → vertical/horizontal → direct/indirect → balance

2. Core Thesis

The application of the Bill of Rights determines who is bound by constitutional rights and how those rights operate. In South Africa, the Bill of Rights applies vertically to the state under section 8(1), and may also apply horizontally to private persons under section 8(2), while section 39(2) allows constitutional rights and values to influence ordinary law indirectly through interpretation and development of legislation, common law and customary law.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: why application matters

The Bill of Rights is central to South Africa's transformative constitutional order because it protects dignity, equality and freedom and gives practical effect to constitutional supremacy. However, before a court can decide whether a right has been infringed or limited, it must ask how the Bill of Rights applies.

The application enquiry asks:

Which right is involved?

Who is the alleged violator?

Is the relationship vertical or horizontal?

Is the right being applied directly or indirectly?

Which constitutional provision governs the application?

Are there competing rights or justifications that must be balanced?

The Theme 4 Final Boss sets this out as the decision tree: identify the Chapter 2 right, ask direct or indirect, ask vertical or horizontal, identify s 8(1), s 8(2) or s 39(2), and balance competing rights using constitutional values and section 36 where relevant.

B. Direct application

Direct application occurs when a litigant relies directly on a constitutional right in the Bill of Rights to challenge law or conduct. The Constitution itself is the source of the claim or defence.

Theme 4 defines direct application as a situation where a person, company or organ of state violates a Chapter 2 right, or where legislation, common law or customary law is inconsistent with the Bill of Rights. If applied directly, the Bill of Rights overrides inconsistent law or conduct.

Example: If the state arrests a person for lawful protest, the person may rely directly on constitutional rights such as freedom of expression in s 16 and assembly in s 17. If a law authorises the violation and cannot be justified under s 36, the law or conduct may be declared invalid under constitutional supremacy.

Exam sentence: Direct application means the Constitution itself is used as the legal standard to test law or conduct.

C. Indirect application

Indirect application occurs when the Bill of Rights does not operate as the direct cause of action, but influences the interpretation or development of ordinary law.

The key provision is section 39(2). It requires every court, when interpreting legislation and when developing common law or customary law, to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Theme 4 defines indirect application as asking whether ordinary legal rules promote Bill of Rights values; if they do not, courts must interpret legislation or develop common law/customary law to conform with constitutional values.

Example: A claimant may sue in delict using the common law, but the court may develop the common law duty of care in light of constitutional values. This is indirect application because the case is formally about delict, but the Constitution shapes the common law rule.

Exam sentence: Indirect application means ordinary law remains the immediate source of the claim, but that law must be interpreted or developed in light of the Bill of Rights.

D. Vertical application: state versus individual

Vertical application concerns the relationship between the state and a private person. It applies when the alleged rights violator is the state, an organ of state, or a public institution exercising public power.

Section 8(1) provides that the Bill of Rights applies to all law and binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. Section 7(2) adds that the state must respect, protect, promote and fulfil the rights in the Bill of Rights.

Your tutorial material puts it neatly: vertical application concerns the relationship between the state and persons, and asks whether law or state conduct unjustifiably infringes a protected right. For example, if a public school, municipality, police official or government department excludes someone from a benefit because of race, that implicates equality and may be attacked as unconstitutional state conduct.

Examples of vertical application:

RightVertical exampleEquality, s 9A government department refuses to hire or promote someone because of race, gender or sexual orientation.Freedom of expression, s 16Police arrest a person for lawful protest or expression.Access to information, s 32A person requests state-held information through PAIA.Housing, s 26A community challenges the state's housing programme.Healthcare / water / social security, s 27A person challenges unreasonable state failure to provide access.Fair trial, s 35Accused person challenges unlawful detention or unfair trial conduct.Property, s 25A landowner challenges state expropriation.

E. Horizontal application: private person versus private person

Horizontal application concerns private relationships. It applies when one private person, company or private institution allegedly infringes the right of another private person.

Section 8(2) provides that a provision of the Bill of Rights binds a natural or juristic person if, and to the extent that, it is applicable, taking into account the nature of the right and the nature of any duty imposed by the right.

This is very important: horizontal application is not automatic. The court must ask whether the particular right is suitable for application in that private relationship.

Your tutorial answer explains that horizontal application concerns relationships between private persons or entities, and that s 8(2) allows a right to bind a natural or juristic person where applicable, considering the nature of the right and duty. Section 8(3) instructs courts, when applying a right horizontally, to apply or develop the common law consistently with constitutional values.

Why horizontal application matters: Horizontal application is transformative because rights violations do not only come from the state. Private power can also undermine dignity, equality, housing, education, expression, privacy and labour rights. Theme 4 emphasises that horizontal application shows the Constitution does not merely restrain the state; it can reshape private law and private relationships where power imbalance, human needs or constitutional values require it.

Examples of horizontal application:

RightHorizontal exampleEquality, s 9A private employer refuses to hire someone because of race, gender or disability.Equality, s 9(4)A privately owned restaurant refuses to serve someone because of race.Housing, s 26A private landowner seeks eviction without proper legal process.Education, s 29A private school refuses admission in a way that undermines basic education or equality.Expression, s 16A private media company refuses to publish something; court must balance expression and editorial autonomy.PrivacyA private person unlawfully publishes another person's private information.

F. Direct horizontal application

Direct horizontal application occurs when a private person relies directly on a constitutional right against another private person.

Theme 4 defines direct horizontal application as a situation where the Bill of Rights applies directly to private conduct, allowing a private person to rely directly on a constitutional right in a dispute with another private person.

Section 9(4) is the easiest example. It expressly states that no person may unfairly discriminate directly or indirectly against anyone. This makes equality particularly suitable for horizontal application because the constitutional text itself directly speaks to private persons. So if a private employer or private restaurant discriminates unfairly, the applicant may rely on equality principles. In practice, private equality claims are often given effect through legislation such as PEPUDA or through interpretation/development of ordinary law, even though s 9(4) recognises that private discrimination may have constitutional significance.

G. Indirect horizontal application

Indirect horizontal application occurs when a private-law dispute is resolved through ordinary law, but the ordinary law is interpreted or developed in light of constitutional values.

Theme 4 defines indirect horizontal application as the influence of the Bill of Rights on private law through s 39(2), requiring courts to interpret legislation or develop the common law in a way that promotes the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Example: A delictual claim may be decided through common law, but the court may develop the common law duty of care to reflect constitutional values such as dignity, freedom and security of the person. That is indirect horizontal application.

H. Carmichele: key case for indirect application

Carmichele v Minister of Safety and Security 2001 (4) SA 938 (CC) is the key case for indirect application and development of common law.

Facts: The applicant was severely attacked and injured by a man who had previously been released on bail despite the police and prosecutors knowing about his history of violent behaviour. The police and public prosecutors had failed to oppose bail or take steps to warn or protect the applicant. The case raised the question of whether the state owed a delictual duty of care to members of the public who were foreseeably at risk from such a person.

Issue: Whether courts must develop the common law of delict to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights under section 39(2).

Court reasoning: The Constitutional Court confirmed that courts have a duty under s 39(2) to develop the common law where necessary to align it with constitutional values. The common law duty of care could be developed to hold the state liable for failing to take reasonable steps to protect individuals from foreseeable harm, in line with constitutional values of dignity, freedom and security of the person.

Judgment: The matter was sent back so the common law could be developed or applied consistently with constitutional values.

Constitutional significance: It is a foundational case for indirect horizontal application and constitutional development of common law.

Exam use: Use for s 39(2), indirect application, common law development, horizontal effect and transformative constitutionalism.

I. Grootboom: key case for vertical application

Government of the Republic of South Africa v Grootboom 2001 (1) SA 46 (CC) is a strong vertical application example.

Facts: The respondents, including many children, had been evicted from their informal homes and shacks and were living in appalling conditions with inadequate shelter. They challenged the state's housing programme under section 26.

Issue: Whether the state had taken reasonable measures to realise the right of access to adequate housing.

Court reasoning: The Court required a reasonable housing programme that made provision for people in crisis and desperate need, not only long-term housing plans.

Judgment: The state's programme was unreasonable because it did not adequately provide for people in emergency and desperate need.

Constitutional significance: An important vertical socio-economic rights case showing how communities can hold the state constitutionally accountable.

Exam use: Use for vertical application, socio-economic rights, reasonableness review and transformative constitutionalism. Do not use Grootboom as a horizontal application example — the state was the respondent.

J. First Certification Judgment: horizontal application is valid but not unlimited

The First Certification Judgment — Certification of the Constitution of the Republic of South Africa, 1996 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) is useful for a 90% answer because it shows that horizontal application was controversial during certification.

Objectors argued that horizontal application would let courts interfere with private law and legislative functions. The Constitutional Court rejected this. It reasoned that courts have always developed the common law, so there is no separation of powers objection to courts continuing to do so in a constitutional era. The Court also rejected the argument that balancing competing rights is not a proper judicial role.

Most importantly, the Court confirmed that horizontal application is not unqualified. Section 8(2) is conditioned by the phrase "if, and to the extent that, it is applicable", taking into account the nature of the right and the nature of the duty. It also linked the development of customary law to s 39(2), requiring courts to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights.

Exam sentence: The First Certification Judgment shows that horizontal application is constitutionally legitimate, but carefully controlled by the nature of the right, the nature of the duty and the court's ordinary role in developing common law and customary law.

K. Direct vs indirect and vertical vs horizontal: do not mix them up

These are two different distinctions.

DistinctionQuestion it answersVertical vs horizontalWho is the alleged rights violator? State or private actor?Direct vs indirectHow is the Bill of Rights being used? Directly as the claim, or indirectly through ordinary law?

The four possible combinations:

CombinationMeaningExampleDirect verticalPerson relies directly on Constitution against stateProtester challenges police arrest under ss 16 and 17Indirect verticalOrdinary law involving state is interpreted/developed through Bill of RightsAdministrative or statutory rule interpreted to protect rightsDirect horizontalPerson relies directly on constitutional right against private actorEquality claim against private discrimination under s 9(4)Indirect horizontalPrivate law is interpreted/developed through constitutional valuesCarmichele-type common law development

L. Practical exam application framework

For any scenario, write it in this order:

  1. Step 1: Identify the right — equality, dignity, housing, expression, privacy, education, fair trial.
  2. Step 2: Identify the relationship — is the respondent the state or a private actor? State = vertical. Private actor = horizontal.

Step 3: Identify the section — s 8(1) if vertical; s 8(2) if horizontal; s 8(3) if horizontal application requires common law development; s 39(2) if ordinary law must be interpreted or developed.

  1. Step 4: Decide direct or indirect — direct = Constitution itself is relied on; indirect = ordinary law is shaped by constitutional values.
  2. Step 5: Balance rights if necessary — if rights conflict, use constitutional values and, where appropriate, s 36.
  3. Step 6: Conclude — state whether the Bill of Rights applies and how.

The application of the Bill of Rights concerns who is bound by rights and how those rights operate. Vertical application concerns the relationship between the state and private persons. Section 8(1) provides that the Bill of Rights applies to all law and binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. Section 7(2) also requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights.
