7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

The Bill of Rights is broad in application because it gives effect to South Africa's transformative constitutional order. The correct approach is to identify the right, ask whether the application is direct or indirect, ask whether the relationship is vertical or horizontal, and then identify the relevant constitutional section: s 8(1), s 8(2) or s 39(2).

Vertical application concerns disputes between the state and private persons. Section 8(1) provides that the Bill of Rights applies to all law and binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. Section 7(2) requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights. For example, if a government department discriminates against a person based on race or gender, that conduct can be challenged under the right to equality in s 9.

Horizontal application concerns disputes between private persons or entities. Section 8(2) provides that a right binds a natural or juristic person if, and to the extent applicable, considering the nature of the right and duty. Horizontal application is not automatic. A court must ask whether the particular right is suitable for private application. This is important because private power can also undermine dignity, equality, housing, education and expression.

Direct application occurs when the Constitution itself is relied on as the legal standard. Indirect application occurs when ordinary law is interpreted or developed in light of constitutional values, especially under s 39(2). Carmichele v Minister of Safety and Security 2001 (4) SA 938 (CC) is the key case for indirect application. The applicant was attacked by a man released on bail despite his known violent history, and the police and prosecutors had failed to act. The Constitutional Court confirmed that courts must develop the common law under s 39(2) to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights, including developing the duty of care to hold the state liable for foreseeable harm.

Therefore, the Bill of Rights does not only restrain the state. It can also influence private relationships directly or indirectly, especially where constitutional values, power imbalance or basic human needs require protection.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

The application of the Bill of Rights determines who is bound by constitutional rights and how those rights operate. In South Africa, this question is especially important because the Constitution is transformative. Rights are not only designed to restrain public power; they also shape the legal order and, in appropriate circumstances, private relationships. The Bill of Rights therefore has both vertical and horizontal dimensions, and it may operate directly or indirectly.

The correct starting point is the application decision tree. One must identify the right in Chapter 2, ask whether the right is being applied directly or indirectly, determine whether the relationship is vertical or horizontal, identify the relevant constitutional section, and balance competing rights using constitutional values and s 36 where relevant. Theme 4 identifies the main application provisions as s 8(1), s 8(2) and s 39(2).

Direct application occurs when a litigant relies directly on a constitutional right. The Constitution itself becomes the standard against which law or conduct is tested. Theme 4 defines direct application as occurring where a person, company or organ of state violates a Chapter 2 right, or where legislation, common law or customary law is inconsistent with the Bill of Rights.

Indirect application occurs when ordinary law is interpreted or developed in light of constitutional values. Section 39(2) requires courts, when interpreting legislation and developing the common law or customary law, to promote the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. This means the claim may formally arise under legislation, delict, contract or customary law, but constitutional values still shape the legal rule.

Vertical application concerns the relationship between the state and private persons. Section 8(1) provides that the Bill of Rights applies to all law and binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. Section 7(2) requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights. For example, if a public school, municipality, police official or government department excludes a person from a benefit because of race, the equality right in s 9 is implicated and the state conduct may be challenged as unconstitutional.

Horizontal application concerns relationships between private persons or private entities. Section 8(2) provides that a provision of the Bill of Rights binds a natural or juristic person if, and to the extent that, it is applicable, considering the nature of the right and duty. Section 8(3) instructs courts, when applying a right horizontally, to give effect to that right by applying or, where necessary, developing the common law consistently with constitutional values.

Horizontal application is transformative because it recognises that rights violations do not only come from the state. Private power can also threaten dignity, equality, housing, education, privacy and expression. Theme 4 explains that horizontal application shows that the Constitution does not merely restrain the state; it can reshape private law and private relationships where power imbalance, human needs or constitutional values require it.

Direct horizontal application occurs where a private person relies directly on a constitutional right against another private person. Equality is the clearest example because s 9(4) states that no person may unfairly discriminate directly or indirectly against anyone. Thus, if a private restaurant refuses to serve a customer because of race, this is not merely a private wrong; it is a constitutional equality issue usually given practical effect through legislation such as PEPUDA or, where appropriate, through ordinary-law development.

Indirect horizontal application occurs when constitutional values influence private law through s 39(2). Carmichele v Minister of Safety and Security 2001 (4) SA 938 (CC) is the key case. The applicant was severely attacked and injured by a man released on bail despite his known history of violent behaviour, after the police and prosecutors had failed to oppose bail or protect her. The Constitutional Court confirmed that courts have a duty under s 39(2) to develop common law where necessary to align it with constitutional values, making Carmichele foundational for indirect horizontal application and common law development.

A vertical example is Government of the Republic of South Africa v Grootboom 2001 (1) SA 46 (CC), where the respondents, including many children, had been evicted from their informal homes and were living in appalling conditions. The Court required a reasonable housing programme that made provision for people in crisis and desperate need. Theme 4 uses Grootboom as an important vertical socio-economic rights case showing communities challenging government for access to housing.

Critically, horizontal application is not unlimited. The First Certification Judgment 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) rejected objections that horizontal application would improperly allow courts to invade the legislative sphere, explaining that courts have always developed common law and that balancing competing rights is a proper judicial function. However, the Court emphasised that horizontal application is controlled by the wording of s 8(2): it applies only "if, and to the extent that, it is applicable," considering the nature of the right and duty.

In conclusion, the Bill of Rights applies broadly but carefully. Vertically, it binds the state and controls public power. Horizontally, it may bind private persons where the nature of the right and duty justify it. Direct application allows reliance on the Constitution itself, while indirect application allows constitutional values to shape ordinary law. This framework gives effect to the transformative Constitution by ensuring that dignity, equality and freedom influence both public power and private legal relationships.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Rights in the Bill of Rights are not absolute. Section 36 provides that rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom.

The court uses a two-stage enquiry. First, it asks whether a right has been limited. Secondly, it asks whether the limitation is justified under s 36. The relevant factors are the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and less restrictive means. THEME 4 ESSAY BANK 5

Limitation of Rights under Section 36: Proportionality, Justification and S v Makwanyane

1. Possible Exam Questions This Essay Bank Can Answer

Possible exam questionWhat the examiner wants"Explain the general limitation clause in section 36."Definition + law of general application + reasonable and justifiable"Are rights absolute in South Africa?"No + strict limitation framework"Explain the two-stage enquiry for rights limitation."Infringement first, justification second"Discuss the proportionality test under section 36."s 36 factors + balancing"Explain S v Makwanyane and its importance for limitation analysis."Facts + proportionality + death penalty + values"How do dignity, equality and freedom guide limitation of rights?"s 36 + value-based constitutionalism"Critically discuss rights limitation in a constitutional democracy."Rights not absolute, but limitations must be justified"Apply section 36 to a hypothetical facts scenario."Step-by-step application

2. Core Thesis

Section 36 reflects the Constitution's balance between rights and legitimate social purposes: rights are not absolute, but they may be limited only by law of general application and only where the limitation is reasonable, justifiable and proportionate in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

AuthorityUses 1Foundational values: dignity, equality, rights, rule of laws 2Constitution supreme; inconsistent law/conduct invalids 7(1)Bill of Rights is cornerstone of democracys 36General limitation clauses 36(1)(a)Nature of the rights 36(1)(b)Importance of the purpose of limitations 36(1)(c)Nature and extent of limitations 36(1)(d)Relation between limitation and purposes 36(1)(e)Less restrictive meanss 39Interpretation must promote dignity, equality and freedomS v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC)Proportionality and value-based limitation — decided under s 33 of the Interim ConstitutionFirst Certification Judgment 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) para 90Confirms s 36 substantially repeats the Makwanyane approachDe Vos and FreedmanFive-stage proportionality guide

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: rights are fundamental, but not absolute

The Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of South African constitutional democracy. It protects rights and affirms the democratic values of dignity, equality and freedom. However, rights are generally not absolute. In a constitutional democracy, rights may sometimes conflict with other rights or with important public purposes such as safety, equality, public order, health, or the protection of vulnerable people.

Section 36 is the mechanism that controls this process. It ensures that rights are not limited casually or merely because the state finds a right inconvenient. A limitation must be authorised by law of general application and justified through proportionality. Within s 36, one factor asks about the relation between the limitation and its purpose, but this must not be collapsed into the separate legality doctrine, where rationality is a minimum requirement for the exercise of public power.

Theme 4 makes this very clear: rights are not absolute, but the Constitution permits their limitation only under strict conditions.

B. Definition of the limitation clause

A limitation clause allows constitutionally valid limitation of rights in certain circumstances. Section 36 provides that rights in the Bill of Rights may be limited only:

in terms of law of general application, and

to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable,

in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom,

taking into account all relevant factors, including the five listed factors in s 36(1)(a)–(e).

Exam sentence: Section 36 does not weaken rights; it protects rights by ensuring that any limitation must be justified through constitutional values and proportionality.

C. The two-stage enquiry

Do not start by applying s 36 immediately. The correct method is a two-stage enquiry.

StageQuestionWhat you doStage 1: Infringement / limitationHas a right in the Bill of Rights been limited?Identify the right, explain its content, and show how the facts interfere with it.Stage 2: JustificationIs the limitation reasonable and justifiable under s 36?Apply the s 36 factors and conclude whether the limitation is constitutional.

If there is no limitation of a right, there is no need to justify anything under s 36. If there is a limitation, the burden shifts to justification: the limiting party, usually the state, must show that the limitation is constitutionally acceptable.

D. "Law of general application"

Before proportionality, section 36 requires that the limitation must be in terms of law of general application. This means the limitation must be authorised by a legal rule that applies generally, rather than by an arbitrary individual command. It can include legislation, common law or another valid legal norm, but it must be accessible, non-arbitrary and general enough to satisfy the rule of law.

Exam sentence: The "law of general application" requirement links section 36 to the rule of law because rights may not be limited by arbitrary or ad hoc exercises of power.

E. The five section 36 factors

Section 36 lists five factors. Know these well enough to reproduce them clearly.

s 36 factorMeaningHow to apply itNature of the rightHow important is the right and what interests does it protect?Rights like life, dignity and equality carry very high weight.Importance of the purpose of the limitationWhat is the law trying to achieve?The purpose must be legitimate and important in an open and democratic society.Nature and extent of the limitationHow serious is the interference?A severe limitation requires a stronger justification.Relation between limitation and purposeIs the limitation rationally connected to its goal?If the measure is not properly related to its purpose, that weighs strongly against justification.Less restrictive meansCould the purpose be achieved while limiting the right less?The existence of a less restrictive effective means weighs strongly against justification, although it must still be assessed within the overall proportionality enquiry.

The existence of a less restrictive effective means often weighs strongly against justification, but it must still be assessed as part of the overall proportionality enquiry rather than treated as an automatic trump.

Precision note: The s 36 proportionality enquiry should not be collapsed into legality rationality review. Under s 36, the core enquiry is proportionality; one factor asks whether there is a proper relation between the limitation and its purpose. By contrast, rationality review under the rule of law / principle of legality is a separate minimum requirement for the exercise of public power.

F. Proportionality: the heart of section 36

The real enquiry under s 36 is proportionality. It is not a mechanical checklist. A court must weigh the importance of the right against the importance of the limitation's purpose and the severity of the limitation.

The First Certification Judgment 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) (para 90) confirms this. It states that limitation of constitutional rights involves weighing competing values and ultimately an assessment based on proportionality. It also confirms that section 36(1) of the 1996 Constitution substantially repeats the approach formulated in S v Makwanyane, and that the criteria in s 36 conform to internationally accepted standards of proportionality or balancing.

Exam sentence: Proportionality asks whether the constitutional harm caused by limiting the right is justified by the importance and effectiveness of the purpose pursued.

G. De Vos and Freedman five-stage proportionality guide

Theme 4 includes a useful De Vos and Freedman five-stage proportionality guide (pages 453–455):

StageQuestion1What is the purpose of the limitation, and is it legitimate in an open and democratic society?2What is the relationship between the limitation and its legitimate purpose? Are they rationally connected?3Are there alternative, less restrictive means?4Is the limitation proportionate, considering the extent of the infringement, nature of the right, breadth of the limitation and social good achieved?5If proportionate, the infringement is constitutionally valid; if not, it is unconstitutional.

This is a useful structure for a long answer because it shows you understand that the test is not only about naming the factors — it is about weighing them.

H. S v Makwanyane: the foundational case

S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC)

Important note on constitutional context: Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993, not section 36 of the 1996 Constitution. It remains the foundational case for limitation analysis under the 1996 Constitution because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) substantially repeats the proportionality approach formulated in Makwanyane.

Facts: Makwanyane and Mchunu had been convicted in the Witwatersrand Local Division on four counts of murder each and sentenced to death under section 277(1)(a) of the Criminal Procedure Act 51 of 1977. The appeal raised a constitutional challenge based on rights including life, dignity and protection against cruel, inhuman or degrading punishment.

Issue: Whether the death penalty was compatible with the Interim Constitution and, if it limited rights, whether that limitation was justified.

Court reasoning: Chaskalson P held that the limitation of constitutional rights involves the weighing up of competing values and an assessment based on proportionality. There is no absolute standard for reasonableness and necessity; the enquiry must be conducted case by case, considering the nature and importance of the right, the purpose of the limitation, the extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and whether less restrictive means are available.

The state relied on important purposes: deterrence of violent crime, prevention of further murders, retribution and public safety. But those purposes had to be balanced against the rights and values at stake. The death penalty destroys life, assaults dignity, involves risks of arbitrariness and error, and operates in a criminal justice system shaped by historical social and racial inequality.

The Court was not persuaded that capital punishment was materially more effective than life imprisonment as a deterrent. Prevention could be achieved through long-term or life imprisonment. Retribution was accepted as a legitimate purpose of punishment, but it could not outweigh life and dignity. Public opinion also could not replace the Court's duty to uphold the Constitution.

Judgment: The death penalty was declared unconstitutional. Section 277(1)(a) of the Criminal Procedure Act was held to be inconsistent with the Constitution to the extent that it authorised the death penalty for murder.

Constitutional significance: Makwanyane established South African limitation analysis as a proportionality enquiry. In every limitation case, the state must show that the limitation serves a sufficiently important purpose, is properly connected to that purpose, impairs the right no more than is constitutionally acceptable, and is proportionate in light of dignity, equality and freedom.

I. Public opinion and constitutional democracy

One of the most examinable points in Makwanyane is the Court's treatment of public opinion. The Court accepted that many people may have supported the death penalty, but constitutional rights cannot depend only on majority opinion. Courts have a duty to uphold the Constitution, including when doing so is unpopular. This links to value-based constitutionalism and the counter-majoritarian role of courts.

Exam sentence: Makwanyane shows that public opinion may be relevant background, but it cannot determine whether a rights limitation is constitutional.

J. Relationship between s 36 and s 39

SectionFunctions 36Determines whether a limitation of a right is reasonable and justifiable.s 39Guides interpretation of rights and ordinary law through dignity, equality and freedom.

Section 36 is the justification stage, while section 39 shapes the meaning of rights and law before and during that justification enquiry. South African constitutional adjudication is not mechanical: courts must ask whether law and conduct are consistent with the values of an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom.

K. Balancing competing rights: PE Municipality and Fourie

Port Elizabeth Municipality v Various Occupiers 2005 (1) SA 217 (CC): competing claims between property interests and occupiers facing eviction. Sachs J emphasised that fairness required considering both owners' rights and occupiers' dignity, especially in the context of South Africa's history of forced removals. Use where a limitation or balancing question involves property, housing, dignity, eviction, homelessness or historical disadvantage.

Minister of Home Affairs v Fourie 2006 (1) SA 524 (CC): exclusion of same-sex couples from marriage violated equality and dignity. Use where a limitation or balancing question involves equality, dignity, religion, marriage, pluralism or social transformation.

L. Critical engagement: limitation is necessary but dangerous

Why limitation is necessary: Rights can conflict with other rights or important social goals — expression vs dignity, property vs housing, privacy vs public safety. A constitutional democracy needs a limitation clause.

Why limitation is dangerous: Limitation can become a tool for weakening rights if courts accept vague purposes too easily or allow "public interest" to be used without careful analysis. This is why s 36 insists on law of general application, dignity, equality, freedom, proportionality and less restrictive means.

Critical exam sentence: Section 36 is both a permission and a protection: it permits rights to be limited where justified, but protects rights by requiring the state to give strong constitutional reasons for the limitation.

5. Case-Law Block: S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC)

ElementExam contentConstitutional contextDecided under s 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993. Central to s 36 of the 1996 Constitution because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed s 36(1) substantially repeats the Makwanyane approach.FactsMakwanyane and Mchunu were convicted on four counts of murder each and sentenced to death under s 277(1)(a) of the Criminal Procedure Act 51 of 1977.IssueWhether the death penalty was compatible with the Interim Constitution, and if it limited rights, whether the limitation was justified.Court reasoningChaskalson P held that limitation requires weighing competing values and proportionality. The Court assessed deterrence, prevention, retribution and public safety against life, dignity, equality, arbitrariness and less restrictive means.JudgmentThe death penalty was declared unconstitutional.Constitutional significanceFoundational for limitation analysis, proportionality, dignity, value-based constitutionalism and counter-majoritarian adjudication.Exam useUse for any question on limitation, dignity, death penalty, proportionality, public opinion, values or constitutional democracy. Rights in the Bill of Rights are not absolute. Section 36 provides that rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom.

The court uses a two-stage enquiry. First, it asks whether a right has been limited. Secondly, it asks whether the limitation is justified under s 36. The relevant factors are the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and less restrictive means. 7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Section 36 is the general limitation clause of the Constitution. It recognises that rights are not absolute, but also ensures that rights cannot be limited casually. A right may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom.

The correct approach is a two-stage enquiry. First, the court asks whether a right in the Bill of Rights has been infringed or limited. Secondly, if there is a limitation, the court asks whether it is justified under s 36. One should not jump to "reasonable and justifiable" without first identifying the right and the limitation.

Section 36 requires proportionality. The court considers the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between limitation and purpose, and whether less restrictive means could achieve the same purpose. A severe limitation of an important right requires stronger justification.

The leading case is S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC). It is important to note that Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993, not section 36 of the 1996 Constitution. It remains the foundational case for limitation analysis because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) substantially repeats the proportionality approach formulated in Makwanyane. In that case, Chaskalson P held that limitation involves weighing competing values and applying proportionality. The state relied on deterrence, prevention, retribution and public safety, but the Court held that the death penalty destroyed life, assaulted dignity, risked arbitrariness and error, and was not shown to be materially more effective than life imprisonment. Public opinion could not replace the Court's duty to uphold the Constitution.

Therefore, s 36 is both flexible and protective: it allows necessary limitations, but only where the limitation is legally authorised, legitimate, rational, proportionate and consistent with dignity, equality and freedom.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

Section 36 is the general limitation clause of the Constitution. It reflects the idea that rights in the Bill of Rights are fundamental but not absolute. In a constitutional democracy, rights may sometimes be limited to protect other rights or important public purposes. However, because rights are central to dignity, equality and freedom, any limitation must be carefully justified.

Section 36 provides that rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom. This prevents rights from being balanced away casually and requires the court to ask whether the limitation is legally authorised, rational, proportionate and consistent with constitutional values.

The requirement of law of general application means that rights may not be limited by arbitrary or ad hoc exercises of power. The limitation must be authorised by a legal rule that applies generally and is consistent with the rule of law. This protects people from officials limiting rights merely because it is convenient.

The correct structure is a two-stage enquiry. First, the court asks whether a right in the Bill of Rights has been infringed or limited. Secondly, if there is a limitation, the court asks whether it is reasonable and justifiable under s 36. These stages must not be collapsed.

The proportionality enquiry considers all relevant factors, including the five listed in s 36. These are the nature of the right, the importance of the purpose of the limitation, the nature and extent of the limitation, the relation between the limitation and its purpose, and less restrictive means. The more important the right and the more severe the limitation, the stronger the justification must be. A measure that does not rationally achieve its purpose, or where a less restrictive effective alternative exists, is unlikely to be justified.

The First Certification Judgment 1996 (4) SA 744 (CC) confirms at paragraph 90 that rights limitation involves the weighing of competing values and an assessment based on proportionality, and that section 36(1) of the 1996 Constitution substantially repeats the approach formulated in S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC). Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution of 1993, but it remains the foundational authority for limitation analysis because section 36 substantially repeats its approach.

Makwanyane concerned the constitutionality of the death penalty. Makwanyane and Mchunu had been convicted on four counts of murder each and sentenced to death under s 277(1)(a) of the Criminal Procedure Act. The issue was whether the death penalty was compatible with the Interim Constitution and, if it limited rights such as life and dignity, whether that limitation was justified.

Chaskalson P held that limitation requires weighing competing values and applying proportionality. The Court considered the state's purposes, including deterrence, prevention, retribution and public safety. However, these had to be balanced against the rights and values at stake. The death penalty destroyed life, assaulted dignity, created risks of arbitrariness and error, and operated in a society shaped by historical social and racial inequality. The Court was also not persuaded that capital punishment was materially more effective than life imprisonment as a deterrent. The Court also rejected the idea that public opinion could decide the issue. Even if many members of the public supported the death penalty, courts have a duty to uphold the Constitution. This shows that South Africa is a constitutional democracy, not a system of unlimited majority rule.

Critically, s 36 is necessary because rights can conflict with other rights and legitimate public interests. Without a limitation clause, rights could become rigid and unworkable. But limitation is also dangerous because vague appeals to public interest, safety or morality can weaken rights. Section 36 therefore operates as both a permission and a protection: it permits justified limitations, but requires strong constitutional reasons before rights may be restricted.

In conclusion, section 36 is central to South African constitutional adjudication because it turns rights limitation into a structured justification enquiry. A limitation must be authorised by law of general application, must pursue a legitimate and important purpose, must be rationally connected to that purpose, must impair the right no more than is constitutionally acceptable, and must be proportionate in light of dignity, equality and freedom. Makwanyane, applied through section 36, remains the leading authority because it shows how proportionality, values and constitutional democracy operate in practice.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

The rule of law means that public power must be exercised according to law and not arbitrary will. It is a founding value in section 1(c) of the Constitution, together with constitutional supremacy. It requires legality, rationality, accountability and respect for constitutional limits.

The principle of legality is the minimum requirement of the rule of law. It means that every exercise of public power must have legal authority and must be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose. Fedsure confirms that public power must be lawfully authorised, while Pharmaceutical Manufacturers confirms that public power must be rational.

In EFF v Speaker 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court, and that the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with section 83(b) read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and invalid. The National Assembly's resolution absolving the President was inconsistent with sections 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside. This shows that even the President and Parliament must comply with constitutional obligations.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

The rule of law is a founding value of South Africa's constitutional democracy under section 1(c) of the Constitution. It requires public power to be exercised according to law and not arbitrary will. It is closely linked to constitutional supremacy in section 2, because all law and conduct must comply with the Constitution.

The principle of legality is the minimum enforceable requirement of the rule of law. It requires all exercises of public power to have legal authority, remain within the limits of that authority, and be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose. Fedsure Life Assurance Ltd v Greater Johannesburg Transitional Metropolitan Council 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC) confirms that public power must be lawfully authorised, while Pharmaceutical Manufacturers Association of SA: In re Ex parte President of the RSA 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC) confirms that rationality is a minimum requirement of legality.

10. What to Memorise

THEME 4 ESSAY BANK 6

Rule of Law, Principle of Legality and EFF v Speaker: Public Power Must Be Lawful, Rational and Accountable

2. Core Thesis

The rule of law is a founding value of South Africa's constitutional democracy which requires all public power to be authorised by law, exercised rationally, and subjected to constitutional accountability; the principle of legality is its minimum enforceable expression, while EFF v Speaker shows that even the President and National Assembly must obey constitutional obligations and binding remedial action.

3. Key Authorities to Remember

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: why rule of law matters

The rule of law is one of the foundational values of South Africa's constitutional democracy. It is listed in section 1(c) together with constitutional supremacy. This tells us something important: the rule of law is not just an ordinary legal doctrine. It is part of the foundation of the Republic.

In the South African context, the rule of law responds to a history where law was used to authorise racial domination, repression and abuse of public power. The new constitutional order requires more than obedience to legal form. Public power must now be authorised by law, rational, accountable and consistent with the Constitution.

Theme 4's source map identifies EFF v Speaker as authority for the rule of law, Public Protector remedial action, Chapter 9 institutions, accountability, separation of powers and constitutional obligations.

B. Definition of the rule of law

The rule of law means that public power must be exercised according to law, not arbitrary will. It requires legality, rationality, accountability and respect for constitutional limits.

Put simply: Government may only do what the law authorises, and it must exercise that power rationally and accountably.

The rule of law is closely linked to constitutional supremacy. Section 1(c) makes both the supremacy of the Constitution and the rule of law founding values. Section 2 gives this practical force by providing that the Constitution is supreme, inconsistent law or conduct is invalid, and constitutional obligations must be fulfilled.

C. Principle of legality

The principle of legality is the minimum enforceable requirement of the rule of law. It means that every exercise of public power must:

have a source in law;

remain within the limits of that legal power;

be rationally connected to a legitimate governmental purpose.

Theme 4 expressly describes legality as a minimum requirement of the rule of law, even where no specific right is directly invoked. State action must still have legal authority, the decision-maker must act within power, and the action must be rational.

Exam sentence: The rule of law is the broader constitutional value; legality is the concrete doctrine courts use to test whether public power is authorised and rational.

D. Rule of law vs legality

ConceptMeaningExam useRule of lawBroad constitutional value requiring lawfulness, rationality, accountability and non-arbitrarinessUse for s 1(c), constitutional democracy, accountabilityLegalityMinimum legal requirement that public power must be authorised by law and rationally related to a legitimate purposeUse for Fedsure, Pharmaceutical Manufacturers, executive/public power

E. Fedsure: public power must be lawfully authorised

In Fedsure Life Assurance Ltd v Greater Johannesburg Transitional Metropolitan Council 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC), the Constitutional Court developed legality as an incident of the rule of law. The key principle is that public bodies may exercise only those powers lawfully conferred on them.

Theme 4 summarises Fedsure as a foundational legality and rule of law case: public power must be lawful and constitutionally authorised.

Exam use: Use when the issue is whether a public body had the power to act, whether government acted outside its powers, or whether public power was legally authorised.

F. Pharmaceutical Manufacturers: public power must be rational

In Pharmaceutical Manufacturers Association of SA: In re Ex parte President of the RSA 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC), the Constitutional Court confirmed that public power must be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose.

Theme 4 summarises the case as authority for rationality as a minimum requirement of legality, especially in relation to executive power and constitutional supremacy.

Exam use: Use when the issue is rationality, executive power, public power with no rational connection to its purpose, or legality even without a specific rights infringement.

G. Accountability and Chapter 9 institutions

The rule of law is not only about courts. It is also supported by constitutional institutions designed to hold public power accountable. Chapter 9 institutions support constitutional democracy. Section 181(2) provides that Chapter 9 institutions are independent and subject only to the Constitution and the law. Section 181(3) requires other organs of state to assist and protect them to ensure their independence, impartiality, dignity and effectiveness.

Section 182(1)(c) gives the Public Protector power to take appropriate remedial action. EFF v Speaker is the key case explaining that this remedial action is binding unless set aside by a court. The Constitutional Court described the Public Protector as a mechanism giving even poor and marginalised people a voice and "teeth" against corruption and abuse, and emphasised that state power and resources belong to the public and must be used for the public benefit.

H. EFF v Speaker: the key case

Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC)

Facts: The Public Protector investigated security upgrades at President Jacob Zuma's private residence at Nkandla. She found that some features were non-security related and that the President and his family had unduly benefited. She ordered remedial action, including that the President repay a reasonable portion of the costs and that the ministers involved be reprimanded.

For well over one year, neither the President nor the National Assembly did what they were required to do in terms of the remedial action. Instead, the National Assembly adopted a resolution that effectively absolved the President of liability. The Economic Freedom Fighters and the Democratic Alliance approached the Constitutional Court.

Issue: The main issues were:

Was the Public Protector's remedial action binding?

Did the President breach his constitutional obligations by failing to comply?

Did the National Assembly breach its constitutional obligations by failing to hold the President accountable?

Could the National Assembly validly set aside or ignore the Public Protector's remedial action?

Court reasoning: The Constitutional Court, per Mogoeng CJ, linked the case to South Africa's need to break from unchecked abuse of state power and resources. The Court stated that South Africa adopted accountability, the rule of law and constitutional supremacy as values of constitutional democracy, and that public office-bearers ignore constitutional obligations at their peril.

The Court reasoned that the Public Protector's remedial action is sourced in the Constitution. If remedial action could be ignored, the Public Protector would lose effectiveness and dignity. The rule of law requires legally authorised decisions to be obeyed unless they are set aside by a court.

The Court also held that the National Assembly has a constitutional obligation under ss 55(2)(a) and (b) to provide mechanisms for oversight and to hold the executive accountable. In EFF, the National Assembly resolved to absolve the President instead of facilitating enforcement of the Public Protector's remedial action.

Judgment: The Court held that:

the Public Protector's remedial action under s 182(1)(c) was binding unless set aside by a court;

the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with s 83(b), read with ss 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and was invalid;

the National Assembly's resolution absolving the President was inconsistent with ss 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b), and 181(3), invalid and set aside;

the President had to personally pay the amount determined by National Treasury and reprimand the ministers involved.

Constitutional significance: EFF v Speaker is crucial because it shows that:

the President is not above the Constitution;

Parliament is not above the Constitution;

Chapter 9 institutions have real constitutional force;

Public Protector remedial action is binding unless set aside by a court;

accountability is enforceable, not merely political;

the rule of law requires compliance with constitutional obligations;

separation of powers does not allow one branch to undermine the Constitution.

I. Separation of powers caution in EFF

A strong answer should not make it sound like courts can tell Parliament exactly how to do everything. The Court recognised that it falls outside judicial authority to prescribe exactly how the National Assembly must scrutinise executive action or which oversight mechanisms it must use. The mechanics of oversight are discretionary matters for the National Assembly. However, that discretion cannot be used to act contrary to the Constitution or to set aside binding remedial action.

Exam sentence: EFF respects separation of powers because the Court did not prescribe Parliament's oversight mechanisms, but it still enforced the constitutional boundary that Parliament may not use its discretion to undermine binding constitutional obligations.

J. Critical engagement: rule of law is necessary but not enough

For a 90% answer, include a critique. The rule of law is essential, but formal legality alone is not enough. A legal system may have clear rules and still be unjust if those rules serve oppression. Woolman and Swanepoel make this point: a commitment to the rule of law is a necessary but insufficient condition for a just society, because even an exploitative regime can use legal forms to disguise oppressive intent (Woolman and Swanepoel in De Vos and Freedman (eds) South African Constitutional Law in Context 2 ed (2021) ch 2). This is precisely what apartheid did.

This is why South Africa's rule of law must be understood together with:

constitutional supremacy;

dignity;

equality;

accountability;

rights;

openness;

transformative constitutionalism.

Exam sentence: The South African rule of law is not merely formal legality; it is legality within a supreme, rights-based and value-based constitutional order.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

The rule of law means that public power must be exercised according to law and not arbitrary will. It is a founding value in section 1(c) of the Constitution, together with constitutional supremacy. It requires legality, rationality, accountability and respect for constitutional limits.

The principle of legality is the minimum requirement of the rule of law. It means that every exercise of public power must have legal authority and must be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose. Fedsure confirms that public power must be lawfully authorised, while Pharmaceutical Manufacturers confirms that public power must be rational.

In EFF v Speaker 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court, and that the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with section 83(b) read with sections 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and invalid. The National Assembly's resolution absolving the President was inconsistent with sections 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside. This shows that even the President and Parliament must comply with constitutional obligations.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

The rule of law is a founding value of South Africa's constitutional democracy under section 1(c) of the Constitution. It requires public power to be exercised according to law and not arbitrary will. It is closely linked to constitutional supremacy in section 2, because all law and conduct must comply with the Constitution.

The principle of legality is the minimum enforceable requirement of the rule of law. It requires all exercises of public power to have legal authority, remain within the limits of that authority, and be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose. Fedsure Life Assurance Ltd v Greater Johannesburg Transitional Metropolitan Council 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC) confirms that public power must be lawfully authorised, while Pharmaceutical Manufacturers Association of SA: In re Ex parte President of the RSA 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC) confirms that rationality is a minimum requirement of legality.

Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC) is the leading case for rule of law, accountability and Chapter 9 institutions. The Public Protector investigated the Nkandla upgrades and ordered remedial action. The President failed to comply, and the National Assembly adopted a resolution absolving him. The Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action under s 182(1)(c) was binding unless set aside by a court, and the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with s 83(b) read with ss 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and the National Assembly's resolution was inconsistent with ss 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside.

The case shows that the rule of law is not abstract. It means constitutional obligations must be obeyed, even by the President and the National Assembly. It also shows that Chapter 9 institutions are not symbolic; they support constitutional democracy by enforcing accountability and checking abuse of public power.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

The rule of law is one of the founding values of South Africa's constitutional democracy. Section 1(c) lists the supremacy of the Constitution and the rule of law as founding values, while section 2 provides that the Constitution is supreme and inconsistent law or conduct is invalid. The rule of law therefore means that public power must be authorised by law, rational, accountable and limited by the Constitution.

This is especially important in South Africa because apartheid showed that law can be used as an instrument of oppression. The new constitutional order therefore requires more than formal obedience to legal rules. Public power must be exercised within a supreme, rights-based and value-based Constitution.

The rule of law means that public power must be exercised according to law, not arbitrary will. Theme 4 defines it as requiring legality, rationality, accountability and respect for constitutional limits. It ensures that public power cannot be exercised outside lawful authority or in an arbitrary manner.

The principle of legality is the minimum enforceable expression of the rule of law. It requires every exercise of public power to have a source in law, remain within the limits of that legal authority, and be rationally connected to a legitimate governmental purpose. Theme 4 explains that legality applies even where no specific right is directly invoked.

Fedsure Life Assurance Ltd v Greater Johannesburg Transitional Metropolitan Council 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC) confirms that public power must be lawful and constitutionally authorised. Pharmaceutical Manufacturers Association of SA: In re Ex parte President of the RSA 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC) confirms that the exercise of public power is legitimate only where it is lawful and rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose.

The rule of law is also supported by institutions of accountability, especially Chapter 9 institutions. Section 181(2) protects their independence and requires other organs of state to assist and protect them. Section 182(1)(c) empowers the Public Protector to take appropriate remedial action. These institutions are designed to support constitutional democracy by checking abuse of public power.

The leading case is Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC). The Public Protector investigated the Nkandla security upgrades and found that President Zuma had unduly benefited from non-security features. She ordered remedial action, including repayment and reprimands. The President did not comply, and the National Assembly adopted a resolution absolving him from liability.

The Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court. It held that the President's failure to comply was inconsistent with s 83(b), read with ss 181(3) and 182(1)(c), and invalid. It also held that the National Assembly's resolution was inconsistent with ss 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b), and 181(3), invalid and set aside. Mogoeng CJ emphasised that accountability, the rule of law and constitutional supremacy are foundational values, and that public office-bearers ignore constitutional obligations at their peril.

The case also respects separation of powers. The Court did not prescribe exactly how the National Assembly must conduct oversight, because the mechanics of oversight are within Parliament's discretion. However, the Court held that Parliament may not use that discretion to undermine the Constitution or set aside binding remedial action.

Critically, the rule of law is necessary but not enough. Woolman and Swanepoel argue that a commitment to the rule of law is a necessary but insufficient condition for a just society, because even an exploitative regime can use legal forms to disguise oppressive intent. This is precisely what apartheid demonstrated. This is why South African rule of law must be understood together with dignity, equality, constitutional supremacy, rights, accountability and transformation.

In conclusion, the rule of law and principle of legality ensure that public power is not arbitrary. Legality requires lawful authority and rationality, while the broader rule of law requires accountability and constitutional compliance. EFF v Speaker shows the practical force of these principles: even the President and National Assembly must obey the Constitution, and Chapter 9 institutions must be respected as real mechanisms of constitutional accountability.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

South Africa is primarily a representative constitutional democracy, but the Constitution also contains participatory and limited direct democratic features. Section 1(d) founds South Africa on universal adult suffrage, a national common voters roll, regular elections and a multi-party democratic system to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness.

It is representative because citizens elect representatives to govern on their behalf. Section 19 protects political rights, including voting and standing for public office, and section 42(3) says the National Assembly represents the people and ensures government by the people. It is participatory because sections 59, 72 and 118 require public involvement in legislative processes. It has limited direct democratic elements such as petitions and referenda under sections 17, 84(2)(g) and 127(2)(f). Therefore, South African democracy is not only voting; it is representative, participatory and constitutional.

Mark focus: s 1(d) + s 19 + s 42(3) + ss 59/72/118 + conclusion on type of democracy.

70/80/90+ upgrade guide

A 90% answer should also mention the tension between constitutional democracy and majority rule. Because South Africa is a constitutional democracy, courts may sometimes invalidate laws supported by elected representatives. This creates the counter-majoritarian dilemma. Theme 4's separation of powers lecture notes that judicial review under s 172 can involve declarations of invalidity and remedies, and judges can declare law supported by a majority invalid even though they are not elected representatives.

However, South African constitutional law generally treats judicial review as a necessary and legitimising limit within democracy. Majority politics is not necessarily fully representative; human rights protect everyone, including citizens, permanent residents and vulnerable groups; and courts can enhance democracy by enforcing participation, accountability and rights.

Exam sentence: Judicial review does not destroy democracy; in South Africa it helps constitutionalise democracy by ensuring that majority power remains faithful to rights, values, participation and accountability.

4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: democracy is more than voting

Democracy is one of the central ideas of the 1996 Constitution. However, South African democracy must not be reduced to voting every few years. The Constitution creates a richer democratic model that includes representative institutions, participatory processes, limited direct democratic mechanisms and constitutional limits on majority power.

The best exam answer is: South Africa is primarily a representative and constitutional democracy, but it is strengthened by participatory democracy and limited direct democratic features.

Theme 4 expressly warns that a strong answer to "Is South Africa direct, representative or participatory?" should say that South Africa is mainly a representative and constitutional democracy, with important participatory mechanisms and some direct democratic elements.

B. Definition of democracy: Theunis Roux

The lectures use Theunis Roux's idea that the core of democracy is that decisions affecting members of a political community should be taken by the members themselves, or at least by elected representatives whose power ultimately derives from those members. This definition captures both direct and representative democracy and lends itself to understanding that democracy, as found in the 1996 Constitution, embodies different conceptions.

Democracy can therefore operate in two main ways:

Directly — people make decisions themselves.

Representatively — people elect representatives to make decisions on their behalf.

But in South Africa, this is still incomplete unless we add:

Participatory democracy — people must have meaningful opportunities to be heard.

Constitutional democracy — all democratic power is limited by the Constitution.

C. Section 1(d): the constitutional foundation of democracy

Section 1(d) is the main constitutional foundation for democracy. It says South Africa is founded on:

universal adult suffrage;

a national common voters roll;

regular elections;

a multi-party system of democratic government;

accountability;

responsiveness;

openness.

Theme 4 defines section 1(d) in exactly this way and treats it as the central authority for South Africa's democratic state.

Exam sentence: Section 1(d) shows that democracy in South Africa is not only electoral; it also requires accountability, responsiveness and openness.

D. Direct democracy

Direct democracy means that members of the political community make decisions themselves. It is often described as the purest form of democracy because the people do not act through representatives.

South Africa has limited direct democratic elements, but it is not mainly a direct democracy.

Constitutional provisionDirect democratic features 17Citizens may assemble, demonstrate, picket and present petitions peacefully and unarmeds 84(2)(g)President may call a national referendums 127(2)(f)Premier may call a provincial referendum

Theme 4 expressly states that direct democracy involves members themselves making decisions and that South Africa has elements of direct democracy, but not a strong substantive direct democracy system.

Common exam trap: Do not say "South Africa is a direct democracy." Say: South Africa contains limited direct democratic mechanisms, but its dominant democratic form is representative, participatory and constitutional.

E. Representative democracy

Representative democracy is the dominant institutional form of South African democracy. It means citizens do not usually make laws themselves. Instead, they elect representatives who exercise public power through constitutional institutions.

Section 42(3) says the National Assembly is elected to represent the people and to ensure government by the people under the Constitution. Section 19(3)(a) gives every adult citizen the right to vote in elections. Section 1(d) confirms universal adult suffrage, regular elections and a multi-party system.

Theme 4 explains that representative democracy exists because most decisions are not made directly by the citizenry; citizens elect representatives to govern for a limited time, and the National Assembly represents the people and ensures government by the people.

F. Multi-party democracy and electoral reform

South Africa's representative democracy is also a multi-party democracy. Section 1(d) expressly refers to a multi-party system of democratic government.

Historically, political parties were the main way citizens were represented in Parliament, especially under proportional representation. But this model raised questions about whether citizens could participate outside party structures.

New Nation Movement NPC v President of the Republic of South Africa 2020 (6) SA 325 (CC)

The Electoral Act had allowed only political parties to contest national and provincial elections. The issue was whether excluding independent candidates violated political rights under section 19. The Constitutional Court held that excluding independent candidates violated citizens' political rights and declared the Electoral Act unconstitutional to that extent.

The case is significant because it expanded democratic participation beyond party-based representation and led to electoral reform.

Electoral Amendment Act 1 of 2023 allows independent candidates not affiliated with political parties to contest elections for the National Assembly and provincial legislatures. It came into effect for the 2024 elections.

Exam sentence: New Nation Movement shows that representative democracy is not frozen in a party-only model; political rights under section 19 may require electoral arrangements that allow citizens to stand as independent candidates.

G. Limits of representative democracy

A strong answer should not treat representative democracy as perfect. Theme 4 identifies several limits:

political parties can become elite-driven;

citizens may struggle to influence elected representatives between elections;

those not active in political parties may have limited influence;

one-party dominance can weaken democratic accountability;

party dominance may blur the line between party and state;

electoral reform helps, but does not automatically solve accountability problems.

Exam sentence: Representative democracy is necessary in a modern state, but it must be supported by participation, accountability and constitutional limits to prevent democracy from becoming merely elite-driven party control.

H. Participatory democracy

Participatory democracy means citizens must have opportunities to be heard when decisions are made that affect them. It does not mean the public always makes the final decision. It means public institutions must create meaningful opportunities for involvement.

ProvisionInstitutionDutys 59(1)(a)National AssemblyMust facilitate public involvement in legislative and other processess 72(1)(a)NCOPMust facilitate public involvements 118(1)(a)Provincial legislaturesMust facilitate public involvements 152(1)(e)Local governmentMust encourage community involvement

Theme 4 defines participatory democracy as giving citizens an opportunity to have their voices heard when decisions affect them, and explains that participatory democracy tries to counteract political exclusion from decision-making.

I. Doctors for Life: key case on public participation

Doctors for Life International v Speaker of the National Assembly 2006 (6) SA 416 (CC)

Facts: The case concerned whether there had been sufficient public participation in legislative processes affecting people's lives, specifically certain health-related legislation.

Issue: Whether the Constitution requires public participation in law-making.

Court reasoning: The Court held that South Africa has both representative and participatory democracy, and that these two forms are mutually supportive. Public participation gives vitality to representative democracy.

Judgment: Legislation was invalidated where there had not been sufficient opportunity for public participation.

Constitutional significance: Foundational case for participatory democracy and public involvement in law-making.

Exam sentence: Doctors for Life shows that South African democracy is not exhausted by elections; law-making must include meaningful public involvement where the Constitution requires it.

J. Constitutional democracy

South Africa is not just a representative democracy. It is a constitutional democracy.

Constitutional democracy means democratic power is exercised under a supreme Constitution and limited by rights, values, judicial review and the rule of law.

Theme 4 defines constitutional democracy as a system where decisions affecting a political community are made in terms of a Constitution with features such as constitutional supremacy, founding values, human rights, democracy and judicial review.

This matters because majority rule is not unlimited. Even elected representatives may not pass laws or take action that violates the Constitution. Democratic power is subject to:

constitutional supremacy under s 2;

rights under the Bill of Rights;

limitation analysis under s 36;

interpretation through values under s 39;

judicial review and remedies under s 172.

Exam sentence: Constitutional democracy means the people govern through democratic institutions, but always under the Constitution.

K. Historical and transformative significance

Democracy in the 1996 Constitution is historically significant because apartheid denied political rights to the majority of South Africans. Woolman and Swanepoel explain that the Final Constitution's entrenchment of direct, participatory and representative democracy reflects how South Africa has overcome some of apartheid's depredations, including the wholesale denial of basic democratic rights to most of the population.

This historical point is useful because it shows that democracy in South Africa is not a neutral technical concept. It is part of the constitutional break from apartheid exclusion.

Exam sentence: Democracy under the 1996 Constitution is transformative because it responds to apartheid's exclusion by creating universal adult suffrage, political rights, representative institutions, participation and constitutional limits on public power.

L. Critical engagement: democracy and judicial review

A 90% answer should also mention the tension between constitutional democracy and majority rule. Because South Africa is a constitutional democracy, courts may sometimes invalidate laws supported by elected representatives. This creates the counter-majoritarian dilemma. Theme 4's separation of powers lecture notes that judicial review under s 172 can involve declarations of invalidity and remedies, and judges can declare law supported by a majority invalid even though they are not elected representatives.

However, South African constitutional law generally treats judicial review as a necessary and legitimising limit within democracy. Majority politics is not necessarily fully representative; human rights protect everyone, including citizens, permanent residents and vulnerable groups; and courts can enhance democracy by enforcing participation, accountability and rights.

Exam sentence: Judicial review does not destroy democracy; in South Africa it helps constitutionalise democracy by ensuring that majority power remains faithful to rights, values, participation and accountability.

5. Case-Law Blocks

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

South Africa is primarily a representative constitutional democracy, but the Constitution also contains participatory and limited direct democratic features. Section 1(d) founds South Africa on universal adult suffrage, a national common voters roll, regular elections and a multi-party democratic system to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness.

It is representative because citizens elect representatives to govern on their behalf. Section 19 protects political rights, including voting and standing for public office, and section 42(3) says the National Assembly represents the people and ensures government by the people. It is participatory because sections 59, 72 and 118 require public involvement in legislative processes. It has limited direct democratic elements such as petitions and referenda under sections 17, 84(2)(g) and 127(2)(f). Therefore, South African democracy is not only voting; it is representative, participatory and constitutional.

Mark focus: s 1(d) + s 19 + s 42(3) + ss 59/72/118 + conclusion on type of democracy.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

South Africa is best described as a constitutional and representative democracy, strengthened by participatory requirements and limited direct democratic mechanisms. The lectures use Theunis Roux's definition that democracy means decisions affecting members of a political community should be taken by the members themselves, or at least by elected representatives whose power ultimately derives from those members.

The main constitutional foundation is section 1(d), which founds South Africa on universal adult suffrage, a national common voters roll, regular elections and a multi-party democratic system to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. This shows that democracy is not merely electoral; it also requires accountable and responsive government.

South Africa has limited direct democracy through mechanisms such as the right to assemble, demonstrate, picket and petition under s 17, and the President's and Premiers' powers to call referenda under ss 84(2)(g) and 127(2)(f). However, South Africa is not primarily a direct democracy.

It is mainly a representative democracy because citizens elect representatives to govern for a limited time. Section 19 gives political rights, including the right to vote and stand for public office. Section 42(3) says the National Assembly represents the people and ensures government by the people under the Constitution. In New Nation Movement NPC v President of the Republic of South Africa 2020 (6) SA 325 (CC), the Court held that excluding independent candidates from elections violated political rights under s 19, showing that representative democracy must be open to individual participation beyond political parties.

It is also a participatory democracy because the National Assembly, NCOP and provincial legislatures must facilitate public involvement under ss 59, 72 and 118. In Doctors for Life International v Speaker of the National Assembly 2006 (6) SA 416 (CC), the Court held that representative and participatory democracy are mutually supportive and that public participation gives vitality to representative democracy.

Finally, South Africa is a constitutional democracy because democratic power is limited by constitutional supremacy, rights, values and judicial review. Democracy is therefore not unlimited majority rule; it is government by the people under the Constitution.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

South Africa is best described as a constitutional and representative democracy, enriched by participatory democracy and limited direct democratic mechanisms. Democracy under the 1996 Constitution is not merely the holding of elections every few years. It includes universal suffrage, political rights, elected representation, public participation, accountability, responsiveness, openness and constitutional limits on majority power.

The lectures use Theunis Roux's definition of democracy: decisions affecting members of a political community should be taken by the members themselves, or at least by elected representatives whose power ultimately derives from those members. This definition captures direct and representative democracy, but the South African Constitution goes further by also requiring participation and constitutional limits.

The main constitutional foundation is section 1(d). It provides that South Africa is founded on universal adult suffrage, a national common voters roll, regular elections and a multi-party system of democratic government to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. This shows that South African democracy is electoral, but also accountable, responsive and open.

Direct democracy means that members of the political community make decisions themselves. South Africa has some direct democratic elements, but it is not mainly a direct democracy. Examples include the right to assemble, demonstrate, picket and present petitions peacefully and unarmed under s 17, the President's power to call a national referendum under s 84(2)(g), and a Premier's power to call a provincial referendum under s 127(2)(f). Theme 4 warns that the better answer is that South Africa has direct democratic elements, but its main system is representative, participatory and constitutional.

Representative democracy is the dominant institutional form. Citizens elect representatives who exercise public power through constitutional institutions. Section 19 protects political rights, including the right to vote and stand for public office. Section 42(3) states that the National Assembly is elected to represent the people and ensure government by the people under the Constitution. Political parties have historically been central to South Africa's proportional representation system, but representative democracy is not frozen in that mould.

South Africa is also a multi-party democracy. Section 1(d) expressly refers to a multi-party system of democratic government, supporting political pluralism and preventing the democratic system from being reduced to one party or one political voice. However, representative democracy has limits. Theme 4 notes that political parties may become elite-driven, citizens may have limited influence between elections, and one-party dominance can weaken accountability or blur the line between party and state.

New Nation Movement NPC v President of the Republic of South Africa 2020 (6) SA 325 (CC) is important for representative democracy and political rights. The Electoral Act had allowed only political parties to contest national and provincial elections. The Constitutional Court held that excluding independent candidates violated citizens' political rights under s 19 and declared the Electoral Act unconstitutional to that extent. This led to the Electoral Amendment Act 1 of 2023, which now allows independent candidates to contest National Assembly and provincial legislature elections.

Participatory democracy means citizens must have meaningful opportunities to be heard in decisions that affect them. It does not mean the public always makes the final decision. Instead, public bodies must facilitate meaningful involvement. The key provisions are s 59(1)(a) for the National Assembly, s 72(1)(a) for the NCOP and s 118(1)(a) for provincial legislatures. Theme 4 defines participatory democracy as giving citizens an opportunity to have their voices heard when decisions affect them.

The leading case is Doctors for Life International v Speaker of the National Assembly 2006 (6) SA 416 (CC). The Court held that representative and participatory democracy are mutually supportive and that public participation gives vitality to representative democracy. Legislation was invalidated where there had not been sufficient opportunity for public participation. This case shows that democracy is not exhausted by voting; it also requires continuing public involvement in law-making.

South Africa is finally a constitutional democracy. This means democratic power is exercised under a supreme Constitution and limited by rights, values, judicial review and the rule of law. Democracy is therefore not unlimited majority rule. Elected institutions must comply with the Constitution, including s 2, the Bill of Rights, s 36, s 39 and judicial review under s 172. Theme 4 defines constitutional democracy as decision-making under a Constitution with features such as constitutional supremacy, founding values, human rights, democracy and judicial review.

Critically, constitutional democracy creates a tension between democracy and judicial review. Courts may invalidate laws supported by elected representatives, raising the counter-majoritarian difficulty. Theme 4 notes that this is a concern because judges are not democratically elected representatives. However, judicial review can also enhance democracy by ensuring that majority power remains subject to rights, participation, accountability and constitutional values.

In conclusion, South Africa should not be described as only direct or only representative democracy. It is primarily a representative constitutional democracy, enriched by participatory obligations and limited direct democratic mechanisms. Its democratic model responds to apartheid's exclusion by combining universal suffrage, political rights, elected institutions, public participation, rights protection and constitutional limits on majority power.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Separation of powers is a constitutional doctrine that divides public power between the legislature, executive and judiciary. It is a procedural limit on public power because it regulates which institution may act, how it may act, and how it may be checked. The three branches are not a hierarchy.

The doctrine has four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. South Africa does not follow a rigid classical model. The Final Constitution does not contain a single express "separation of powers" clause, but the doctrine is textually embedded in sections 43, 85 and 165, which separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions. The legislature and executive overlap because Cabinet is drawn largely from Parliament, but judicial independence must be protected. Chapter 9 institutions also add another layer of checks and balances. Therefore, South Africa has a flexible and contextual model of separation of powers.

Mark focus: definition + four principles + South African flexible model + s 165 judicial independence.

Answer:

Separation of powers is a procedural doctrine that limits public power by dividing authority between the legislature, executive and judiciary. It prevents concentration of power, promotes institutional specialisation and supports accountability. The three branches are not a hierarchy — each has distinct functions and constitutional limits.

The doctrine rests on four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. The legislature makes law, the executive implements law and policy, and the judiciary interprets and applies law. Checks and balances ensure that no branch acts without constitutional control.

2. Core Thesis

Separation of powers is a procedural doctrine that limits public power by dividing authority between the legislature, executive and judiciary, while creating checks and balances to prevent abuse. In South Africa, the doctrine is flexible and contextual rather than rigid: the legislature and executive overlap, judicial independence is strongly protected, Chapter 9 institutions add an extra accountability layer, and courts must enforce constitutional supremacy while showing appropriate deference to the political branches.
