4. Full Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: separation of powers as a limit on public power

Separation of powers is a constitutional doctrine that limits public power by dividing governmental authority between different institutions. It is part of constitutionalism because it prevents public power from being concentrated in one person or institution. This matters especially in South Africa because colonialism and apartheid showed how concentrated state power can be used to oppress, exclude and control.

Theme 4 explains that constitutions limit public power in both substantive and procedural ways. Rights are substantive limits because they restrict what the state may do to people. Separation of powers is a procedural limit because it controls the way public power is exercised, which institution may act, and how institutions check one another.

Exam sentence: Separation of powers does not only ask whether government may act; it asks which branch may act, how it may act, and which institution may check that action.

B. Definition of separation of powers

Separation of powers divides government into three branches:

Legislature — makes law.

Executive — implements law and policy.

Judiciary — interprets and applies law, resolves disputes and enforces the Constitution.

Theme 4 states that separation of powers divides government into the executive, legislature and judiciary, and emphasises that the branches are not a hierarchy.

This is important. Separation of powers does not mean the judiciary is "above" Parliament, or that Parliament is "above" the courts. It means each branch has distinct constitutional functions and must act within constitutional limits.

C. Purpose of separation of powers

The doctrine has three main purposes:

PurposeExplanationAvoid concentration of powerNo single branch should monopolise state power.Promote specialisationEach branch performs functions suited to its institutional competence.Ensure accountabilityBranches check, scrutinise and hold one another accountable.

Theme 4 explains that separation of powers avoids concentration of power, promotes specialisation, and links modern separation of powers closely to human rights because procedural limits help protect substantive rights.

D. The four principles of separation of powers

PrincipleMeaningExam explanationDivision of government powerPower is allocated between different branches.Prevents one institution from holding all public power.Separation of functionsDifferent branches perform different core tasks.Legislature makes law; executive implements; judiciary adjudicates.Separation of personnelThe same persons should not completely control all branches.SA has some overlap between legislature and executive, but judicial personnel must remain independent.Checks and balancesBranches may monitor, scrutinise or control each other.Parliament oversees executive; courts conduct judicial review; Chapter 9 institutions support accountability.

Exam sentence: The doctrine is not only about separating institutions; it is also about giving them constitutional tools to check abuse of power.

E. Classical model vs South African model

The classical model imagines three distinct branches with clear separation between them — sometimes called the trias politica. However, South Africa does not follow a rigid or absolute separation. The South African model is flexible, contextual and historically shaped.

If an exam asks whether South Africa follows separation of powers, the answer is: Yes, but not in a rigid classical sense. South Africa follows a flexible constitutional model with overlap, checks and balances, judicial independence and Chapter 9 accountability institutions.

F. The textual basis: separation of powers in the 1996 Constitution

The Final Constitution does not contain a single provision expressly labelled "separation of powers," but the doctrine is embedded in its structure. Section 43 vests legislative authority separately in Parliament at the national level, provincial legislatures, and municipal councils. Section 85 vests national executive authority in the President together with Cabinet. Section 165 vests judicial authority in the courts. These three provisions separately allocate the core functions of government to distinct institutions, giving the doctrine a clear textual foundation.

Constitutional Principle VI of the Interim Constitution also required separation of powers with appropriate checks and balances to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. The Constitutional Court has consistently treated the doctrine as a fundamental principle of the 1996 constitutional order, and this links directly to section 1(d) of the 1996 Constitution, which protects accountability, responsiveness and openness as founding values.

G. Legislature in South Africa

The legislature is mainly governed by Chapter 4 of the Constitution. Parliament consists of the National Assembly and the National Council of Provinces under s 42. Legislative authority at the national level is dealt with in s 43.

The legislature makes law, represents the people, deliberates on public issues, holds the executive accountable and exercises oversight. The National Assembly's oversight function is especially important. Section 55(2) requires the National Assembly to provide mechanisms to ensure that executive organs of state are accountable to it and to maintain oversight over the exercise of national executive authority.

Theme 4 notes that the National Assembly exercises oversight, can hold the executive accountable, and can initiate serious political accountability mechanisms such as votes of no confidence or impeachment processes.

H. Executive in South Africa

The executive is governed mainly by Chapter 5, especially ss 84 and 85. Section 85 vests executive authority in the President, exercised together with Cabinet.

The executive develops and implements policy, administers state affairs, prepares and implements legislation, coordinates government, and exercises powers such as commissions of enquiry. There is overlap between the legislature and executive because Cabinet ministers are generally drawn from the National Assembly. Theme 4 explains that this overlap is inherited from the Westminster system and reflects the policy-to-legislation relationship.

This overlap is important for exams: South Africa does not have a strict separation of personnel between legislature and executive.

I. Judiciary in South Africa

The judiciary is governed by Chapter 8. Section 165 provides that judicial authority is vested in the courts, courts are independent and subject only to the Constitution and the law, and organs of state must assist and protect the courts to ensure their independence, impartiality, dignity, accessibility and effectiveness.

The judiciary decides disputes, interprets law, applies the Constitution, reviews law and conduct and issues binding orders. Judicial review is central to the judiciary's role. Section 172 requires courts to declare law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution invalid to the extent of its inconsistency. Section 167(5) gives the Constitutional Court final authority on whether an Act of Parliament, provincial Act or conduct of the President is constitutional.

J. Judicial independence

Judicial independence is especially protected because courts must be able to decide disputes without fear, favour or political pressure. Theme 4 says there is no overlap with the judiciary in the same way as between the legislature and executive because judicial independence must be protected.

Judicial independence is supported by section 165, the judicial appointment process under s 174, the limited grounds for removal under s 177 (incapacity, gross incompetence or gross misconduct), and Judicial Service Commission involvement.

South African Association of Personal Injury Lawyers v Heath 2001 (1) SA 883 (CC)

The Constitutional Court confirmed that appointing a judge to perform executive-type investigative functions may threaten judicial independence and separation of powers. Theme 4 specifically mentions this case in relation to judicial independence and impartiality.

Exam use: Use SAAPIL v Heath to show that judicial independence is not only about judges deciding cases; it also prevents judges from being placed in roles that compromise the judiciary's institutional independence.

K. Delegation and flexibility: Executive Council Western Cape

In Executive Council of the Western Cape Legislature v President of the Republic of South Africa 1995 (4) SA 877 (CC), the Court considered delegation of law-making authority. Theme 4 explains that delegation may be permissible to some degree, but its constitutionality depends on the nature and extent of the delegation.

This case shows that separation of powers is flexible but not unlimited. Delegation may support efficiency and specialisation, but excessive delegation may undermine democracy by allowing the executive to take over core legislative power.

Exam sentence: Executive Council Western Cape shows that South African separation of powers permits practical overlap, but still guards against excessive concentration of law-making power in the executive.

L. De Lange v Smuts: no universal model

In De Lange v Smuts NO 1998 (3) SA 785 (CC), the Constitutional Court recognised separation of powers as part of South Africa's constitutional structure, but not as a rigid universal formula. The South African model must be understood with reference to the text, history and structure of the Constitution.

Theme 4 uses De Lange for the proposition that South Africa's separation of powers must be understood contextually and historically, not by mechanically importing a foreign model.

Exam sentence: De Lange supports the view that separation of powers is not a rigid formula; its content depends on each constitutional system's history, text and institutional design.

M. Chapter 9 institutions as an extra accountability layer

Chapter 9 institutions complicate the classical three-branch model. They do not fit neatly into the legislature-executive-judiciary triangle. They are independent institutions supporting constitutional democracy.

Examples include the Public Protector, the South African Human Rights Commission, the Commission for Gender Equality, the Auditor-General, and the Electoral Commission. Section 181 provides that these institutions are independent and subject only to the Constitution and the law, and that organs of state must assist and protect them. Theme 4 says Chapter 9 institutions are a unique set of institutions outside the classic model, exercise oversight, occupy a unique position, and introduce another layer of checks and balances.

Link to EFF v Speaker: In Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Public Protector ordered remedial action against President Zuma. The President did not comply, and the National Assembly adopted a resolution absolving him. The Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court, and that both the President and National Assembly failed to fulfil constitutional obligations. Theme 4 explains that EFF links rule of law, Chapter 9 institutions, parliamentary oversight, executive accountability and judicial enforcement.

N. Checks and balances

Checks and balances are the mechanisms through which branches and institutions control, supervise or hold one another accountable.

CheckExplanationParliament over executiveNational Assembly oversight under s 55(2)Courts over law/conductJudicial review under s 172Constitutional Court over legislation/presidential conductFinal authority under s 167(5)Chapter 9 institutions over public powerPublic Protector, Auditor-General, SAHRC and othersExecutive accountability to ParliamentQuestions, committees, no confidence, impeachmentJudicial removal processLimited removal grounds and JSC involvement under s 177

O. Counter-majoritarian dilemma

The counter-majoritarian dilemma is the tension created when unelected judges invalidate laws or conduct by elected representatives. This is unavoidable in a system of constitutional supremacy. Under s 172, courts may declare law or conduct invalid. This means courts can invalidate law supported by a democratic majority. Theme 4 explains that judicial review can require declarations of invalidity and remedies, and this creates practical problems because judges can affect law despite not being democratically elected representatives.

Why it is a problem:

Judges are not elected.

Courts may invalidate decisions of elected branches.

Courts may appear to "make law" through remedies.

Judicial decisions may conflict with political will.

Why judicial review is still justified: Theme 4 gives several responses: safeguards exist such as stare decisis, s 39, appeal and review; majoritarian politics is not always truly representative; South Africa has pluralism and proportional representation; human rights protect everyone, including minorities and vulnerable groups; and judicial review can be a necessary and legitimising limit within democracy.

Exam sentence: The counter-majoritarian dilemma is real, but in South Africa judicial review is generally treated as a necessary and legitimising feature of constitutional democracy rather than a contradiction of democracy.

P. Deference

Deference means that courts should respect the institutional competence and democratic role of the legislature and executive, while still enforcing the Constitution. Deference does not mean courts must avoid difficult constitutional questions. It means courts must enforce constitutional boundaries without taking over policy-making or administration where the Constitution leaves choices to the political branches.

Theme 4 defines deference as the idea that courts should respect the institutional competence and democratic role of other branches while still enforcing the Constitution. It warns that courts must not abdicate their role, but must also avoid prescribing policy details where discretion belongs to another branch.

EFF v Speaker and deference: The Court held that the National Assembly failed in its constitutional obligations, but it also recognised that courts should not prescribe exactly how the National Assembly must conduct oversight or design its internal mechanisms. The Court enforced the constitutional boundary without taking over Parliament's internal accountability design.

Exam sentence: Deference requires courts to respect institutional roles, but not to permit constitutional violations in the name of institutional respect.

Q. Critical engagement

A high-mark answer must avoid treating separation of powers as rigid isolation. South Africa's model requires balance: too much separation can prevent effective government; too much overlap can concentrate power; too much judicial intervention can threaten democratic legitimacy; too much judicial deference can allow constitutional violations; Chapter 9 institutions strengthen accountability but may create institutional tensions.

The correct South African approach is contextual. Courts must ask what the Constitution requires, what function each institution performs, and whether the conduct threatens accountability, independence, democracy or constitutional supremacy.

Exam sentence: The South African doctrine of separation of powers is not a wall between branches; it is a constitutional design for coordinated but limited power, institutional accountability and protection against abuse.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

Separation of powers is a constitutional doctrine that divides public power between the legislature, executive and judiciary. It is a procedural limit on public power because it regulates which institution may act, how it may act, and how it may be checked. The three branches are not a hierarchy.

The doctrine has four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. South Africa does not follow a rigid classical model. The Final Constitution does not contain a single express "separation of powers" clause, but the doctrine is textually embedded in sections 43, 85 and 165, which separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions. The legislature and executive overlap because Cabinet is drawn largely from Parliament, but judicial independence must be protected. Chapter 9 institutions also add another layer of checks and balances. Therefore, South Africa has a flexible and contextual model of separation of powers.

Mark focus: definition + four principles + South African flexible model + s 165 judicial independence.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

Separation of powers is a procedural doctrine that limits public power by dividing authority between the legislature, executive and judiciary. It prevents concentration of power, promotes institutional specialisation and supports accountability. The three branches are not a hierarchy — each has distinct functions and constitutional limits.

The doctrine rests on four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. The legislature makes law, the executive implements law and policy, and the judiciary interprets and applies law. Checks and balances ensure that no branch acts without constitutional control.

South Africa does not follow a rigid classical model. The Final Constitution does not contain a single express clause using the words "separation of powers," but the doctrine is textually embedded: sections 43, 85 and 165 separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions. Constitutional Principle VI of the Interim Constitution also required separation of powers with checks and balances to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. The Constitutional Court has consistently applied the doctrine as implicit in the Final Constitution.

The South African model is flexible. There is overlap between the legislature and executive because Cabinet ministers are generally drawn from Parliament. However, judicial independence is strongly protected by s 165. Courts enforce constitutional supremacy through judicial review under s 172 and the Constitutional Court has final authority under s 167(5). In De Lange v Smuts NO 1998 (3) SA 785 (CC), the Court confirmed that there is no single universal model of separation of powers and that the South African model must be understood contextually.

Chapter 9 institutions also complicate the classical model because they fall outside the ordinary three branches but support constitutional democracy. Theme 4 explains that these institutions exercise oversight and add another layer of checks and balances.

Therefore, South Africa's separation of powers is not strict isolation. It is a flexible constitutional system of divided authority, institutional overlap, judicial independence and accountability.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

Separation of powers is central to South African constitutionalism because it limits public power, prevents concentration of authority and supports accountability. South Africa does not follow a rigid classical model. Instead, it has a flexible and contextual model shaped by constitutional supremacy, democratic accountability, Westminster inheritance, judicial independence and Chapter 9 institutions.

Separation of powers divides public power between the legislature, executive and judiciary. The legislature makes law, the executive implements law and policy, and the judiciary interprets and applies law. Theme 4 describes separation of powers as a procedural limit on public power because it regulates the way government exercises power, rather than only the substantive content of what government may do.

The doctrine has four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. These principles work together. Separation prevents power from being concentrated, while checks and balances ensure that branches can scrutinise and control one another.

Although the Final Constitution does not contain a single express "separation of powers" clause, the doctrine has a clear textual foundation: section 43 vests legislative authority in Parliament, provincial legislatures and municipal councils; section 85 vests executive authority in the President together with Cabinet; and section 165 vests judicial authority in the courts. Constitutional Principle VI of the Interim Constitution also required separation of powers with appropriate checks and balances to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. The Constitutional Court treats the doctrine as a fundamental principle of the 1996 constitutional order.

The legislature is governed by Chapter 4. Parliament consists of the National Assembly and NCOP and makes law, holds the executive accountable and maintains oversight. Section 55(2) requires the National Assembly to maintain oversight over executive authority. The executive is governed mainly by ss 84 and 85, with executive authority vested in the President together with Cabinet. South Africa has overlap between the legislature and executive because Cabinet is generally drawn from Parliament — an inheritance from the Westminster system.

The judiciary is governed by Chapter 8. Section 165 protects judicial authority and independence. Courts must decide disputes independently and enforce the Constitution. Judicial review under s 172 allows courts to declare unconstitutional law or conduct invalid, while s 167(5) gives the Constitutional Court final authority on the constitutionality of Acts of Parliament, provincial Acts and presidential conduct.

The flexibility of the model is shown by Executive Council of the Western Cape Legislature v President of the Republic of South Africa 1995 (4) SA 877 (CC), which concerned delegation of law-making authority. The case shows that some overlap is practical and necessary, but excessive delegation may threaten democracy and concentrate legislative power in the executive.

In De Lange v Smuts NO 1998 (3) SA 785 (CC), the Court confirmed that there is no single universal model of separation of powers and that the South African model must be understood contextually. The case demonstrates that separation of powers is not a rigid formula; its content depends on each constitutional system's history, text and institutional design.

Judicial independence is a particularly important feature. In South African Association of Personal Injury Lawyers v Heath 2001 (1) SA 883 (CC), the Court confirmed that appointing a judge to perform executive-type investigative functions may threaten judicial independence and separation of powers. This case shows that judicial independence must be protected not only in judicial decision-making, but also in the institutional roles assigned to judges.

Chapter 9 institutions create another important feature of South Africa's model. They do not fit neatly into the classical three-branch model. Section 181 protects their independence and requires organs of state to assist and protect them. In Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Public Protector's remedial action was held to be binding unless set aside by a court, and the President and National Assembly were found to have breached constitutional obligations. This shows that Chapter 9 institutions are real accountability mechanisms, not symbolic bodies.

A major criticism of separation of powers in a constitutional democracy is the counter-majoritarian dilemma. This is the tension created when unelected judges invalidate laws or conduct by elected representatives. Theme 4 explains that judicial review under s 172 can require declarations of invalidity and remedies, meaning judges may affect law supported by a democratic majority even though judges are not elected representatives. However, South African constitutional law generally treats judicial review as a necessary and legitimising feature of constitutional democracy. Safeguards such as stare decisis, s 39, appeal and review reduce the risk of purely personal judicial decision-making.

The answer to the counter-majoritarian dilemma is partly judicial deference. Deference means courts must respect the institutional competence and democratic role of the legislature and executive while still enforcing the Constitution. Theme 4 warns that courts must not abdicate their role, but must also avoid prescribing policy details where the Constitution leaves discretion to another branch. EFF v Speaker illustrates this balance: the Court enforced constitutional obligations by declaring the President's and National Assembly's conduct invalid, but it also recognised that it is not ordinarily for courts to prescribe exactly how Parliament must design its oversight mechanisms.

In conclusion, separation of powers in South Africa is best understood as a flexible constitutional design, not rigid institutional isolation. It divides power, separates functions, protects judicial independence and creates checks and balances. At the same time, it accepts necessary overlap between the legislature and executive and recognises Chapter 9 institutions as an extra accountability layer. The key is balance: courts must enforce constitutional supremacy without becoming the legislature or executive, while political branches must exercise their powers within constitutional limits.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

The 1996 Constitution limits public power through constitutionalism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, rights, judicial review and separation of powers. Constitutionalism means power is structured and constrained by the Constitution. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect; section 2 of the 1996 Constitution confirms that the Constitution is supreme and that inconsistent law or conduct is invalid. Section 1(c) protects constitutional supremacy and the rule of law, meaning public power must be lawful, rational and non-arbitrary.

The Constitution is also transformative and value-based. The Preamble recognises past injustice and commits South Africa to healing divisions, while section 1 protects values such as dignity, equality, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness and openness. The Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy under s 7(1). Therefore, the Constitution does not merely organise government; it limits power and directs society towards dignity, equality, freedom and substantive justice.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

The 1996 Constitution is a supreme, value-based, democratic and transformative constitutional order. It limits public power while also directing South Africa away from colonial and apartheid injustice towards dignity, equality, freedom and social justice.

First, constitutionalism means the Constitution must both structure and constrain state power. It creates institutions such as Parliament, the executive, courts and Chapter 9 institutions, but also limits them through rights, legality, accountability and judicial review. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect. Section 2 gives effect to constitutional supremacy by providing that the Constitution is supreme and inconsistent law or conduct is invalid.

Secondly, the Constitution is value-based and transformative. The Preamble recognises the injustices of the past and commits South Africa to healing divisions and building a society based on democratic values, social justice and human rights. Section 1 entrenches dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, rule of law, democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) confirms that the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy. Karl Klare defines transformative constitutionalism as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

4. Full Integrated Exam-Ready Study Answer

A. Introduction: the Constitution as a complete constitutional order

The 1996 Constitution is not merely a technical legal document. It is a supreme, value-based, democratic and transformative constitutional order. It was adopted after a history in which colonialism and apartheid used law to entrench exclusion, racial hierarchy, unequal citizenship and concentrated public power. The Constitution therefore performs two connected functions: it limits public power and it promotes transformation.

The central point is that South Africa moved from a system where law could be used as an instrument of oppression to a system where all law and public power must be justified under a supreme Constitution. Theme 4 describes the Constitution as a framework that structures power, limits power, transforms society, protects rights, embeds democracy and makes public power accountable.

B. Constitutionalism: power must be structured and constrained

The first concept is constitutionalism. Constitutionalism means that government authority is derived from and limited by a constitution. In Theme 4, the key phrase is that a constitution must both structure and constrain state power. It structures power by creating institutions such as Parliament, the executive, the courts, provincial and local government, and Chapter 9 institutions. It constrains power by limiting what those institutions may do.

This is important because a state can have a constitution in a descriptive sense while still being oppressive. The stronger South African idea is prescriptive constitutionalism: the Constitution does not merely describe institutions; it judges public power according to constitutional values, rights, legality, accountability and transformation.

Link to exam: If the question asks how public power is limited, constitutionalism is your starting point: power is not free-floating; it is allocated, structured and limited by the Constitution.

C. Constitutional supremacy: the Constitution is the highest law

The second concept is constitutional supremacy. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect; the 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened this. Section 1(c) lists supremacy of the Constitution and the rule of law as founding values, while section 2 provides that the Constitution is supreme, that inconsistent law or conduct is invalid, and that constitutional obligations must be fulfilled.

Constitutional supremacy becomes practical through judicial review. Section 172 requires courts to declare law or conduct inconsistent with the Constitution invalid to the extent of its inconsistency.

Integrated point: Constitutional supremacy limits public power because no institution is above the Constitution. It also promotes transformation because constitutional values and rights can be enforced against old laws, new laws and unconstitutional conduct.

D. Transformative constitutionalism: the Constitution points from past to future

The Constitution does not only limit power; it aims to change society. This is transformative constitutionalism.

Karl Klare's definition describes transformative constitutionalism as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

Mureinik, writing in the Interim Constitution context, explained the shift from a culture of authority to a culture of justification: every exercise of public power must be justified through reasons, rights and values. Langa CJ developed this further in the transformative constitutionalism context: under a transformative constitution, judges must justify decisions not only by reference to authority, but also by reference to constitutional values and ideas. He describes the Constitution as a bridge from a deeply divided past to a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and opportunities for all.

Integrated point: Transformative constitutionalism explains why the Constitution is not satisfied with formal legality. Law must help dismantle the continuing effects of apartheid and build substantive equality, participation and belonging.

E. Foundational values: the Constitution is value-based

The Constitution is also value-based. Section 1 entrenches values such as dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal adult suffrage, regular elections, multi-party democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) confirms that the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy and affirms the democratic values of human dignity, equality and freedom. These values are repeated in s 36, which governs limitation of rights, and s 39, which governs interpretation.

S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) — decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution, but central to the 1996 constitutional order because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) substantially repeats the Makwanyane proportionality approach — is the strongest case example. The Court held the death penalty unconstitutional after weighing values such as life, dignity, equality, deterrence, retribution and proportionality, and held that public opinion cannot replace the Court's duty to uphold the Constitution.

Integrated point: Foundational values connect transformation, rights, democracy and limitation. They ensure that the Constitution is not morally neutral.

F. Application of the Bill of Rights: rights bind both public and sometimes private power

Section 7(2) requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights. Section 8(1) binds the legislature, executive, judiciary and all organs of state. This is vertical application.

Section 8(2) allows rights to bind natural and juristic persons where applicable, considering the nature of the right and duty. This is horizontal application. Section 8(3) allows courts to apply or develop the common law when giving horizontal effect to rights. Section 39(2) supports indirect application by requiring courts to interpret legislation and develop common law or customary law in a way that promotes the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. Carmichele v Minister of Safety and Security 2001 (4) SA 938 (CC) confirms that courts must develop common law under s 39(2) to align it with constitutional values.

Integrated point: The Bill of Rights does not only restrain the state. It can also reshape ordinary law and private relationships where dignity, equality, freedom or power imbalance require it.

G. Limitation of rights: rights are strong, but not absolute

Section 36 provides that rights may be limited only by law of general application and only to the extent that the limitation is reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on human dignity, equality and freedom. The limitation analysis has two stages: first, has a right been limited; and second, is the limitation justified under s 36, applying proportionality through the five listed factors.

Integrated point: Section 36 shows that the Constitution does not create absolute individualism. It creates a structured culture of justification: if rights are limited, the limitation must be constitutionally justified.

H. Rule of law and legality: public power must be lawful and rational

The rule of law is a founding value in s 1(c). It means public power must be exercised according to law, not arbitrary will. The principle of legality is the minimum enforceable requirement of the rule of law: public power must have legal authority, remain within that authority, and be rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose. Fedsure 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC) confirms lawful authority; Pharmaceutical Manufacturers 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC) confirms rationality.

But rule of law must not be treated as merely formal. Apartheid had law, but not justice. South Africa's rule of law must be understood together with dignity, equality, constitutional supremacy, rights and transformation.

Integrated point: Rule of law limits public power by preventing arbitrariness. Transformative constitutionalism deepens rule of law by requiring justification, not mere formal legality.

I. Democracy: people govern, but under the Constitution

South Africa is a representative constitutional democracy, strengthened by participatory democracy and limited direct democratic elements. Section 1(d) founds South Africa on universal adult suffrage, a national common voters roll, regular elections and a multi-party system of democratic government to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 19 protects political rights. Section 42(3) provides that the National Assembly represents the people and ensures government by the people under the Constitution.

South Africa is also participatory. Sections 59, 72 and 118 require public involvement in legislative processes. In Doctors for Life International v Speaker of the National Assembly 2006 (6) SA 416 (CC), the Court held that representative and participatory democracy are mutually supportive and that public participation gives vitality to representative democracy. In New Nation Movement NPC v President of the Republic of South Africa 2020 (6) SA 325 (CC), the Court held that excluding independent candidates from national and provincial elections violated political rights under s 19.

Integrated point: Democracy under the Constitution is not unlimited majority rule. It is government by the people under a supreme Constitution, with rights, participation, accountability and judicial review.

J. Separation of powers: public power is divided and checked

Separation of powers is a procedural limit on public power. It divides authority between the legislature, executive and judiciary. The doctrine rests on four principles: division of government power, separation of functions, separation of personnel and checks and balances. South Africa follows a flexible model, not a rigid classical model. Sections 43, 85 and 165 separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions, giving the doctrine a clear textual foundation. Judicial independence is strongly protected by s 165. Courts enforce constitutional supremacy through judicial review under s 172 and the Constitutional Court's final authority under s 167(5).

Constitutional Principle VI required separation of powers with checks and balances to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness.

Integrated point: Separation of powers prevents concentration of power, but checks and balances ensure that branches remain accountable to the Constitution and to each other.

K. Chapter 9 institutions and accountability: EFF v Speaker

Chapter 9 institutions add an extra accountability layer beyond the classical three branches. Section 181 provides that these institutions are independent and subject only to the Constitution and the law, and that organs of state must assist and protect them. The Public Protector's power to take appropriate remedial action comes from s 182(1)(c).

Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC) is the key integrated case. The Public Protector ordered remedial action against President Zuma regarding Nkandla. The President failed to comply, and the National Assembly adopted a resolution absolving him. The Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court, that the President's failure was inconsistent with s 83(b) read with ss 181(3) and 182(1)(c) and invalid, and that the National Assembly's resolution was inconsistent with ss 42(3), 55(2)(a) and (b) and 181(3), invalid and set aside.

The case connects constitutional supremacy, rule of law, accountability, presidential obligations, Parliament's oversight duties, Chapter 9 institutions, judicial review and separation of powers in a single judgment.

Integrated point: EFF shows that constitutional accountability is enforceable. Even the President and National Assembly must comply with constitutional obligations.

L. Critical tension: courts, democracy and deference

The Constitution gives courts strong review powers, but courts are unelected. This creates the counter-majoritarian dilemma: judges may invalidate laws or conduct supported by elected representatives. This tension is real, but South African constitutional law generally treats judicial review as a necessary part of constitutional democracy. Courts protect rights, enforce accountability, ensure participation and prevent abuse of power.

However, courts must also show deference by respecting the institutional competence of Parliament and the executive. Deference does not mean abdication: courts should not prescribe policy details where discretion belongs to another branch, but they must enforce constitutional limits where the Constitution is violated. EFF illustrates this balance precisely.

Integrated point: The Constitution requires balance: courts must not govern, but political branches must not violate the Constitution.

M. Conclusion: the integrated answer

The 1996 Constitution limits public power and promotes transformation at the same time. Constitutionalism structures and constrains power. Constitutional supremacy makes the Constitution the highest law. The Bill of Rights protects dignity, equality and freedom. Section 36 permits only justified limitations. Section 39 ensures value-based interpretation. The rule of law and legality prevent arbitrary public power. Democracy gives people political voice but subjects majority power to constitutional limits. Separation of powers divides authority and creates checks and balances. Chapter 9 institutions support accountability. Together, these doctrines create a constitutional order that moves South Africa from apartheid's culture of authority to a culture of justification, where all public power must be lawful, rational, accountable, democratic, rights-respecting and transformative.

6. 5-Mark Version

Answer:

The 1996 Constitution limits public power through constitutionalism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, rights, judicial review and separation of powers. Constitutionalism means power is structured and constrained by the Constitution. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect; section 2 of the 1996 Constitution confirms that the Constitution is supreme and that inconsistent law or conduct is invalid. Section 1(c) protects constitutional supremacy and the rule of law, meaning public power must be lawful, rational and non-arbitrary.

The Constitution is also transformative and value-based. The Preamble recognises past injustice and commits South Africa to healing divisions, while section 1 protects values such as dignity, equality, non-racialism, non-sexism, accountability, responsiveness and openness. The Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy under s 7(1). Therefore, the Constitution does not merely organise government; it limits power and directs society towards dignity, equality, freedom and substantive justice.

7. 10-Mark Version

Answer:

The 1996 Constitution is a supreme, value-based, democratic and transformative constitutional order. It limits public power while also directing South Africa away from colonial and apartheid injustice towards dignity, equality, freedom and social justice.

First, constitutionalism means the Constitution must both structure and constrain state power. It creates institutions such as Parliament, the executive, courts and Chapter 9 institutions, but also limits them through rights, legality, accountability and judicial review. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect. Section 2 gives effect to constitutional supremacy by providing that the Constitution is supreme and inconsistent law or conduct is invalid.

Secondly, the Constitution is value-based and transformative. The Preamble recognises the injustices of the past and commits South Africa to healing divisions and building a society based on democratic values, social justice and human rights. Section 1 entrenches dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, rule of law, democracy, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) confirms that the Bill of Rights is the cornerstone of democracy. Karl Klare defines transformative constitutionalism as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction.

Thirdly, the Bill of Rights limits power directly and indirectly. Section 8(1) binds the state, while s 8(2) can bind private persons where applicable. Section 39(2) requires ordinary law to be interpreted and developed in line with the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. Rights are not absolute, but s 36 permits limitations only where reasonable and justifiable in an open and democratic society based on dignity, equality and freedom.

Finally, the Constitution limits public power through rule of law, democracy and separation of powers. The principle of legality requires public power to be authorised by law and rational. Democracy under s 1(d) includes elections, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Sections 43, 85 and 165 separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions, while courts enforce constitutional supremacy through s 172. In Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Constitutional Court showed that even the President and National Assembly must comply with constitutional obligations.

Therefore, the Constitution limits public power not to weaken government, but to ensure that all power is lawful, accountable, rights-respecting and transformative.

8. 15-Mark Version

Answer:

The 1996 Constitution is a supreme, value-based, democratic and transformative constitutional order. It must be understood against South Africa's history of colonialism, apartheid and parliamentary sovereignty, where law was often used to entrench racial hierarchy and concentrated public power. The Constitution now performs two connected functions: it limits public power and promotes transformation.

The first principle is constitutionalism. Constitutionalism means government authority is derived from and limited by a constitution. The Constitution must both structure and constrain state power. It structures power by creating institutions such as Parliament, the executive, the judiciary, provincial and local government and Chapter 9 institutions. It constrains power by subjecting those institutions to rights, legality, accountability and constitutional review.

The second principle is constitutional supremacy. Parliamentary sovereignty was abolished when the Interim Constitution of 1993 took effect. The 1996 Final Constitution confirmed and deepened this. Section 1(c) lists constitutional supremacy and the rule of law as founding values, while section 2 provides that the Constitution is supreme and that inconsistent law or conduct is invalid. Judicial review gives this practical force because section 172 requires courts to declare unconstitutional law or conduct invalid.

Thirdly, the Constitution is transformative. Karl Klare defines transformative constitutionalism as a long-term project of constitutional enactment, interpretation and enforcement aimed at transforming South Africa's political and social institutions and power relationships in a democratic, participatory and egalitarian direction. Mureinik, writing in the Interim Constitution context, explained this as a move from a culture of authority to a culture of justification, where every exercise of public power must be justified through reasons, rights and values. Langa CJ later developed this in the transformative constitutionalism context: under a transformative constitution, judges must justify decisions not only by reference to authority, but also by reference to constitutional ideas and values. He describes the Constitution as a bridge from a deeply divided past to a future based on human rights, democracy, peaceful co-existence and opportunities for all.

Fourthly, the Constitution is value-based. Section 1 entrenches values such as dignity, equality, human rights, non-racialism, non-sexism, constitutional supremacy, rule of law, universal suffrage, accountability, responsiveness and openness. Section 7(1) describes the Bill of Rights as the cornerstone of democracy and affirms dignity, equality and freedom. Section 39 requires courts to interpret rights, legislation, common law and customary law consistently with constitutional values. In S v Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) — decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution but central to the 1996 Constitution because the First Certification Judgment (para 90) confirmed that section 36(1) substantially repeats the Makwanyane proportionality approach — the Court used dignity, life, equality and proportionality to hold the death penalty unconstitutional.

The Bill of Rights is central to limiting power and promoting transformation. Section 7(2) requires the state to respect, protect, promote and fulfil rights. Section 8(1) binds the state, while s 8(2) may bind private persons where applicable. Section 39(2) requires ordinary law to be interpreted and developed in light of the spirit, purport and objects of the Bill of Rights. In Carmichele v Minister of Safety and Security 2001 (4) SA 938 (CC), the Court confirmed courts must develop the common law to align it with constitutional values. Rights are not absolute: section 36 allows limitation only where the state can justify it through proportionality.

The rule of law also limits public power. Section 1(c) protects the rule of law, and the principle of legality requires all public power to be legally authorised and rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose, as confirmed by Fedsure 1999 (1) SA 374 (CC) and Pharmaceutical Manufacturers 2000 (2) SA 674 (CC). Rule of law must not be treated as merely formal: apartheid had law without justice. South Africa's rule of law must be understood together with dignity, equality, constitutional supremacy, rights and transformation.

The Constitution is also democratic. Section 1(d) entrenches universal adult suffrage, regular elections and a multi-party democratic system to ensure accountability, responsiveness and openness. South Africa is primarily a representative constitutional democracy, but it also includes participatory democracy through ss 59, 72 and 118. In Doctors for Life International v Speaker of the National Assembly 2006 (6) SA 416 (CC), the Court held that representative and participatory democracy are mutually supportive. In New Nation Movement NPC v President of the Republic of South Africa 2020 (6) SA 325 (CC), the Court held that excluding independent candidates from elections violated political rights under s 19.

Separation of powers is another limit. It divides authority between the legislature, executive and judiciary and creates checks and balances. South Africa follows a flexible model: sections 43, 85 and 165 separately vest legislative, executive and judicial authority in distinct institutions. Chapter 9 institutions add a further accountability layer. In Economic Freedom Fighters v Speaker of the National Assembly 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC), the Constitutional Court held that the Public Protector's remedial action was binding unless set aside by a court, and that both the President and National Assembly failed to fulfil constitutional obligations. This case connects constitutional supremacy, rule of law, accountability, presidential obligations, Chapter 9 institutions, judicial review and separation of powers in a single judgment.

Critically, the Constitution creates a tension between democracy and judicial review. Courts may invalidate laws or conduct supported by elected branches, raising the counter-majoritarian dilemma. However, South Africa is a constitutional democracy, not unlimited majority rule. Courts must enforce constitutional supremacy, rights and accountability, but they must also show deference by respecting the institutional competence of Parliament and the executive. Deference cannot mean allowing constitutional violations to stand.

In conclusion, the 1996 Constitution limits public power through constitutionalism, supremacy, rule of law, rights, judicial review, democracy, separation of powers and Chapter 9 institutions. At the same time, it promotes transformation through dignity, equality, freedom, substantive justice, participation and a culture of justification. Its goal is not simply to prevent abuse, but to build a democratic society in which all public power is lawful, rational, accountable, rights-respecting and directed towards healing the injustices of the past.

10. What to Memorise

Mini skeleton:

1996 Constitution = supreme + value-based + democratic + transformative.

Constitutionalism = structure + constrain power.

Parliamentary sovereignty abolished by Interim Constitution 1993; confirmed by s 2 of 1996 Constitution.

Transformation = past injustice → culture of justification → substantive equality.

s 1 = founding values.

s 7 = Bill of Rights cornerstone.

s 8 = Bill of Rights application.

s 36 = justified limitation.

s 39 = value-based interpretation.

ss 43, 85, 165 = textual basis for separation of powers.

Rule of law = lawful, rational, accountable power.

Democracy = representative + participatory + constitutional.

Separation of powers = divided power + checks and balances.

Chapter 9 = extra accountability layer.

EFF 2016 (3) SA 580 (CC) = President and Parliament are not above the Constitution; ss 55(2)(a) and (b) specifically.

Makwanyane 1995 (3) SA 391 (CC) = decided under s 33 IC; central through First Certification Judgment para 90.

One-sentence memory trigger: Theme 4 is the story of how South Africa replaced apartheid's culture of authority with a supreme, rights-based, value-driven and democratic Constitution that demands justification for all public power.

11. 70 / 80 / 90+ Upgrade Guide

Cross-theme authority map

Harris I / Harris II / Collins

Old-order judicial review, manner-and-form limits, parliamentary sovereignty and constitutional crisis.

First and Second Certification

Legal legitimacy of the Final Constitution, Constitutional Principles and certification by the Constitutional Court.

Makwanyane

Value-based constitutionalism, dignity, limitation, proportionality and public opinion not deciding rights.

EFF v Speaker

Public Protector remedial action, accountability, rule of law, President and National Assembly obligations.

Case-law quick bank

City of Tshwane v Afriforum

Facts: AfriForum opposed Pretoria street-name changes linked to the colonial/apartheid past.

Issue: symbolic redress, heritage, public space and belonging.

Court reasoning: the Preamble guides inclusive belonging; public symbols are not neutral; transformation can require symbolic change; Ubuntu supports mutual recognition.

Judgment: symbolic redress may be constitutionally legitimate where done through lawful public processes.

Constitutional significance: Preamble, transformation, ubuntu and public space.

Exam use: Theme 1 Preamble, Theme 2 historical redress and Theme 4 values.

Harris I

Facts: Parliament tried to remove voters from the common roll without following entrenched procedure.

Issue: whether manner-and-form requirements were binding.

Court reasoning: entrenched procedural requirements had to be followed.

Judgment: Act invalid.

Constitutional significance: courts could enforce procedure under parliamentary sovereignty.

Exam use: limited judicial review.

Harris II

Facts: Parliament created a High Court of Parliament to overturn the Appellate Division.

Issue: whether Parliament could make itself a court of appeal.

Court reasoning: Parliament could not collapse judicial power into Parliament to undo judgments.

Judgment: High Court of Parliament invalid.

Constitutional significance: limited separation of powers and judicial independence under old order.

Exam use: do not confuse with Collins.

Collins

Facts: Parliament expanded the Senate and followed the formal procedure.

Issue: whether courts could review motive or substantive injustice once procedure was followed.

Court reasoning: formal compliance was enough under parliamentary sovereignty.

Judgment: legislation upheld.

Constitutional significance: procedural review could be defeated and could not deliver substantive justice.

Exam use: old-order crisis and need for supremacy.

Freedom Charter

What happened: adopted in 1955 as a liberation movement vision.

Constitutional issue: equal citizenship, democracy and socio-economic justice as political ideals.

Significance: normative precursor, not binding law.

Exam use: Theme 2 resistance and Theme 3 constitutional vision.

Sharpeville

What happened: 1960 state violence against anti-pass protest.

Constitutional issue: repression, rights denial and legitimacy crisis.

Significance: intensified resistance and international condemnation.

Exam use: why apartheid legality lacked legitimacy.

Rivonia

What happened: 1963-64 trial of liberation leaders under repressive security legislation.

Constitutional issue: legality versus legitimacy.

Significance: valid-looking law used to criminalise resistance.

Exam use: shows need for rights, democracy and constitutional supremacy.

First Certification Judgment

Facts: Constitutional Court assessed whether the New Text complied with the 34 Constitutional Principles.

Issue: certification of the Final Constitution.

Court reasoning: certification was a legal exercise; socio-economic rights and flexible separation of powers were acceptable.

Judgment: first text not certified.

Constitutional significance: legal legitimacy of constitution-making.

Exam use: Theme 3 certification and CPs.

Second Certification Judgment

Facts: Amended Text returned to the Constitutional Court.

Issue: whether defects had been cured.

Court reasoning: amended provisions complied with the CPs.

Judgment: certified.

Constitutional significance: legal completion of transition.

Exam use: final stage before 1996 Constitution.

S v Makwanyane

Facts: challenge to the death penalty.

Issue: compatibility with Interim Constitution rights.

Court reasoning: dignity, life, equality, proportionality and public opinion not decisive.

Judgment: death penalty unconstitutional.

Constitutional significance: value-based constitutionalism and limitation.

Exam use: section 33 IC / section 36 continuity.

EFF v Speaker

Facts: Nkandla remedial action dispute.

Issue: binding force of Public Protector remedial action and constitutional duties of President/NA.

Court reasoning: remedial action is binding unless set aside by a court; accountability and rule of law required compliance.

Judgment: President and National Assembly failed constitutional obligations.

Constitutional significance: Chapter 9, rule of law, accountability and separation of powers.

Exam use: Theme 4 accountability.

Carmichele

Facts: police/prosecutorial failures after a dangerous accused was released.

Issue: common-law development in light of constitutional duties.

Court reasoning: courts must develop common law under section 39(2).

Judgment: common law had to be reconsidered constitutionally.

Constitutional significance: indirect application and common-law development.

Exam use: horizontal/application questions.

Daniels

Facts: occupier sought protection and home-related rights in a private-law setting.

Issue: whether constitutional values and legislation protect vulnerable occupiers.

Court reasoning: property, dignity and statutory interpretation must be read through constitutional values.

Judgment: rights-protective interpretation accepted.

Constitutional significance: constitutional values in private-law/statutory contexts.

Exam use: application, interpretation and dignity.

S v Bhulwana

Facts: reverse onus/presumption issue in criminal law.

Issue: whether statutory burden violated presumption of innocence.

Court reasoning: limitation and proportionality had to be justified.

Judgment: reverse onus unconstitutional.

Constitutional significance: rights limitation and criminal procedure.

Exam use: section 36/limitation and proportionality.

Judicial authority and independence.

Declaration of invalidity for unconstitutional law or conduct.

Petty apartheid; segregated public amenities.

Attempted removal of Coloured voters from common roll; Harris crisis.

Repressive statute used against political opposition.

Citation of Constitutional Laws Act 5 of 2005

Citation/constitutional-law reference point where used in Theme 3/4 materials.

Constitutional subsidiarity warning

Where legislation gives effect to a right, litigants generally may not bypass it and rely directly on the constitutional right unless they challenge that legislation.

Exam technique and answer strategy guide

Use issue → principle/provision → authority/case → application → conclusion. Match the breadth of the essay bank to the question: narrow questions need narrow answers; broad synthesis questions can use synthesis banks.

For 5 marks, define and anchor authority. For 10 marks, add case/event reasoning. For 15 marks, add critical engagement, historical context and examiner-facing precision.

Common mistakes master list

  • Confusing Harris II with Collins.
  • Saying the Freedom Charter was binding law.
  • Forgetting that Makwanyane was decided under section 33 of the Interim Constitution.
  • Collapsing section 36 proportionality into legality rationality.
  • Using Essay Bank 9 as a spaghetti answer.
  • Saying horizontal application is automatic.
  • Forgetting exact EFF final-order sections.
  • Bypassing legislation and relying directly on the Bill of Rights without considering constitutional subsidiarity.

Final memorisation checklist

  • Harris I = procedure not followed.
  • Harris II = Parliament cannot become a court.
  • Collins = procedure followed, motive not reviewed.
  • CODESA → MPNF → Interim Constitution → elections → Constitutional Assembly → certification → Final Constitution.
  • Makwanyane = proportionality, dignity, public opinion not decisive.
  • EFF = binding remedial action unless set aside by court.

Optional MCQ / self-test

Use the buttons for quick scoring, then open the explanations. Answers are intentionally short so this works as a last-minute test bank.

20 Theme 1 MCQs

1. Which statement best captures law in Theme 1?

Reveal explanation

Theme 1 treats law as a human, social and unfinished project.

2. Why does law presuppose society?

Reveal explanation

Law manages social interaction and competing interests.

3. Constitutional law is mainly part of:

Reveal explanation

It regulates public power, state institutions and state-individual relationships.

4. The legislature primarily:

Reveal explanation

Legislative authority is law-making authority.

5. The executive primarily:

Reveal explanation

Executive power gives practical effect to law and policy.

6. The judiciary primarily:

Reveal explanation

Courts resolve disputes and interpret law.

7. Ubuntu is best captured by:

Reveal explanation

Ubuntu links personhood to relationship and community.

8. A codified constitution is:

Reveal explanation

South Africa has a written/codified Constitution.

9. A rigid constitution is one that:

Reveal explanation

Rigidity means amendment is deliberately harder than ordinary law-making.

10. South Africa is best described as:

Reveal explanation

South Africa has three spheres with cooperative governance and strong national features.

11. The Preamble is:

Reveal explanation

It guides constitutional purpose, interpretation and transformation.

12. City of Tshwane is useful for:

Reveal explanation

The case links public space, symbolic transformation and inclusive belonging.

13. Constitutionalism requires:

Reveal explanation

Constitutionalism constrains government through fundamental law.

14. Rule of law in constitutional democracy includes:

Reveal explanation

Modern South African rule of law is tied to constitutional supremacy and accountable power.

15. Private law mainly regulates:

Reveal explanation

Public law concerns state power; private law concerns private parties.

16. What does constitutionalism prevent?

Reveal explanation

Constitutionalism limits government through fundamental law.

17. What is social contract theory useful for?

Reveal explanation

It frames why people accept legal authority for peace and order.

18. What is the legal definition of a constitution?

Reveal explanation

A constitution is the fundamental law according to which the country is governed.

19. What does public law regulate?

Reveal explanation

Constitutional law is public law.

20. Which phrase links Ubuntu to personhood?

Reveal explanation

Ubuntu grounds law in relationship and shared humanity.

Reveal explanation

Tricameral Parliament excluded the African majority.

20 mixed Theme 3/4 MCQs

1. What came after CODESA in the negotiation process?

Reveal explanation

MPNF followed CODESA and helped produce the Interim Constitution.

2. What did the 34 Constitutional Principles do?

Reveal explanation

They were certification standards.

3. Who drafted the Final Constitution?

Reveal explanation

The National Assembly and Senate sat jointly as the Constitutional Assembly.

4. What was the First Certification result?

Reveal explanation

The Constitutional Court refused to certify the first text.

5. What was accepted in First Certification?

Reveal explanation

The Court accepted these as compatible with the CPs.

6. When did the Final Constitution commence?

Reveal explanation

Signed 10 December 1996; commenced 4 February 1997.

7. What does Mureinik's bridge describe?

Reveal explanation

It explains the transformative move in constitutionalism.

8. What does Langa add?

Reveal explanation

Langa stresses constitutional values and transformation.

9. What first abolished parliamentary sovereignty?

Reveal explanation

The 1996 Constitution confirmed and deepened it.

10. What does section 2 do?

Reveal explanation

It makes inconsistent law or conduct invalid.

11. Makwanyane was decided under:

Reveal explanation

Section 36 later substantially repeats the limitation approach.

12. EFF remedial action is:

Reveal explanation

EFF is central for Public Protector remedial action.

13. EFF connects to:

Reveal explanation

It covers President, NA, Chapter 9 and judicial review.

14. Carmichele is useful for:

Reveal explanation

It is a common-law development case.

15. Horizontal application is:

Reveal explanation

Section 8(2) requires nature of right/duty analysis.

16. Section 36 proportionality is:

Reveal explanation

Do not collapse these standards.

17. Constitutional values:

Reveal explanation

Values guide interpretation within constitutional structure.

18. Essay Bank 9 should be used as:

Reveal explanation

Avoid spaghetti answers.

19. Constitutional subsidiarity means:

Reveal explanation

Use PEPUDA/LRA where they give effect to rights unless challenging them.

20. Section 74 is about:

Reveal explanation

It governs constitutional amendments.

15 short-answer prompts

1. Define constitutionalism in one paragraph.

Model answer: Constitutionalism means government authority is derived from and limited by fundamental law. It requires limited government, rule of law, separation of powers, checks and balances, rights protection and, in South Africa, transformation.

2. Explain why law presupposes society.

Model answer: One person alone needs no legal rules. Law becomes necessary when people interact because it manages competing interests, resources, authority and conflict.

3. Distinguish public and private law.

Model answer: Public law regulates state power, state-individual relationships and relationships among organs of state. Private law regulates disputes and relationships between private parties.

4. Explain the Preamble's constitutional purpose.

Model answer: The Preamble gives the Constitution historical context, values and direction. It recognises injustices of the past, aims to heal divisions, establishes democratic values, social justice and human rights, and guides interpretation.

5. Use City of Tshwane to explain belonging and redress.

Model answer: City of Tshwane concerned street-name changes. It shows public symbols are not neutral, the Preamble guides inclusive belonging, symbolic redress can be constitutionally significant, and Ubuntu supports mutual recognition.

6. List and explain the branches of government.

Model answer: The legislature makes law, the executive implements or executes law, and the judiciary interprets and adjudicates law. Separation of powers prevents concentration of power.

7. Explain why constitutional history matters.

Model answer: The Constitution responds to historical abuse. Apartheid legality shows the danger of parliamentary supremacy, and history explains why constitutional supremacy, rights, equality and transformation matter.

8. List the five official constitutions.

Model answer: The five official constitutions are the 1910 Union Constitution/South Africa Act 1909, the 1961 Republic Constitution, the 1983 Tricameral Constitution, the 1993 Interim Constitution and the 1996 Final Constitution.

9. Explain parliamentary supremacy with the South African nuance.

Model answer: Parliament was substantively supreme, but South African parliamentary sovereignty was not fully Diceyan because limited manner-and-form requirements existed under the South Africa Act 1909.

10. Distinguish Harris I, Harris II and Collins.

Model answer: Harris I concerned failure to follow entrenched procedure. Harris II invalidated the High Court of Parliament. Collins upheld Senate expansion and formal compliance, with no review of motive or substance.

11. Distinguish petty and grand apartheid.

Model answer: Petty apartheid refers to everyday segregation of facilities and social life. Grand apartheid refers to structural and territorial separation, homelands, forced removals and political exclusion.

12. Explain why the Freedom Charter matters but is not binding law.

Model answer: The Freedom Charter was not legally binding, but it was a political and normative precursor to constitutional democracy. It articulated equal citizenship, universal suffrage, rights and socio-economic justice.

13. Explain Rivonia as legality versus legitimacy.

Model answer: Rivonia shows that apartheid used formally valid security laws to criminalise resistance. Mandela and others used the trial to challenge legitimacy, showing law can be valid in form but unjust in substance.

14. Explain why the Interim Constitution was revolutionary.

Model answer: The Interim Constitution, Act 200 of 1993, used legal continuity but created constitutional rupture through supremacy, a Bill of Rights, the Constitutional Court, GNU, Constitutional Principles and a path to an elected Constitutional Assembly.

15. Explain constitutional subsidiarity in application questions.

Model answer: Where legislation gives effect to a right, litigants generally must rely on that legislation first rather than bypassing it and relying directly on the Constitution, unless challenging the legislation. Examples include PEPUDA and the LRA.
